Almost global large deviations principle for the KdV equation
Riccardo Berforini D’Aquino, Ricardo Grande

Abstract
We study extreme wave formation for the Korteweg–de Vries equation on the torus with random initial data of average size ε \varepsilon . We establish a large deviations principle for the supremum of the solution over arbitrarily long polynomial timescales t ≤ ε − n t\leq\varepsilon^{-n} for any fixed n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} . This identifies the leading-order asymptotics of the probability of observing unusually large amplitudes. In this integrable setting, the dynamics evolves on invariant tori where Fourier moduli are almost conserved, ruling out mechanisms for extreme wave formation based on resonant energy exchange. As a result, large amplitudes can only arise through coherent structures or dispersive focusing, which corresponds to the quasi-synchronization of many phases. We show that the latter is dominant in the weakly nonlinear regime. Our approach combines a Birkhoff normal form analysis with probabilistic arguments, exploiting the stability of the integrable dynamics to control the probability of phase quasi-synchronization over long timescales.



1
Introduction

In dispersive wave systems, different modes propagate at different speeds, yet spatially localized structures with large peaks can still form. Such events, often referred to as extreme or rogue waves, correspond to rare deviations from typical amplitudes and are attributed to a combination of linear and nonlinear effects, whose precise role remains unclear
[
36
]
.

In this work, we adopt a probabilistic viewpoint to study extreme wave formation under random initial data. Following the framework of Dematteis, Grafke, Onorato and Vanden-Eijnden
[
11
,
12
]
1
1

1

Their work was based on the cubic NLS equation and the Dysthe equation on
\mathbb{T}
, but the general framework applies to more complex models of water wave dynamics.
In particular, the one-dimensional Dysthe equation can be transformed into a KdV-type equation
[
24
,
34
]
.
, we characterize the most likely mechanisms leading to such events by establishing a large deviations principle for the supremum of the solution.

One mechanism, known as
nonlinear focusing
, attributes the formation of extreme waves to nonlinear energy transfer across modes. In this scenario, large amplitudes arise through interactions between waves of different wavenumbers. This mechanism was recently shown to be dominant for the beating NLS equation on
\mathbb{T}

[
22
]
.
A second mechanism, known as
dispersive focusing
, attributes extreme wave formation to constructive interference between modes. In this case, large amplitudes arise when phases simultaneously synchronize at a given point. This mechanism was shown to drive extreme wave formation for the cubic NLS equation and the gravity water waves system on
\mathbb{T}
over nonlinear timescales
[
5
,
21
]
.

These works study rogue wave formation for Gaussian initial data of typical size
\varepsilon
, and are limited to timescales
t\ll\varepsilon^{-3}
. In this paper, we characterize extreme wave formation for the KdV equation on
\mathbb{T}
over arbitrarily long polynomial timescales
t\leq\varepsilon^{-n}
for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
. While we focus on the KdV equation, the same approach applies to the cubic NLS equation.

In this integrable setting, mechanisms based on resonant energy exchanges are incompatible with the dynamics, since solutions evolve on invariant tori determined by the initial Fourier moduli, which are (to leading order) conserved. As a result, large amplitudes can only arise through coherent structures associated with the integrable flow or through constructive interference between modes.

Identifying which of these mechanisms is most likely remains challenging, as it requires propagating precise statistical information for solutions of nonlinear PDEs over long timescales. In particular, the initial data are typically not distributed according to an invariant Gibbs measure of the underlying equation; instead, the probability measure is often supported on high-regularity data obtained empirically from direct measurements in the sea
[
1
,
9
]
or constructed to match standard wave spectra
[
25
]
.

This leads to the problem of understanding the out-of-equilibrium evolution of the tails of such measures under the flow of nonlinear PDEs. This question has been the subject of significant recent work, including developments in wave turbulence theory
[
13
,
14
,
23
]
and quasi-invariant measures
[
35
,
40
]
. While the latter approach can yield upper and lower bounds for the tails of the evolved measure, they are not yet sharp enough to establish a large deviations principle, which is needed to fully characterize extreme wave formation.

To establish a sharp large deviations principle over such long timescales, we study the probability that the phases of a large number of modes quasi-synchronize. This event lies at the heart of dispersive focusing, which we show to be the dominant mechanism of extreme wave formation for this integrable dynamics. A key difficulty is to control the time evolution of the probability of this quasi-synchronization event. Despite the complete integrability of the system, the Fourier phases are highly nonlinear functions of the initial Fourier moduli and phases, and tracking their exact distribution seems out of reach. Our approach bypasses this obstacle by constructing an explicitly measurable subset of this event, combining random fixed point arguments, stability theory for integrable Hamiltonian systems, and a normal form construction which may be of independent interest.

1.1
Main results

Consider the Korteweg–De Vries
[
27
]
equation (KdV):

(1.1)

u_{t}+u_{xxx}+uu_{x}=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{T}.

The KdV equation (
1.1
) is a Hamiltonian system,

(1.2)

\partial_{t}u=\partial_{x}\nabla H(u),\qquad H(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(u_{x}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}u^{3})\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
\nabla
denotes the
L^{2}
-gradient. Beyond the Hamiltonian, this equation enjoys infinitely many conservation laws, including the average
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
and the mass
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
. The conserved average allow us to consider solutions to (
1.1
) evolving in the Sobolev space of real-valued functions with zero average
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
, cf. (
1.48
).

The KdV equation is globally well-posed in

L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, which follows from a local well-posedness result and the conserved mass
[
3
,
15
]
. See also
[
10
]
for the sharp global well-posedness result in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s\geq-\frac{1}{2}
.

We consider (
1.1
) with a zero-mean, random initial datum

(1.3)

u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0,x)=\varepsilon\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}}c_{k}\eta_{k}^{\omega}e^{ikx},

where
(\eta_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d standard, complex, Gaussian r.v. (
1.52
),
\overline{\eta_{k}^{\omega}}=\eta_{-k}^{\omega}
and
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of real coefficients, with

(1.4)

c_{k}=c_{-k}\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}|k|^{2s}c_{k}^{2}<+\infty,\qquad\forall s\geq 0.

In particular,
u_{0}^{\omega}(x)
is itself a centered, real Gaussian r.v. with variance
2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}
.

The condition (
1.4
) guarantees that
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\in\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
a.s., which implies the a.s. existence of a global solution
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
to (
1.1
). Indeed, note that

(1.5)

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0,x)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}=2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}|k|^{2s}c_{k}^{2},

which is finite by (
1.4
).

The main result of this manuscript is a large deviations principle for the sup-norm of the solution to the KdV equation over arbitrarily long polynomial timescales:

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider the KdV equation (
1.1
) with random initial datum (
1.3
). Let
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
be the corresponding (a.s. global) unique solution. Fix
\mathtt{n}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda>0
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then we have

(1.6)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}}\left\lvert\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}\right\rvert=0.

In particular, for any
t=t(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}
we have the following large deviations principle:

(1.7)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}.

Let us make some comments.

1.

Timescales
: Previous works on large deviations principles for dispersive equations have reached timescales
t\ll\varepsilon^{-3}

[
21
,
22
,
5
]
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result to achieve arbitrarily long polynomial timescales. To reach these times, we exploit the integrability of the KdV equation in order to track the probability that a large collection of nonlinear phases of
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t)
quasi-synchronize, building on the novel approach introduced in
[
5
]
.

2.

Dispersive focusing
: As a byproduct of our proof, we show in
Theorem

1.2
below that the dominant mechanism of formation of extreme waves in the KdV equation in the weakly nonlinear regime is
dispersive focusing
, i.e. the quasi-synchronization of a large number of phases of the Fourier modes giving rise to constructive interference. This is in contrast to
[
22
]
, where energy exchange between Fourier modes is the main mechanism.

3.

Regularity
:
Our result only requires a finite amount of regularity. In particular, hypothesis (
1.4
) can be weakened, as we do in
Theorem

4.1
. However, we didn’t try to optimize the minimal regularity
s
, which depends on the normal form procedure (see
Section

1.2.3
below), and we leave this question for future work.

4.

Growth of
L^{\infty}
-norm
: Let us highlight that we do not study the growth of the
L^{\infty}
-norm of individual trajectories. Instead,
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
characterize the most likely initial data which give rise to large solutions to (
1.1
) at time
t
. Large solutions are, in turn, defined by comparison with the average initial size which is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
, cf. (
1.5
).

5.

Comparison with
[
21
]
: In
[
21
]
, the authors establish a large deviations principle analogous to (
1.7
) for the cubic NLS equation on
\mathbb{T}
, valid over timescales
t=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-2(1-\delta)})
for a.s. smooth initial data. We expect that the methods developed in this manuscript can be used to extend the results of
[
21
]
to arbitrary polynomial timescales
t\sim\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}
for any
\mathtt{n}\in\mathbb{N}
. Notably, the KdV equation presents additional challenges due to the presence of derivatives in the nonlinear term, which requires working in high-regularity spaces to avoid loss of derivatives. By contrast, our approach may require significantly weaker regularity assumptions in the NLS setting.

As previously announced, the second main result of this manuscript is the identification of the main mechanism behind the formation of an extreme wave.
Consider the a.s. global solution to (
1.1
):

(1.8)

u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)=2\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}|u_{k}^{\omega}(t)|\cos\left(\psi_{k}^{\omega}(t)+kx\right),

where
\psi_{k}^{\omega}(t)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, are the phases of the Fourier coefficients of the solution at time
t
. Let
\mathtt{N}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and define the set

(1.9)

\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)=\left\{\omega\in\Omega\,|\,\left\lvert\psi_{k}^{\omega}(t)\ \mbox{mod}\ 2\pi\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1-\delta}{15}},\,\,\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,\mathtt{N}\}\ \mbox{such that}\ c_{k}>0\right\},

i.e. the event that the first
N
phases are very close to zero. We note that the contributions towards the
L^{\infty}
-norm from Fourier coefficients
u_{k}^{\omega}(t)
such that
u_{k}^{\omega}(0)=0
, cf. (
1.3
), are negligible on account of the quasi-conservation of the Fourier moduli by the KdV dynamics (cf. (
3.2
)). As a result, phases associated to such Fourier coefficients are “free”.

The following result shows that the main mechanism for the formation of extreme waves is the quasi-synchronization of the phases.

Theorem 1.2

(Dispersive Focusing)
.

Under the same assumptions of
Theorem

1.1
we have

(1.10)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}}\left\lvert\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\cap\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)\right)+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}\right\rvert=0.

Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
are proved in
Section

4
, see
Theorem

4.1
and
Theorem

4.2
, respectively. We outline the main ideas of the proof below.

1.2
Ideas of the proof

Theorem

1.2
essentially follows from the proof of
Theorem

1.1
, so we only discuss the latter. The proof of
Theorem

1.1
relies on the construction of a sequence of approximate solutions to the KdV dynamics (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) via a Birkhoff normal form procedure. These approximations allow us to track the probability that a large number of Fourier modes quasi-synchronize. Ensuring that this probability does not decrease too quickly over long timescales is a key idea of the proof.

In order to derive an approximate solution to the KdV dynamics, we use the Birkhoff normal form procedure to construct a close-to-the-identity local diffeomorphism
\Phi:B_{s}(0,\varepsilon)\subseteq\dot{H}^{s}\longrightarrow\dot{H}^{s}
such that

(1.11)

\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi(v)=\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2m}(\mathfrak{J})+\mathtt{R}_{r}(v),\qquad\mathfrak{J}=(|v_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}}.

where
\mathcal{H}
is the KdV Hamiltonian (
1.2
) in Fourier coordinates, and
\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{R}_{r}
is a remainder.
The number of normal form steps
r\geq 3
is chosen to ensure that the desired timescales
t\sim\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}
can be reached, while the minimal regularity
s
depends on
r
through the normal form procedure, cf. (
1.38
). We detail the main ideas in the construction of
\Phi
in
Section

1.2.3
.

In the new coordinates
v=\Phi^{-1}(u)
, the KdV equation (
1.1
) in Fourier variables reads

(1.12)

\partial_{t}v_{k}(t)=i\,\theta_{k}(\mathfrak{J}(t))\,v_{k}(t)+X_{\mathtt{R}}(v)_{k},\qquad\theta_{k}(\mathfrak{J})=\frac{k}{\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}{\partial}_{\mathfrak{J}_{k}}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2m}(\mathfrak{J}),\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\},

As justified in
Section

1.2.3
, we are able to neglect
X_{\mathtt{R}}(v)
which leads to the approximate solution

(1.13)

v_{k}(t)\approx e^{it\,\theta_{k}(\mathfrak{J}(0))}v_{k}(0),\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}.

Neglecting
X_{\mathtt{R}}(v)
is highly nontrivial, as it may be unbounded in
\dot{H}^{s}
.
In (
1.40
) we establish the approximation (
1.13
) by finding an
a priori
bound which yields control of
v
in a high norm (see (
1.37
)–(
1.39
)), allowing us to justify (
1.13
) in a low norm.

1.2.1
Non-Gaussian approximation

Although the approximation (
1.13
) depends solely on the initial datum
v(0)=\Phi^{-1}(u(0))
, the complexity of the nonlinear change of coordinates
\Phi
makes it extremely difficult to characterize its probability distribution. However, using the fact that
\Phi
is close-to-the-identity (cf. (
1.31
)), we can replace
v_{k}(0)
by
u_{k}(0)
. This leads to the following approximation of the KdV dynamics:

(1.14)

u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)=2\varepsilon\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}\,R_{k}^{\omega}\cos\left(\phi_{k}^{\omega}+t\,\theta_{k}(\vec{J}^{\omega})+kx\right),\qquad\vec{J}^{\omega}=(|\Phi^{-1}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},

where we used the explicit formulae for
u_{k}(0)=\varepsilon c_{k}\eta_{k}^{\omega}
from (
1.3
), and expressed each complex Gaussian in polar coordinates as
\eta_{k}^{\omega}=R_{k}^{\omega}e^{i\phi_{k}^{\omega}}
, where
R_{k}^{\omega}
is Rayleigh-distributed and
\phi_{k}^{\omega}
is uniformly distributed on
[0,2\pi)
.

A big advantage of the approximation (
1.14
) is that the distributions of the moduli are explicit. This allows us to estimate

(1.15)

\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{intro: approssimazione bella}}}{{\leq}}2\varepsilon\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})

up to a set of negligible probability, cf. (
4.25
). The inequality (
1.15
) leads to sharp upper bounds for the left-hand side of (
1.7
) over the desired timescales, see
Theorem

4.5
.

Obtaining sharp lower bounds for
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
is one of the main challenges in this paper. While the nonlinear phases in (
1.14
) can be ignored in the upper bound, they play a fundamental role in the lower bound. In particular, note the phases
\phi_{k}^{\omega}+t\,\theta_{k}(\vec{J}^{\omega})
are in general not uniformly distributed in
[0,2\pi)
on account of the highly nonlinear dependency of
\vec{J}^{\omega}
(via
\Phi^{-1}
) on
(\phi_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(R_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.

This issue was avoided in
[
21
]
by approximating
2
2

2

Note that for small timescales
t\ll\varepsilon^{-2}
, one can approximate
\vec{J}^{\omega}\approx 0
in (
1.14
). In this case,
\theta_{k}(0)
is precisely the dispersive relation
k^{3}
(resp.
k^{2}
in NLS) and the approximation is the linear evolution, which trivially preserves the Gaussianity of the initial data.

(1.16)

\vec{J}^{\omega}=(|\Phi^{-1}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\approx(|(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

which led to the approximation

(1.17)

\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)=2\varepsilon\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}\,R_{k}^{\omega}\cos\left(\phi_{k}^{\omega}+t\,\theta_{k}(\varepsilon\vec{R}^{\omega})+kx\right),\qquad\vec{R}^{\omega}=(R_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}.

Exploiting that
\vec{R}^{\omega}
is independent of
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
, one can prove that
\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
is Gaussian, which can be used to derive sharp lower bounds.

However, the approximation (
1.17
) is only valid over ‘‘short’’ timescales
3
3

3

This timescale is due to the fact that
\left\lVert\Phi^{-1}(u)-u\right\rVert\approx\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2}
, cf. (
2.31
), but may be longer
if this difference is cubic or quartic. The degree of this error in turn depends on the degree of the nonlinearity in the PDE.

,
since
|t\vec{J}^{\omega}-t\varepsilon\vec{R}^{\omega}|\gg 1
for timescales

t\gg\varepsilon^{-3}
.
In order to reach timescales
t\sim\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}
for any
\mathtt{n}\in\mathbb{N}
, we do not attempt to track the probability distribution of the nonlinear phases (
1.14
). Instead, we adapt a novel idea in
[
5
]
based on studying the probability of quasi-synchronization of many phases.

1.2.2
Quasi-synchronization and large deviations

For
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the quasi-synchronization event:

(1.18)

\mathfrak{M}(t;\varepsilon,M):=\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid|\varphi_{k}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\omega})\ \mbox{mod}\ 2\pi|\leq\varepsilon\quad\mbox{for}\ k=1,\ldots,M\}.

with

\varphi_{k}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi})=\phi_{k}+t\,\theta_{k}\left((|\Phi^{-1}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0;\vec{\phi}))_{j}|^{2})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\right),\qquad\vec{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{M},

where
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0;\vec{\phi})
is the “partially randomized” initial datum whose first
M
modes have deterministic phases
\vec{\phi}
, while all moduli and the rest of the phases remain random, cf. (
4.16
). Note that, when we plug in the uniformly distributed phases
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
, the nonlinear phases
\varphi_{k}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\omega})
in (
1.18
) are precisely those in (
1.14
).

For
M\gg 1
, to be fixed later, and
\omega\in\mathfrak{M}(t;\varepsilon,M)
, note that we can essentially invert (
1.15
), namely

(1.19)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{intro: approssimazione bella}}}{{\geq}}2\varepsilon\,(1-\varepsilon)\,\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+M^{1-s})

up to a set of negligible probability, cf. (
4.25
). Note that we exploit the decay of the coefficients
c_{k}
, cf. (
1.4
), to control the terms with
k>M
with high probability. In particular, we fix
M=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-\delta}\rfloor
and
s\geq\delta^{-1}
so that
o(M^{1-s})=o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})
in (
1.19
).

The key obstacle is guaranteeing that
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{M}(t;\varepsilon,M))
is not too small
4
4

4

Compared with the probability of an extreme wave
{\mathbb{P}}(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta})
which, as shown via the upper bound (
1.15
), is at most exponential in
-\varepsilon^{-2\delta}
.
as time passes, which is highly nontrivial since
\varphi(t)
is a non-explicit, nonlinear function of
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
.
To do so, we first construct a single random variable
\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}
, via a Brouwer fixed point argument, such that
\varphi(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega})=0
. Then we construct a neighborhood around this fixed point

(1.20)

\mathcal{N}(\beta):=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\,\Big|\,\left\lVert\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}-\vec{\phi}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}<\beta\right\}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}(t;\varepsilon,M)

with
\beta=\beta(t,\varepsilon,M)\in(0,\pi)
well-chosen so that the inclusion holds. An advantage of this neighborhood is that we can prove that
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{N}(\beta))=(\beta/\pi)^{M}
, cf.
4.8
. Moreover, we prove a key
factorization property
: for any event
\mathcal{A}
in the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}=\sigma((R_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in\mathbb{N}},(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j>M})
,

(1.21)

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta))={\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A})\,{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{N}(\beta))=\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)^{M}\,{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A})

despite the fact that
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
depends on the vector
\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}
, which is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable.

The factorization property (
1.21
), together with (
1.19
), allows us to obtain an explicit lower bound:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{intro:lb},\eqref{intro:facprop}}}{{\approx}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(2\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-\delta})\right)\,{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{N}(\beta))

(1.22)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-\delta}\rfloor}}{{\geq}}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}+\varepsilon^{-\delta}\,\log\frac{\beta}{\pi}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\right),

where we used sharp lower bounds for the tails of the random variable
\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}
, cf.
4.3
– see
Theorem

4.9
for the full details.

Crucially, we prove in
Theorem

4.9
that we can choose
\beta\sim t^{-1}
for
t\geq 1
while guaranteeing the inclusion property (
1.20
). This guarantees that the right-hand side in (
1.22
) is not too small over timescales
t\sim\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}
for any
\mathtt{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.

While a similar strategy was used in
[
5
]
, the size of the neighborhood (
1.20
) depended on the Lipschitz constant of the nonlinear flow. As such,
\beta
decays exponentially in
t
which, in view of (
1.22
), is an obstacle to reaching long timescales
t\sim\varepsilon^{-\mathtt{n}}
. In this work, however, we prove that
\beta
depends only on the Lipschitz constant of the flow of an integrable system
5
5

5

In our case, this integrable system is not KdV but (
1.12
) without the term
X_{\mathtt{R}}
. In fact, our strategy will yield a large deviations principle for a system which is only integrable up to order
2m
, up to timescales
t\ll\varepsilon^{-m}
.
in
action-angle coordinates
. As a result,
\beta
decays only as
t^{-1}
, which is the key to reaching the arbitrarily long polynomial timescales of
Theorem

1.1
.

1.2.3
Birkhoff normal form

Formal transformations.

Let us now explain the Birkhoff normal form procedure we use to construct the change of coordinates
\Phi
in (
1.11
). Recall the KdV Hamiltonian (
1.2
) in Fourier coordinates and the Poisson bracket:

(1.23)

\mathcal{H}(u)=\mathcal{H}_{2}(u)+\mathcal{H}_{3}(u)=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2}|u_{k}|^{2}-\frac{\pi}{3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}}u^{\bm{k}},\qquad\{F,G\}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{ik}{2\pi}\frac{{\partial}F}{{\partial}u_{k}}\frac{{\partial}G}{{\partial}u_{-k}},

where
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}
means
\bm{k}=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\})^{3}
with
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=0
and
u^{\bm{k}}=u_{k_{1}}u_{k_{2}}u_{k_{3}}

(see
Section

1.3
for the notation).
To construct
\Phi
, we make use of the full KdV hierarchy (see
[
29
,
30
,
32
,
28
]
and
Lemma

2.1
), i.e. a countable sequence of conserved quantities in involution, which can be written as:

(1.24)

F^{(j)}(u)=\sum_{n=2}^{j+2}F^{(j)}_{n}(u)=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2j}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{n=3}^{j+2}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}f_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}},\qquad\{F^{(j)},F^{(l)}\}=0\qquad\forall\,j,l\in\mathbb{N},

where
F^{(1)}=\mathcal{H}
. We construct
\Phi
as a composition of several changes of coordinates
\Phi_{n}
,
n\geq 3
. More precisely, each transformation is written as
\Phi_{n}=\Phi_{n}^{(1)}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n}^{(n-1)}
where each
\Phi_{n}^{(l)}
puts the
l
-th Hamiltonian of the KdV hierarchy into Birkhoff normal form at order
n
. In particular,
\Phi_{n}^{(l)}
is the time-1 flow of an auxiliary Hamiltonian
G_{n}^{(l)}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}g_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}u^{\bm{k}}
of degree
n
solving the homological equation:

(1.25)

F_{n}^{(l)}(u)+\{F_{2}^{(l)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}(u)=\widehat{F}_{n}^{(l)}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widehat{f}_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}u^{\bm{k}}

where, using the fact that
\{F_{2}^{(l)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}(u)=-i\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})g_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}u^{\bm{k}}
(cf. (
2.20
) below), we have that

(1.26)

\widehat{f}_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}=0\qquad\mbox{if}\quad\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})=k_{1}^{2l+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2l+1}\neq 0,\qquad\mbox{by choosing}\quad g_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}=\frac{f_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}}{i\,\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}\,\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})\neq 0}.

Although each transformation is tailored to a single Hamiltonian
F^{(l)}
in the hierarchy, the key observation is that it also eliminates non-resonant terms with respect to
F^{(l)}_{2}
in the other Hamiltonians,
without generating new terms
. This is a consequence of the fact that Hamiltonians in the KdV hierarchy Poisson-commute, cf. (
1.24
) and
[
26
, Theorem G.2]
.
More precisely, assuming the KdV hierarchy is in integrable normal form up to order
n-1
, the transformed Hamiltonians still Poisson-commute
\{\tilde{F}^{(j)},\tilde{F}^{(l)}\}=0
and the terms of degree
n
must vanish for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e.

(1.27)

\{F_{2}^{(j)},\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(l)}\}+\{\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(j)},F_{2}^{(l)}\}=0\quad\implies\quad\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})\,\widetilde{f}_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}=\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})\,\widetilde{f}_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}.

Using (
1.27
), one immediately obtains

(1.28)

\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(j)}(u)+\{F_{2}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\widetilde{f}_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})=0}\ u^{\bm{k}}.

After applying all the transformations
\Phi_{n}=\Phi_{n}^{(1)}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n}^{(n-1)}
, the only remaining terms will be supported in

(1.29)

\Omega_{0}(\bm{k})=\ldots=\Omega_{n-1}(\bm{k})=0,\qquad\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})\ \mbox{in \eqref{def: resonant relation}}.

A Vandermonde argument (cf.
Theorem

2.13
) shows that all such terms are integrable, i.e. they depend only on
(|u_{j}|^{2})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. We highlight that the cubic NLS equation on
\mathbb{T}
possesses a hierarchy of first integrals similar to (
2.9
) but with quadratic part of the form
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{j}|u_{k}|^{2}
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, see for instance
[
20
, Chapter 3]
for explicit formulae for the AKNS hierarchy
6
6

6

A completely integrable system of two coupled Schrödinger-type PDEs on with unknowns
p
and
q
which reduces to the cubic NLS equation when
\overline{p}=q
.
. As a result, the arguments presented above naturally extend to the NLS hierarchy. See also
[
17
,
18
]
for a similar idea applied to the Degasperis-Procesi equation.

Resonant normal form.

The main obstacle to making the above steps into a rigorous theorem is closing the Birkhoff normal form procedure in a common space
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
. When trying to do so, one encounters two key challenges:

(i)

A first problem is showing that the maps
\Phi_{n}^{(l)}
generated by
G_{n}^{(l)}
are well-defined in a common space, which requires compensating for the loss of derivatives induced by the Poisson bracket (
1.23
). This is easy to achieve for
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
, since the polynomials
\Omega(\bm{k})
with
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
n=3,4
, in (
1.26
) can be factorized, cf.
Section

2.4
. However, this strategy fails for
n\geq 5
, as a factorization of
\Omega(\bm{k})
with
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
is not available.

(ii)

A second problem is showing that the higher order terms produced by taking Poisson brackets of each Hamiltonian with
G_{n}^{(l)}
can be controlled in a common space
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, a significantly more challenging task than (i).

In order to overcome these difficulties, we implement a resonant normal form procedure inspired by the work of Bernier-Grébert
[
4
]
. Roughly speaking, the key idea is to truncate the support of the auxiliary Hamiltonian
G_{n}^{(l)}
to

(1.30)

\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\quad\mbox{such that}\quad\dfrac{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-1}}{|\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})|}\leq N\qquad\mbox{for some}\ N\geq 1\ \mbox{to be fixed later}.

The exponent
2l-1
reflects both the derivative loss from the Poisson bracket and the growth of the coefficients
f_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}
in (
1.24
), which are polynomial in
\bm{k}
of degree at most
2l-2
(see
Lemma

2.1
). This extends
[
4
]
, where the coefficients are bounded, and only (
1.30
) with
l=1
is needed.

The truncation in (
1.30
) readily overcomes problem (i) above, yielding a local diffeomorphism
\Phi_{n}^{(l)}
defined in a small ball
B_{s}(0,\varepsilon)\subseteq\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
satisfying

(1.31)

\left\lVert\Phi_{n}^{(l)}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim N^{n-3}\,\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}\leq N\,\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\quad\mbox{with}\ N\ll\varepsilon^{-1},

see
Lemma

2.15
below. In order to overcome problem (ii), we introduce the spaces of Hamiltonians
F(u)
such that

(1.32)

\exists\ m\geq 3\quad\mbox{such that}\quad F(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{m}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad\left\lVert\frac{c_{\bm{k}}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j-2}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty.

Thanks to the truncation (
1.30
), these spaces are closed under Poisson brackets with
G_{n}^{(l)}
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
(see
Lemma

2.17
), which is the key to solving (ii) above.

Unfortunately, this truncation induces important changes to the formal computations we did before. In particular, some terms from the Hamiltonians
F_{n}^{(j)}
are not removed by the transformation
\Phi_{n}^{(l)}
,
l=1,\ldots,n-1
, as
G_{n}^{(l)}
does not fully solve the homological equation (
1.28
). This, in turn, modifies the computation in (
1.27
), as the left-hand side of the equation now includes additional terms coming from Poisson brackets between potentially non-integrable terms, which have not been removed by the transformations
\Phi_{3},\ldots,\Phi_{n-1}
. Controlling the contribution of such additional terms is one of the main difficulties addressed in
2.24
.

All in all, we apply this resonant normal form procedure a finite number
r\geq 3
of times, which puts in approximate normal form the first
r-1
Hamiltonians of the KdV hierarchy up to degree
r
, see (
1.11
). Note that
\mathtt{R}_{r}(v)
also includes terms of degree
n\leq r
which were not removed by the transformations
\Phi_{3},\ldots,\Phi_{r}
and which are supported in the complement of (
1.30
) for some
l\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.

Let us mention an alternative normal form procedure by Kappeler and Pöschel
[
26
]
, who use the Lax pair of the KdV equation (
1.1
) to construct action-angle variables.
Here we follow a different strategy, showing that a finite number
r
of first integrals suffices to carry out
r
steps of Birkhoff normal form procedure.
This method is potentially applicable to other Hamiltonian systems, even in the absence of complete integrability, provided that the available conserved quantities satisfy suitable structural properties. On the other hand, the normal form of Kappeler–Pöschel may allow one to lower the minimal regularity
s
required in
Theorem

1.1
, a direction we plan to investigate in future work.

Dynamical consequences.

We now study the KdV dynamics in the new coordinates
v=\Phi^{-1}(u)
using the transformed Hamiltonian (
1.11
), which in Fourier variables reads as (
1.12
). The main obstacle is that we do not know whether
X_{\mathtt{R}}(v)
maps
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
into itself. Since the well-posedness of (
1.12
) follows from that of the KdV equation, it suffices to control the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm of the solution
v
to (
1.12
), which solves:

(1.33)

{\partial}_{t}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}=\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathtt{R}_{r}\}(v(t))=\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathtt{R}_{r}^{(1)}\}(v(t))+\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathtt{R}_{r}^{(2)}\}(v(t))

where

•

\mathtt{R}_{r}^{(1)}
are remainder terms of degree at least
r+1
. Using the truncation (
1.30
), we show that

(1.34)

\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathtt{R}_{r}^{(1)}\}(v(t))\lesssim_{s,r}(N\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})^{r+1}.

•

\mathtt{R}_{r}^{(2)}
is made of remainder terms of degree
n
,
5\leq n\leq r
, supported on indices
\bm{k}
in the complement of (
1.30
) for some
l=1,\ldots,n-1
. In
Theorem

2.12
, we show that such indices
\bm{k}
, when ordered such that
|k_{1}|\geq\ldots\geq|k_{n}|
, satisfy one of two possible properties:

(1.35)

\mbox{either}\quad k_{1}=-k_{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad|k_{1}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}},\qquad\mbox{or}\quad|k_{3}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}}.

Monomials satisfying the first option can be written as
v^{\bm{k}}=|v_{k_{1}}|^{2}v^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}
, where
\bm{k}^{\prime}
is the vector
\bm{k}
without the first two indices. When taking the Poisson bracket between this monomial and the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm, we obtain

(1.36)

\left\lvert\{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},v^{\bm{k}}\}\right\rvert=|v_{k_{1}}|^{2}\left\lvert\{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},v^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{intro:two_cases}}}{{\leq}}N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\,\left\lvert\{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},v^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}\right\rvert\lesssim N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n}.

Monomials satisfying the second option in (
1.35
), give rise to terms in
\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathtt{R}_{r}^{(2)}\}
with at least three large indices
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|\geq|k_{3}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}}
. We use two of these indices among which to split
2s+1
derivatives, while the third index is used to gain a factor of
N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}
, cf.
3.4
.

The
a priori
bounds (
1.34
)–(
1.36
), combined with (
1.33
), yield the quasi-conservation of
\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
over arbitrarily long polynomial timescales:

(1.37)

\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\lesssim\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}+t\,(N\,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,t],\dot{H}^{s})})^{r+1}+t\,\,N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}\,\sum_{n=5}^{r}N^{n-3}\,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,t],\dot{H}^{s})}^{n}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}

after choosing

(1.38)

N\ll\varepsilon^{-1/2},\qquad s\gtrsim r^{2}\qquad\mbox{and}\quad t\ll\varepsilon^{-r/4}.

Using similar bounds to (
1.34
)–(
1.36
), we can control the vector field
X_{\mathtt{R}}
in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T})
despite the loss of derivatives:

(1.39)

\left\lVert X_{\mathtt{R}}(v(t))\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\lesssim(N\,\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{2}})^{r}+\sum_{n=5}^{r}N^{n-3}\,\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{2}}^{n-2}\,\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}_{|k|\geq N^{1/2r}}^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{r}{2}}+N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{intro:bound1}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon^{\frac{r}{2}}.

This bound allows us to justify the approximation (
1.13
) by integrating (
1.12
):

(1.40)

v_{k}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{intro:bound2}}}{{\approx}}e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{k}(\tau)\,d\tau}v_{k}(0)\approx e^{it\,\theta_{k}(0)}v_{k}(0),

where in the second approximation we exploit the quasi-conservation of the moduli
|v_{k}(t)|
in time, as well as the fact that
\theta_{k}
depends only on these moduli
7
7

7

Let us highlight that the fact that
\theta_{k}
is real valued alone is not sufficient to justify this approximation. We critically use the integrability of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2m}
in (
1.11
). The approximation (
1.13
) is, in turn, the key to being able to choose
\beta
in (
1.20
) as
\mathcal{O}(t^{-1})
.

.

1.3
Notation

Sets and indices.

We denote by
\mathbb{N}
the set of positive natural numbers

\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,...\}
and by
\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
the set of nonzero integer numbers. Given two sets
A
and
B
, we denote by
A\sqcup B
their disjoint union, i.e.
A\cup B
such that
A\cap B=\emptyset
. We denote by
\lfloor x\rfloor
and
\lceil x\rceil
the floor and ceiling functions of
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

For
n\geq 3
and
l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we will consider the following sets of indices:

(1.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}=(k_{1},...,k_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n}\,|\,k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n}=0\},

(1.42)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}}{\mbox{Sym}(n)},\quad\text{where $\mbox{Sym}(n)$ is the symmetric group},

(1.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\,|\,|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|\geq\ldots|k_{n}|\},

(1.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\,|\,k_{1}^{2l+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2l+1}=0\},

and we set

\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{n\geq 3}\mathcal{M}_{n},\qquad\qquad\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{n\geq 3}\mathcal{D}_{n},\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 3}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}.

Given
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
n\geq 3
, we denote by
\mu_{3}(\bm{k})
the third largest (in absolute value) index in
\bm{k}
. For
M>0
, we define the sets:

(1.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq M)}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\,|\,\mu_{3}(\bm{k})\geq M\},

(1.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq M)}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\,|\,\exists\,j\neq l\quad\text{s.t.}\quad k_{j}=-k_{l},\ |k_{j}|\geq M\}.

We will denote by
\#\bm{k}
the length of the vector
\bm{k}
,
i.e.

\#\bm{k}=n
if
\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n}
.
Moreover, if
\bm{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
, we will write
u^{\bm{k}}=u_{k_{1}}\ldots u_{k_{n}}
. For two vectors
\bm{k},\bm{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we write
\bm{k}\sim\bm{j}
if
[\bm{k}]=[\bm{j}]
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
, i.e. there exists
\sigma\in\mbox{Sym}(n)
such that
k_{l}=j_{\sigma(l)}
for all
l=1,\ldots,n
.

Finally, we introduce the quantities:

(1.47)

\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})=k_{1}^{2l+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2l+1}.

Functional spaces.

For
1\leq p<\infty
, we denote by
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})
the Banach space of sequences

\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})=\biggl\{x=(x_{k})_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\,\biggl|\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|x_{k}|^{p}<\infty\biggr\}

with the usual norm
\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}=\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|x_{k}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
, and the usual generalization to
p=\infty.

We consider functions
f
defined on the torus
\mathbb{T}\cong\frac{\mathbb{R}}{2\pi\mathbb{Z}}
, which may be identified with
2\pi
-periodic functions
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we denote by
\mathcal{F}L^{s,p}(\mathbb{T})
the Banach space of measurable functions
f:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier coefficients
(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfy

\left\lVert\langle k\rangle^{s}f_{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}_{k}}<\infty,

where
\langle k\rangle=\max\{1,|k|\}
is the Japanese bracket.
In the case
p=2
these spaces coincide with the usual Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, which we identify with the space of sequences

H^{s}(\mathbb{T})=\left\{u=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k}e^{ikx}\,\bigg|\,\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k\rangle^{2s}|u_{k}|^{2}<\infty\right\}.

In the case of
s=0
, the scalar product in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
is given by

\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Since we often work with mean zero, real-valued functions, we introduce the subspace

(1.48)

\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})=\{u\in H^{s}\,|\,u_{0}=0\quad\text{and}\quad u_{-k}=\overline{u_{k}}\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\}\quad\mbox{with norm}\quad\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|k|^{2s}|u_{k}|^{2}.

Moreover, we write
B_{s}(0,r)
for the ball in
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
centered at the origin and with radius
r>0
.
Similarly, we introduce the space
\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{s,p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
of mean-zero real valued functions, with norm

(1.49)

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{s,p}}^{p}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|k|^{ps}|u_{k}|^{p}.

Finally, we often write
u_{x}
instead of
\partial_{x}u
, meaning the partial derivative of
u
with respect to
x
.

Inequalities.

We write
a\lesssim b
if there exists a positive constant
C
such that
a\leq Cb
. If the constant depends on some other quantity
p
, we will write
a\lesssim_{p}b.
When the constant
C
is explicitly written, it may change from line to line. We also employ the notation
f(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}(g(\varepsilon))
if
f(\varepsilon)\lesssim g(\varepsilon)
, and
f(\varepsilon)=o(g(\varepsilon))
if
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{f(\varepsilon)}{g(\varepsilon)}=0
.

Given functions
u,v:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we will often use the Young convolution inequality:

(1.50)

\left\lVert uv\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,r}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,p}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,q}}\qquad\text{where}\qquad 1+\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}.

We also recall the algebra property of
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
when
s>\frac{1}{2}
,

\left\lVert uv\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{s}}.

Finally, we will often use the embedding of
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
into
\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}(\mathbb{T})
which follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

(1.51)

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|u_{k}|\leq\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\langle k\rangle^{2s}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k\rangle^{2s}|u_{k}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{s}}.

Random variables.

Given a random variable
X
, i.e. a measurable function
X:(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F})\to(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))
over a probability space

(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
, we denote by
\sigma(X):=\{X^{-1}(B)\colon B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
. Given two random variables
X
and
Y
, we write
\sigma(X,Y)
for the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\sigma(X)\cup\sigma(Y)
.

Given a sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
and a random variable
X\in L^{1}(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
, we denote by
\mathbb{E}[X^{\omega}\,\lvert\,\mathcal{G}]
the conditional expectation of
X
with respect to
\mathcal{G}
.

A standard complex Gaussian is a random variable of the form

(1.52)

\eta^{\omega}=\mathrm{Re}(\eta^{\omega})+i\,\mathrm{Im}(\eta^{\omega}),

where
\mathrm{Re}(\eta^{\omega})
and
\mathrm{Im}(\eta^{\omega})
are independent, real, centered Gaussian r.v. with variance
1/2
. We will often write
\mathrm{Re}(\eta^{\omega}),\mathrm{Im}(\eta^{\omega})\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,1/2)

and
\eta^{\omega}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,1)
. In particular,

(1.53)

\mathbb{E}[\eta^{\omega}]=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[|\eta^{\omega}|^{2}\right]=1,\qquad\mathbb{E}[(\eta^{\omega})^{2}]=0.

Acknowledgements.

We thank Joackim Bernier, Alberto Maspero and Michela Procesi for many useful comments. This research was backed by the GNAMPA-INdAM and the European Union. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

2
Birkhoff normal form for the KdV hierarchy

2.1
Preliminaries

In this section we study the periodic
KdV equation with real-valued initial datum:

(2.1)

\begin{cases}u_{t}+u_{xxx}+uu_{x}=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{T}\\
u|_{t=0}=u(0)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}),\quad s\geq 0.\end{cases}

from a deterministic point of view.
The KdV equation admits the following formally conserved quantities:

(2.2)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(t,x)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Using the conserved
L^{2}
-norm, one can show that the KdV equation admits a unique real-valued global solution in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s\geq 0
, see
[
15
, Chapter 3]
and the references therein for a detailed presentation of the well-posedness theory.

Moreover, using the conserved mean (
2.2
), it suffices to study the KdV flow for zero-mean initial data. From now on, we can thus consider
u(0)\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, and the global-in-time solution will have zero mean.

The KdV equation is a Hamiltonian system which can be written as

(2.3)

{\partial}_{t}u={\partial}_{x}\nabla_{L^{2}}H,\qquad\mbox{with}\qquad H(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(u_{x}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}u^{3})\,\mathrm{d}x.

where
\nabla_{L^{2}}H
denotes the
L^{2}
-gradient of
H
, which represents the Fréchet derivative of
H
with respect to the standard scalar product in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. In the phase space
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
we will use the Fourier coefficients as coordinates

(2.4)

u_{k}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(t,x)e^{-ikx}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\quad u_{0}=0,\quad\overline{u_{k}}=u_{-k}

where the last equality follows from the fact that
u
is real-valued.
In the Fourier setting, the Hamiltonian in (
2.3
) reads (
1.23
).
The Poisson tensor in (
2.3
) is associated to the Poisson bracket (
1.23
)
which is (at least formally) bilinear, antisymmetric and satisfies the Jacobi identity.
We will prove that it is well-defined on the set of formal polynomials (cf.
Definition

2.2
) and meaningful if
u
belongs to some
\dot{H}^{s}
for
s
sufficiently large (cf.
Lemma

2.8
).

All in all, the KdV equation (
2.1
) can be written in Fourier variables as

(2.5)

{\partial}_{t}u_{k}=\{u_{k},\mathcal{H}\}=ik^{3}u_{k}-\frac{ik}{2}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}u_{k_{1}}u_{k_{2}}.

First integrals.

As in any autonomous Hamiltonian system, the Hamiltonian is conserved along its own flow.
Moreover, all the functionals
F
that Poisson-commute with the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
,
i.e.

\{F,\mathcal{H}\}=0,
are preserved by the flow.
Such functionals are called first integrals, or simply conserved quantities.
It is well-known
[
29
,
32
,
19
,
33
]
that the KdV equation has infinitely many conserved quantities of the form

(2.6)

F^{(j)}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}P^{(j)}(u,{\partial}_{x}u,\ldots,{\partial}_{x}^{j}u)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
P^{(j)}
is a polynomial (whose monomials are of degree
\geq 2
) with rational coefficients. In particular,
F^{(1)}=H
, cf. (
2.3
).
These quantities, known as KdV hierarchy, are in involution, i.e.

(2.7)

\{F^{(j)},F^{(l)}\}=0\qquad\forall\,j,l\in\mathbb{N}.

This will be crucial, as we will use the full KdV hierarchy in order to put the KdV Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in integrable normal form (cf.
Definition

2.7
) up to a small remainder.
In the following lemma, which is proven in
Section

A.1
, we collect some elementary albeit important properties of the KdV hierarchy.

Lemma 2.1

(Structure of the KdV hierarchy)
.

For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(2.8)

F^{(j)}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left[\left({\partial}_{x}^{j}u\right)^{2}+\sum_{n=3}^{j+2}P_{n}(u,{\partial}_{x}u,\ldots,{\partial}_{x}^{j-1}u)\right]\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
P_{n}
is a polynomial of degree
n
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
, which in Fourier can be written as

(2.9)

F^{(j)}(u)=\sum_{n=2}^{j+2}F^{(j)}_{n}(u)=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2j}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{n=3}^{j+2}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}

where for
n\geq 3
and
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}=c(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})^{(j)}
is a polynomial in
n
variables with rational coefficients and of degree at most
2j-2
.
Moreover, the reality condition (
2.13
) holds. Each term
F^{(j)}_{n}(u)
will be called homogeneous
j
-formal polynomial of degree
n
(cf.
Definition

2.2
).

Functional setting and formal polynomials.

Taking into account that
\mathcal{H}=F^{(1)}
, the coefficients
c_{\bm{k}}^{(1)}
are bounded uniformly in
\bm{k}
, cf. (
1.23
) and (
2.9
). As a result,

c_{\bm{k}}^{(1)}\in\{\bm{c}=(c_{\bm{k}})_{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}\,\lvert\,c_{-\bm{k}}=\overline{c_{\bm{k}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert\bm{c}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty\}.

This boundedness property of the coefficients does not hold for the whole KdV hierarchy.
However,
Lemma

2.1
guarantees that
c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
, is a polynomial in
\bm{k}
of degree at most
2l-2
.
In order to exploit this property, for
n\geq 3
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

(2.10)

\begin{split}Y_{n}^{l}=\bigg\{\bm{c}=(c_{\bm{k}})_{{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\,&\bigg|\,c_{-\bm{k}}=\overline{c_{\bm{k}}}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\left\lVert\bm{c}\right\rVert_{Y^{l}}=\left\lVert\frac{c_{\bm{k}}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty\bigg\}.\end{split}

In particular,
\bm{c}^{(l)}\in Y^{l}
for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

In
Theorem

2.18
we will perform canonical transformations which modify the structure of the whole KdV hierarchy in order to put it in normal form.
The spaces (
2.10
) will be preserved by such transformations, in the sense that the new coefficients of
F_{n}^{(j)}
will belong again to
Y_{n}^{j}.

Definition 2.2

(Formal polynomials)
.

Given
l\in\mathbb{N}
, an
l
-
formal polynomial

F
is a Hamiltonian of the form:

(2.11)

F(u)=F_{2}(u)+F_{\geq 3}(u)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}b_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}

such that there exist
M>0
such that

(2.12)

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{|b_{k}|}{|k|^{2l}}<\infty,\qquad\sup_{n\geq 3}\left\lVert(c_{\bm{k}}M^{-\#{\bm{k}}})_{\#{\bm{k}}=n}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}<\infty.

F_{2}(u)
is called homogeneous
l
-formal polynomial of degree 2, or simply quadratic
l
-formal polynomial, while for
n\geq 3
each
F_{n}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}
is called homogeneous
l
-formal polynomial of degree
n
.
8
8

8

In
[
7
, Definition 1]
, a definition of homogeneous polynomials between Banach spaces is given: notice that
F_{n}
,
n\geq 2
, in (
2.11
) is a homogeneous polynomial according to that definition.

If
F
is a real valued function, it means that its coefficients in (
2.11
) satisfy the
reality condition

(2.13)

\overline{b_{k}}=b_{-k}\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\qquad\qquad\overline{c_{\bm{k}}}=c_{-\bm{k}}\quad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}.

Remark 2.3
.

We note the class of
l
-formal polynomials is closed under addition and scalar multiplication, as is the class of homogeneous
l
-formal polynomials of a fixed degree.

The next lemma shows that condition (
2.12
)
guarantees that formal polynomials of the form (
2.11
) are smooth functionals on a small ball of
\dot{H}^{s}
.
This lemma generalizes
[
4
, Lemma 3.2]

for the functionals in (
2.9
) whose coefficients depend polynomially in
\bm{k}
. In particular,
[
4
, Lemma 3.2]
corresponds to the case
l=1
in (
2.12
).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
F
be an
l
-formal polynomial (cf.
Definition

2.2
),
l\in\mathbb{N}
, with coefficients
\bm{c}
satisfying (
2.12
) for some
M>0
. For any
s\geq l
,
F(u)
is a smooth function on
B_{s}\left(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}M}\right)
where
c_{s}=\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{j^{2s}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3}}
.
Here,
B_{s}(0,r)
is the ball of
\dot{H}^{s}
centered at the origin and with radius
r>0
, cf.
Section

1.3
.

Proof.

Note that
F_{2}(u)
in (
2.11
) is absolutely convergent with series bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{l}}^{2})
on account of (
2.12
). As a result,
F_{2}(u)
is well-defined for
u\in\dot{H}^{s}
since
s\geq l
.

Since
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{n\geq 3}\mathcal{M}_{n}
, we may write
F_{\geq 3}(u)=\sum_{n\geq 3}F_{n}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}
, which is a function at least cubic in
u
.
The homogeneous polynomial
F_{n}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}
satisfies

(2.14)

\displaystyle|F_{n}(u)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}|c_{\bm{k}}||u^{\bm{k}}|=M^{n}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\frac{|c_{\bm{k}}|M^{-\#\bm{k}}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}}\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}|u^{\bm{k}}|

\displaystyle\leq M^{n}\,\left\lVert(c_{\bm{k}}M^{-\#{\bm{k}}})_{\#{\bm{k}}=n}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}|u^{\bm{k}}|\leq nCM^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}|k_{1}|^{2l-2}|u^{\bm{k}}|

with
C=\sup_{n\geq 3}\left\lVert(c_{\bm{k}}M^{-\#{\bm{k}}})_{\#{\bm{k}}=n}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}
, where the multiplication by
n
is due to the ordering
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|
.
Indeed, the sum in
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
can be split into
n
sums depending on which component
k_{j}
is maximal. Each of these sums coincides with the sum over
k_{1}
by a change of variables.

Since
l\geq 1
, the Jensen inequality and the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
(
1.41
) yield

(2.15)

|k_{1}|^{l-1}=|k_{2}+\ldots+k_{n}|^{l-1}\leq(n-1)^{l-2}\left(|k_{2}|^{l-1}+\ldots+|k_{n}|^{l-1}\right)

which we will use frequently.
Therefore

\begin{split}|F_{n}(u)|&\leq Cn(n-1)^{l-2}M^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}|k_{1}|^{l-1}\left(|k_{2}|^{l-1}+\ldots+|k_{n}|^{l-1}\right)|u^{\bm{k}}|\\
&\leq Cn(n-1)^{l-1}M^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}|k_{1}|^{l-1}|k_{2}|^{l-1}|u^{\bm{k}}|,\end{split}

where in the second sum we used the symmetry.
By the Young convolution inequality (
1.50
),

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}|k_{1}|^{l-1}|k_{2}|^{l-1}|u^{\bm{k}}|&\leq\left\lVert\left(|{\partial}_{x}|^{l-1}u\right)^{2}\cdot u^{n-2}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{0,\infty}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{l-1}}^{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{0,1}}^{n-2}.\\
\end{split}

where
|{\partial}_{x}|
is the Fourier multiplier operator with symbol
|k|
.
By (
1.51
),

(2.16)

|F_{n}(u)|\leq Cn^{l}\left(c_{s}M\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{n}

and the thesis follows from the convergence criterion for power series.

Finally, the smoothness of
F
follows from the following estimate (which replaces (
2.16
)):

\left\lVert\mathrm{d}F_{n}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{s},\mathbb{R})}\leq Cc_{s}Mn^{l+1}\,\left(c_{s}M\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{n-1}.

The series in
n
has thus the same radius of convergence as that of
F
. Higher-order derivatives are bounded analogously.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
F
be an
l
-formal polynomial (cf.
Definition

2.2
),
l\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

(2.17)

F(u)=\sum_{n\geq 2}F_{n}(u)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}b_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}=0

for all
u\in B_{s}(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}M})
(cf.
Lemma

2.4
). Then
b_{k}=-b_{-k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and

(2.18)

\mathtt{c}([\bm{k}])=\sum_{\bm{j}\sim\bm{k}}c_{\bm{j}}=0\qquad\mbox{for all}\ \bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}\ \ \mbox{(cf.\penalty\ \eqref{def: indices zero momentum quotient})}.

Proof.

By contradiction, let
n
be the smallest integer for which
F_{n}\neq 0
.
Then

(2.19)

F_{n}(u)=-\sum_{m>n}F_{m}(u)\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Fn_growth}}}{{\implies}}\quad|F_{n}(u)|\lesssim_{n}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n+1}.

If
n=2
, there must exists some
b_{k}\neq 0
. Consider
u
such that
u_{k}=u_{-k}=\epsilon
and
u_{j}=0
if
j\neq{\pm k}
. By (
2.19
),

|F_{2}(u)|=|b_{k}+b_{-k}|\,\epsilon^{2}\lesssim\epsilon^{3},

which implies that
b_{k}+b_{-k}=0
. If
n\geq 3
, we use (
1.42
) to write

F_{n}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}\,u^{\bm{k}}=\sum_{[\bm{k}]\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}}\mathtt{c}([\bm{k}])\,u^{\bm{k}},\qquad\mbox{where}\ \mathtt{c}([\bm{k}])=\sum_{\bm{j}\sim\bm{k}}c_{\bm{j}}.

Since
F_{n}\neq 0
, there exists
[\bm{k}]
such that
\mathtt{c}([\bm{k}])\neq 0
. Define

\mathfrak{A}=\{\bm{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\mid j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}\},\quad\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathfrak{A}/\mbox{Sym}(n).

Consider
u
in the subspace of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
given by
u_{r}=0
if
r\notin\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}
which we identify with
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
m=|\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}|\leq n
. Then

F_{n}(u)=\sum_{[\bm{j}]\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathtt{c}([\bm{j}])\,u^{\bm{j}}

is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
n
in
m
variables.
Since
F_{n}\neq 0
, there exists some
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
F_{n}(v)\neq 0
. Setting
u=\epsilon v
, and using (
2.19
), we find

|F_{n}(v)|\,\epsilon^{n}\lesssim\epsilon^{n+1}.

The contradiction follows by taking
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

As a consequence of
Lemma

2.4
, a formal polynomial
F
is smooth and

\frac{{\partial}F}{{\partial}u_{k}}(u)=\mathrm{d}F(u)[e^{ikx}]\qquad\implies\qquad\{u_{k},F\}=\frac{ik}{2\pi}\mathrm{d}F(u)[e^{-ikx}]\in\mathbb{C}

is well-defined for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

Next we introduce a special class of
l
-formal polynomials which Poisson-commute (see
Lemma

2.8
(iii) below).

Definition 2.7
.

Given an
l
-formal polynomial
F=F(u)
(cf.
Definition

2.2
), we say that it is
integrable
or in
integrable normal form
, if
F=F(I)
, where
I=(I_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}=(|u_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}.

The following result shows that Poisson brackets between formal polynomials are well-defined in a ball of
\dot{H}^{s}
for sufficiently large
s\geq 1
.
Moreover we prove that the Poisson bracket between a formal polynomial and the quadratic part
F_{2}^{(j)}
of a Hamiltonian of the KdV hierarchy (
2.9
) is almost the same formal polynomial but where the coefficients
g_{\bm{k}}
are multiplied by
-i\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})
, where
\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})
is (
1.47
).
Finally we prove that integrable formal polynomials Poisson-commute.

Lemma 2.8
.

For
j,l\in\mathbb{N}
, let
F
be a
j
-formal polynomial and
G
an
l
-formal polynomial

F(u)=F_{2}(u)+F_{\geq 3}(u)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}a_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}f_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad G(u)=G_{2}(u)+G_{\geq 3}(u)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}b_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}

satisfying (
2.12
) with
M_{F}
and
M_{G}
, respectively.
Then we have the following facts:

(i) The function
\{F,G\}(u)
is a
(j+l+1)
-formal polynomial with
M_{\{F,G\}}>\max(M_{F},M_{G})
, cf. (
2.12
).

(ii) If
F_{2}(u)=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|k|^{2j}|u_{k}|^{2}
, then

(2.20)

\{F_{2},G_{\geq 3}\}(u)=-i\sum_{n\geq 3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}g_{\bm{k}}\,\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})u^{\bm{k}},

where
\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})
is (
1.47
), and this Poisson bracket is smooth in
B_{s}(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}M_{G}})
for
s\geq j+l-\frac{1}{2}
.

(iii) Letting
I=(I_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}=(|u_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
, if
F
and
G
are integrable (cf.
Definition

2.7
)
then
\{F,G\}=0
.

Proof.

(ii) By
Lemma

2.4
, for
s\geq\max\{j,l\}
,
F(u)
is a smooth function of
u
in
B_{s}\left(0,\frac{1}{c_{j}M_{F}}\right)
and
G(u)
is a smooth function of
u
in
B_{s}\left(0,\frac{1}{c_{l}M_{G}}\right)
, the series being absolutely convergent.
In particular,
{\partial}_{u_{k}}F
and
{\partial}_{u_{k}}G
are well defined, cf.
2.6
. We therefore have:

(2.21)

\{F_{2},G_{\geq 3}\}(u)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}ir^{2j+1}\overline{u_{r}}\frac{{\partial}G_{\geq 3}}{{\partial}u_{-r}}=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}ir^{2j+1}\overline{u_{r}}\sum_{n\geq 3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\left[\delta(k_{1}=-r)+\ldots+\delta(k_{n}=-r)\right]g_{\bm{k}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{u_{-r}}.

We check that this series is absolutely convergent using Tonelli’s theorem.
Using the fact that
u_{-r}=\overline{u_{r}}
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{n\geq 3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}|r|^{2j+1}\left[\delta(k_{1}=-r)+\ldots+\delta(k_{n}=-r)\right]|g_{\bm{k}}||u^{\bm{k}}|\\
&\lesssim\sum_{n\geq 3}M_{G}^{n}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\left(|k_{1}|^{2j+1}+\ldots+|k_{n}|^{2j+1}\right)\frac{|g_{\bm{k}}|M_{G}^{-n}}{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}}\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}|u^{\bm{k}}|\\
&\lesssim\sup_{n\geq 3}\left\lVert(g_{\bm{k}}M_{G}^{-\#{\bm{k}}})_{\#{\bm{k}}=n}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\,\sum_{n\geq 3}M_{G}^{n}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}n\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j+2l-1}|u^{\bm{k}}|\end{split}

which is analogous to (
2.14
). Thus, arguing as in
Lemma

2.4
, the series above is absolutely convergent in
B_{s}(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}M_{G}})
for
s\geq j+l-\frac{1}{2}.

It follows that (
2.20
) holds by Fubini’s theorem. In particular,
\{F_{2},G_{\geq 3}\}(u)
is a
(j+l)
-formal polynomial.

(i) Firstly, notice that

(2.22)

\{F,G\}=\{F_{2}+F_{\geq 3},G_{2}+G_{\geq 3}\}=\{F_{2},G_{2}\}+\{F_{2},G_{\geq 3}\}+\{F_{\geq 3},G_{2}\}+\{F_{\geq 3},G_{\geq 3}\}.

Our goal is to show that each of these terms is well-defined for
u
in
B_{s}\left(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}M_{\{F,G\}}}\right)
for
s
sufficiently large and that they are formal polynomials. Note that the terms
\{F_{2},G_{\geq 3}\}
and
\{F_{\geq 3},G_{2}\}
are well-defined
(j+l)
-formal polynomials by point (ii) above and (
2.12
).

We start with the first term on the right-hand side of (
2.22
). Note that:

\{F_{2},G_{2}\}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{ik}{2\pi}\,|u_{k}|^{2}\,(a_{k}+a_{-k})(b_{-k}+b_{k})=0

by symmetry
k\mapsto-k
. Since
|a_{k}|\lesssim|k|^{2j}
and
|b_{k}|\lesssim|k|^{2l}
(cf. (
2.12
)) the series above is absolutely convergent for
u\in\dot{H}^{s}
with

(2.23)

s\geq j+l+1/2.

In particular,
\{F_{2},G_{2}\}
is trivially a
(j+l+1)
-formal polynomial.

Finally, we tackle the last term on the right-hand side of (
2.22
).

(2.24)

\begin{split}&\{F_{\geq 3},G_{\geq 3}\}(u)=\left\{\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}f_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}},\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\right\}\\
&=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{ir}{2\pi}\left(\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}\left(\delta(k_{1}=r)+\ldots+\delta(k_{\#\bm{k}}=r)\right)f_{\bm{k}}\right)\left(\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}\left(\delta(k_{1}^{\prime}=-r)+\ldots+\delta(k_{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=-r)\right)g_{\bm{k}}\right)\frac{u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{|u_{r}|^{2}}.\end{split}

We next show that the series converges absolutely. To do so, we note that for
\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}

(2.25)

\begin{split}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|r|\,[\delta(k_{1}=r)+\ldots+\delta(k_{\#\bm{k}}=r)]\,&[\delta(k_{1}^{\prime}=-r)+\ldots+\delta(k_{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=-r)]\frac{\lvert u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\rvert}{|u_{r}|^{2}}\\
&=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\#\bm{k}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}|k_{\alpha}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|k_{\beta}^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta(k_{\alpha}=-k_{\beta}^{\prime})\frac{\lvert u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert u_{k_{\alpha}}u_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}\rvert},\end{split}

We are ready to prove the absolute convergence of (
2.24
), using Tonelli’s theorem:

(
2.24
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:A_poisson}}}{{\lesssim}}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}|f_{\bm{k}}||g_{\bm{k}}|\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\#\bm{k}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}|k_{\alpha}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|k_{\beta}^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta(k_{\alpha}=-k_{\beta}^{\prime})\frac{\lvert u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert u_{k_{\alpha}}u_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}\rvert}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{M_cond}}}{{\lesssim}}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}M_{F}^{\#\bm{k}}M_{G}^{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j-2}\left\lVert\bm{k}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-2}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\#\bm{k}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}|k_{\alpha}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|k_{\beta}^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta(k_{\alpha}=-k_{\beta}^{\prime})\frac{\lvert u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert u_{k_{\alpha}}u_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}\rvert}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{n,m\geq 3}M_{F}^{n}M_{G}^{m}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{\beta=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{m}\end{subarray}}\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j-\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{k}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-\frac{3}{2}}\,\delta(k_{\alpha}=-k_{\beta}^{\prime})\frac{\lvert u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\rvert}{\lvert u_{k_{\alpha}}u_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}\rvert}.

We now note that the inner sum in
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{m}
is independent of
\alpha
and
\beta
on account of the permutation invariance of
\mathcal{M}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{m}
.
As a result,

(2.27)

\eqref{eq:FGabs_pre}\lesssim\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}\#\bm{k}\#\bm{k}^{\prime}M_{F}^{\#\bm{k}}M_{G}^{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}\delta(k_{1}=-k_{1}^{\prime})\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j-\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{k}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{|u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}|}{|u_{k_{1}}|^{2}}.

We now define the new vector
\bar{\bm{k}}=(k_{2},\ldots,k_{n},k_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,k_{m}^{\prime})
, and note that
\forall\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,

(2.28)

\frac{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}}{\max(|k_{2}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: jensen inequality}}}{{\leq}}\ (n-1).

As a result,

\begin{split}\eqref{eq:FGabs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:jensen2}}}{{\lesssim}}&\sum_{\alpha\geq 4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m\geq 3\\
n+m-2=\alpha\end{subarray}}n^{2j-\frac{1}{2}}m^{2l-\frac{1}{2}}M_{F}^{n}M_{G}^{m}\sum_{\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\max(|\bar{k}_{1}|,\ldots,|\bar{k}_{\alpha}|)^{2j+2l-3}|u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}|\\
\lesssim&\sum_{\alpha\geq 4}\alpha^{2j+2l-1}\max(M_{F},M_{G})^{\alpha+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m\geq 3\\
n+m-2=\alpha\end{subarray}}\sum_{\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\left\lVert\bar{\bm{k}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j+2l-3}|u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}|\\
\lesssim&\sum_{\alpha\geq 4}\sum_{\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\alpha^{2j+2l}\max(M_{F},M_{G})^{\alpha+2}\left\lVert\bar{\bm{k}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j+2l-3}|u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}|.\end{split}

which is analogous to (
2.14
). Thus, arguing as in
Lemma

2.4
, the series is absolutely convergent for
u\in B_{s}\left(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}M_{\{F,G\}}}\right)
with
M_{\{F,G\}}>\max(M_{F},M_{G})
and

(2.29)

s\geq j+l-\frac{3}{2}.

Having established the absolute convergence of the series in (
2.24
), we may exchange the order of summation and conclude that
\{F_{\geq 3},G_{\geq 3}\}(u)
is a
(j+l-1)
-formal polynomial of the form:

(2.30)

\displaystyle\{F_{\geq 3},G_{\geq 3}\}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\geq 4}\sum_{\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}d_{\bar{\bm{k}}}u^{\bar{\bm{k}}},\quad\mbox{where}

\displaystyle d_{\bar{\bm{k}}}=\frac{-i}{2\pi}\!\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}\sum_{\beta=1}^{m}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bar{k}_{j}\big)\cdot

\displaystyle f(\bar{k}_{1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{\gamma-1},\!\!\!\sum_{j=n}^{n+m-2}\!\!\!\!\bar{k}_{j},\bar{k}_{\gamma},\ldots,\bar{k}_{n-1})g(\bar{k}_{n},\ldots,\bar{k}_{n+\beta-2},\!\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bar{k}_{j},\bar{k}_{n+\beta-1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{n+m-2}).

The proof is concluded by choosing the largest integer among (
2.23
), (
2.29
) and point (ii).

(iii) By point (i),
\{F,G\}(u)
is a smooth function in some open ball of
\dot{H}^{s}
for
s
sufficiently large.
By the absolute convergence of the series defining
\{F,G\}(u)
and the bilinearity of the Poisson bracket, it suffices to prove that

\{I^{\bm{k}},I^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}=0

for any
\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n}
and
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{m}
. Moreover, by the Leibniz rule:

\{I^{\bm{k}},I^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\{I_{k_{i}},I_{k_{j}^{\prime}}\}\prod_{\alpha\neq i,\ \beta\neq j}I_{k_{\alpha}}I_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}.

Therefore it suffices to prove that
for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\{u_{k_{1}}\overline{u}_{k_{1}},u_{k_{2}}\overline{u}_{k_{2}}\}=0,

which follows from a straightforward computation using (
1.23
).
∎

Remark 2.9
.

Note that in general the Poisson bracket loses derivatives, in the sense that any
j_{1},j_{2}
-formal polynomials
F
and
G
are well defined in a ball around the origin of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
if
s=\max(j_{1},j_{2})
, while a priori
\{F,G\}
is not.
This is an important obstacle to the convergence of the standard Birkhoff normal form procedure (
2.41
), as it requires an infinite amount of Poisson brackets.

The key idea is that these brackets are not between
any
formal polynomials, but between the KdV Hamiltonian and the (approximate) solution of a homological equation, see (
2.74
) below. These appoximate solutions will be chosen in such a way to avoid the loss of derivatives, see
Lemma

2.17
.

2.2
Birkhoff normal form

In this section we construct a symplectic transformation
\Phi
in a neighborhood of the origin of
\dot{H}^{s}
, for
s>0
large enough, such that the KdV Hamiltonian (
1.23
) in the new variables is, up to some remainders, in normal form.

Theorem

2.10
below is a corollary of
Lemma

2.30
,
Lemma

2.31
and the more general
Theorem

2.18
below (see also
2.19
), which put the whole KdV hierarchy in normal form.
The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

2.10
, which has interesting implications on the dynamics, as explained in
Section

3
.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 2r
. There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(r)
and a constant
c(r,s)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and all
\epsilon>0
such that
\epsilon\leq c(r,s)N^{-1}
the following holds.
There exists an invertible symplectic map
\Phi:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon)
with inverse
\Phi^{-1}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,\epsilon)

which are close to the identity in the sense that

(2.31)

\left\lVert\Phi^{\pm 1}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{r,s}N\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},

and Lipschitz-continuous:

(2.32)

\left\lVert\Phi^{\pm 1}(u)-\Phi^{\pm 1}(\underline{u})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert u-\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

Moreover,

(2.33)

\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi=\sum_{n=1}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}+R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}+R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}+R_{\geq r+1},

where

(2.34)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2}|u_{k}|^{2},

(2.35)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{4}(u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{|u_{k}|^{4}}{k^{2}},

(2.36)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}(u)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}(I)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n}}b_{\bm{k}}I^{\bm{k}}\qquad\text{where $I_{k}=|u_{k}|^{2}$ and $|b_{\bm{k}}|\lesssim_{n}N^{2n-3}$},

(2.37)

\displaystyle R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}\\
5\leq\#\bm{k}\leq r\\
|k_{3}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}\qquad\text{with $|c_{\bm{k}}|\lesssim_{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-3}$},

(2.38)

\displaystyle R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=\lceil N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\rceil}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}\\
3\leq\#\bm{k}\leq r-2\end{subarray}}c_{m,\bm{k}}I_{m}u^{\bm{k}}\qquad\text{with $|c_{m,\bm{k}}|\lesssim_{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-1}$},

(2.39)

\displaystyle R_{\geq r+1}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}\\
\#\bm{k}\geq r+1\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}\qquad\text{where $|c_{\bm{k}}|\leq\rho^{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-3}$ with $\rho\lesssim_{r}1$}

and all the Hamiltonian terms have real coefficients and satisfy the reality condition (
2.13
), cf.
2.16
below.

Remark 2.11
.

Let us make some comments.

•

The transformation
\Phi
is defined in a neighborhood of the origin of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
of maximal radius
\sim_{r,s}N^{-1}
.
Equation (
2.31
) tells us that
\Phi
is close to the identity provided that
N
is not too large.
In
Theorem

3.1
, we will carefully fix a value
N
in order to obtain valuable information on the dynamics of the KdV equation over long timescales.

•

It is possible to weaken the condition
s\geq 2r
to
s\geq 1
. In particular,
\Phi
also maps
B_{\sigma}(0,\epsilon)
to
B_{\sigma}(0,2\epsilon)
for any
1\leq\sigma\leq s
. We did not optimize the regularity since our approach will require a large
s
in order to control the dynamics over long timescales, cf.
Theorem

3.1
.
See
2.29
for additional details on how to lower the regularity.

•

The formal polynomials (
2.37
)-(
2.38
) are supported on
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}})}
, cf. (
1.45
)-(
1.46
).
They are remainders if
N
is sufficiently large, as proved in
3.4
.
In particular, they are almost negligible for the dynamics, cf.
Theorem

3.5
.

•

Larger values of
r
lead to good approximations over longer timescales (cf.
Theorem

3.1
), but require more regularity
s\geq 2r
to be well-defined.
For fixed
r
,
R_{\geq r+1}
in (
2.39
) is a 1-formal polynomial of order
\geq r+1
, which is small in a neighborhood of the origin. Moreover, the flow of
R_{\geq r+1}
almost preserves the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm over long timescales, cf.
3.4
.

•

The terms (
2.36
) are integrable (cf.
Definition

2.7
). By
Lemma

2.8
-(iii), they Poisson-commute with the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm.
This means that, if there were no remainders, the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm of the flow of the Hamiltonian would be conserved.
In particular all the Fourier moduli would be conserved by the flow and the dynamics would affect only the phases.
We will see that this is approximately true, at least for polynomial timescales
T\sim\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{-m}
, for arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{N}
and initial datum
u_{0}
sufficiently regular and small.

The mapping
\Phi
in
Theorem

2.10
is the composition of several maps

(2.40)

\Phi=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{4}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{r},\qquad r\geq 3.

Following the Birkhoff normal form procedure, each transformation
\Phi_{n}
is the composition of time-1 flows associated to auxiliary Hamiltonians, so that the dynamics in the new coordinates is simpler.

The normal form.

Let us explain the basic idea to construct the transformations
\Phi
in (
2.40
).
Given coordinates
v\in\dot{H}^{s}
, for some
s\geq 0
, the coordinates
u=u(\xi)|_{\xi=1}=\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(v)|_{\xi=1}
are the solution to the initial-value problem:

\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\xi}u_{k}=\{u_{k},G\}\\
u_{k}(\xi)|_{\xi=0}=v_{k}\end{cases}

where
G
is an appropriately chosen Hamiltonian, and where we use the letter
\xi
to indicate the auxiliary time of the flow of
G
, to avoid confusion with the time
t
of the KdV equation.

The derivatives of such transformations are related to the Poisson bracket in the following way:

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}\xi^{\alpha}}(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi})=\{\{\{\mathcal{H},\underbrace{G\},G\}\ldots\}}_{\alpha\ \mathrm{times}}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi}=\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi},\qquad\alpha\in\mathbb{N},

where
\mathrm{ad}_{G}=\{\cdot,G\}
.
This allows us to compute the Taylor formula up to order
m\in\mathbb{N}
centered at
\xi=0
, which we evaluate at
\xi=1
:

(2.41)

\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{G}^{1}=\mathcal{H}+\sum_{\alpha=0}^{m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\,\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{\alpha}\mathcal{H}+\frac{1}{m!}\,\int_{0}^{1}(1-\xi)^{m}\,\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{m+1}\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\xi,

provided that
\Phi_{G}^{\xi}
is well defined up to
\xi=1
.

The rigorous justification of (
2.41
) for the transformations in (
2.40
) follows from
Lemma

2.15
.
The convergence of the Taylor series – including bounds on the remainder – are some of the key elements in the proof of
Theorem

2.10
, cf.
2.20
and
Theorem

2.18
.

The transformation
\Phi_{G}
will be proved to be invertible in a neighborhood around the origin of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
(cf.
Theorem

2.18
), and it preserves the Poisson bracket, a property which will greatly simplify our computations: for two formal polynomials
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
we have:

(2.42)

\{F_{1},F_{2}\}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi}=\{F_{1}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi},F_{2}\circ\Phi_{G}^{\xi}\}.

This follows from the fact that
\Phi_{G}^{\xi}
is symplectic, together with the convergence of the Taylor series (
2.41
) and elementary properties of formal polynomials, see
Section

2.1
.

Let us briefly explain how to construct the maps
\Phi_{n}
in (
2.40
). The maps
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
, which are time-1 flows associated to auxiliary Hamiltonians
G_{3}
and
G_{4}
, are simple because the resonant set
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{1}
,
n=3,4
, is explicit since the resonant relation
\Omega(\bm{k})
in (
1.47
) factorizes for any
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, see
Lemma

2.30
. In particular, the Hamiltonians
G_{n}
,
n=3,4
, are explicit and gain derivatives, which compensates the loss of derivatives in the Poisson bracket (
1.23
).

For
n\geq 5
, the resonant relation
\Omega(\bm{k})
does not factorize, and compensating the loss of derivatives in (
1.23
) becomes a major obstacle.
In order to overcome this, we construct

(2.43)

\Phi_{n}=\Phi_{n}^{(1)}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n}^{(n-1)},

where each
\Phi_{n}^{(l)}
is a time-1 flow of a Hamiltonian
G_{n}^{(l)}
in (
2.51
),
l=1,\ldots,n-1
. This Hamiltonian is supported on a special set of indices
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
defined in (
2.45
), in such a way that
\Phi_{n}
cancels the monomials of degree
n
in the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n-1}
supported on the set
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
. The rest of this subsection is devoted to proving two key properties of these transformations:

•

\Phi_{n}
does not cancel any monomials in
\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n-1}
supported on

(2.44)

\mathfrak{J}_{n,N}=\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{l=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}\right)\ .

In particular, we shall prove that all terms supported on
\mathfrak{J}_{n,N}
are either integrable (
2.36
) or correspond to remainders (
2.37
)-(
2.38
), cf.
Theorem

2.12
.

•

The choice of set of indices
