\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
guarantees that the Hamiltonian vector field associated to
G_{n}^{(l)}
maps
\dot{H}^{s}
into itself (cf.
Lemma

2.15
), and that the Poisson bracket of
G_{n}^{(l)}
with formal polynomials does not lose derivatives (cf.
Lemma

2.17
).

We start by constructing the sets
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
for all
n\geq 3
. As mentioned before, the cases
n=3,4
are simpler and can be treated directly, cf.
Section

2.4
. However, since our construction is necessary for
n\geq 5
, we prefer to present all cases simultaneously.

The support of the maps
\Phi_{n}
.

For
n\geq 3
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
, and
N\gg 1
to be fixed later, we define:

(2.45)

\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}=\left\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l}\,\bigg|\,\dfrac{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-1}}{|\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})|}\leq N\right\}\setminus\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\right),

where the resonant relation
\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})
is defined in (
1.47
), and the sets of indices
\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, etc. are defined in (
1.41
), (
1.44
), (
1.45
) and (
1.46
).
Notice that for
l=1
, the set (
2.45
) is a subset of the set
\mathcal{J}_{n,N}
in
[
4
, Definition 3.5]
.
For
l>1
, this set is a generalization of
[
4
, Definition 3.5]
which allows more general resonant relations
\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})
and polynomial growth in the coefficients (instead of
\ell^{\infty}
).

Our first result is a characterization of the set of indices
\mathfrak{J}_{n,N}
in (
2.44
).
Let

(2.46)

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\bigcap_{l=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l}.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
. There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(n)
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
,

(2.47)

\mathfrak{J}_{n,N}=\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{l=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}\right)=\mathcal{R}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{C}_{n,N}

where
\mathfrak{C}_{n,N}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}
and
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\emptyset
if
n
is odd. Moreover, if
n
is even and

\bm{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
, then the corresponding monomial is integrable (cf.
Definition

2.7
), i.e.
u^{\bm{k}}=I^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}
for some
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{\frac{n}{2}}
.

Theorem

2.12
directly follows from
Theorem

2.13
and
2.14
below.

Firstly, we study the set
\mathcal{R}_{n}
. By (
1.41
), (
1.44
) and (
1.47
),

\bm{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}\qquad\iff\qquad\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})=0\quad\mbox{for all}\ l=0,1,\ldots,n-1.

Thanks to the following theorem, which is proven in
Section

A.2
, we can conclude that if
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
, then the corresponding monomial is integrable (cf.
Definition

2.7
). See also
[
17
, Section 4.1]
and
[
18
, Proposition 3.4]
for similar results in the context of the Degasperis–Procesi equation.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the system of equations

\begin{cases}k_{1}+k_{2}+\ldots+k_{n}&=0\\
k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+\ldots+k_{n}^{3}&=0\\
&\vdots\\
k_{1}^{2n-1}+k_{2}^{2n-1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2n-1}&=0,\end{cases}

with the additional assumption that
k_{j}\neq 0
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
. Then, we have the following:

•

if
n
is odd, there is no solution;

•

if
n=2m
is even, all solutions
(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{2m})
are paired, meaning that there exists a permutation
\sigma\in\mbox{Sym}(2m)
such that
k_{\sigma(2j-1)}+k_{\sigma(2j)}=0
for
j=1,\ldots,m
.

Next we define the set
\mathfrak{C}_{n,N}
in (
2.47
) as

(2.48)

\mathfrak{C}_{n,N}=\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\right)\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}.

2.14
below, proved in
Section

A.3
, guarantees that
\mathfrak{J}_{n,N}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}=\mathfrak{C}_{n,N}
, thereby proving (
2.47
).

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l}
.
There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(n)
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
, if

\left\lvert\frac{k_{1}^{2l-1}}{k_{1}^{2l+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2l+1}}\right\rvert\geq N,

then either there exists
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n-2}
such that

(2.49)

u^{\bm{k}}=|u_{a}|^{2}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\quad\text{where}\quad a\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}}

or

(2.50)

|k_{3}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}}.

2.14
is a generalization of Lemma 3.7 and Corollary 3.8 in
[
4
]
to the various dispersive relations in the KdV hierarchy.

The generators of the map
\Phi_{n}
.

The Hamiltonian functions we use to generate symplectic transformations
\Phi_{n}
in (
2.43
) are of the form

(2.51)

G_{n}^{(l)}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{\widetilde{c}_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}u^{\bm{k}},

for
n\geq 3
and
l=1,\ldots,n-1.

In (
2.51
) the coefficients
\widetilde{c}_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}
are the ones appearing in

\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(l)}=\left(F^{(l)}\circ\Phi_{3}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n-1}\circ\Phi_{n}^{(1)}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n}^{(l-1)}\right)_{n},

which is the homogeneous term of degree
n
in
F^{(l)}
after composing with the previous transformations (cf. (
2.43
) and (
2.80
) below).
By
Lemma

2.8
-(ii) and (
2.41
), it is clear that
G_{n}^{(l)}
is constructed to erase some monomials from
\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(l)}
.
In fact, we will prove that this transformation actually removes some monomials from
\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(j)}
for every
1\leq j\leq r-1
.
This key observation allows us to put the first
r-1
first integrals of the KdV hierarchy in normal form simultaneously (cf.
Theorem

2.18
).

The first result we need is the well-posedness of the Hamiltonian flow associated with
G_{n}^{(l)}
(
2.51
),
n\geq 3
and
l\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
,
at least up to time 1 in a neighborhood of the origin of
\dot{H}^{s}
.
This result is a generalization of
[
4
, Lemma 3.6]
with some important differences:

(i)

the coefficients
g_{\bm{k}}
in (
2.52
) do not need to be bounded (in fact they can grow polynomially in
\bm{k}
, thanks to the functional space
Y_{n}^{l}
cf. (
2.10
));

(ii)

\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})
in (
2.52
) can be any of the resonant relations in the KdV hierarchy; and

(iii)

we obtain precise bounds on the Lipschitz constant of the Hamiltonian flow associated with
G
.

The latter point is crucial to
4.8
, which is at the heart of the main result in this manuscript.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
N,s\geq 1
,
n\geq 3
and
l\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
.
If

(2.52)

G=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{g_{\bm{k}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}u^{\bm{k}}

with
\bm{g}\in Y_{n}^{l}
, then its vector field
X_{G}=\partial_{x}\nabla G
maps
\dot{H}^{s}
into itself and

\left\lVert X_{G}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{n,s}N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}.

As a consequence, there exists
\epsilon_{0}\gtrsim_{n,s}[N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}]^{-\frac{1}{n-2}}
such that for any
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
and any
\xi\in[0,2]
,
G
generates a Hamiltonian flow
\Phi_{G}^{\xi}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow\dot{H}^{s}
which is close to the identity in the sense that

(2.53)

\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{n,s}N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}.

Finally, for
\xi\in[0,2]
,
\Phi_{G}^{\xi}
is Lipschitz-continuous and

(2.54)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi=1}(u_{1})-\Phi_{G}^{\xi=1}(u_{2})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\exp\left(C(n,s)N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}(\left\lVert u_{1}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})^{n-2}\right).

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.4
,
G
is a smooth function in
\dot{H}^{s}
,
s\geq 1
. Indeed, for
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
, we have

\left\lvert\frac{g_{\bm{k}}}{\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}\right\rvert\leq N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}
.

Vector Field:
Next we compute the vector field generated by
G
. Setting
\bm{k}=(\bm{k}^{\prime},k_{n})
with
\bm{k}^{\prime}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})
,

\begin{split}X_{G}(u)_{\mathtt{r}}=&\ \{u_{\mathtt{r}},G\}=\frac{\mathtt{r}}{2\pi}\sum_{(\bm{k}^{\prime},-{\mathtt{r}})\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\left[\frac{g(-\mathtt{r},k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}{\Omega_{l}(-\mathtt{r},k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}+\ldots+\frac{g(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},-\mathtt{r})}{\Omega_{l}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},-\mathtt{r})}\right]u^{\bm{k^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

Therefore

\begin{split}\left\lVert X_{G}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\leq&\ \frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{\mathtt{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|\mathtt{r}|^{2s+2}\bigg\lvert\sum_{(\bm{k}^{\prime},-\mathtt{r})\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\left[\frac{g(-\mathtt{r},k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}{\Omega_{l}(-\mathtt{r},k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}+\ldots+\frac{g(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},-\mathtt{r})}{\Omega_{l}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},-\mathtt{r})}\right]u^{\bm{k^{\prime}}}\bigg\rvert^{2}\\
&\ \hskip-18.49411pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def: functional space}}}{{\leq}}\frac{n^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}^{2}\sum_{\mathtt{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|\mathtt{r}|^{2s}\left(\sum_{(\bm{k}^{\prime},-\mathtt{r})\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\left\lvert\frac{\max(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{n-1}|,|\mathtt{r}|)^{2l-1}}{\Omega_{l}((\bm{k}^{\prime},-\mathtt{r}))}\right\rvert\left\lvert u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\right\rvert\right)^{2}\\
&\ \hskip-19.91684pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def: set JnlN}}}{{\leq}}\frac{n^{2}N^{2}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{\mathtt{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|\mathtt{r}|^{2s}\left\lvert\sum_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n-1}=\mathtt{r}}|u_{k_{1}}|\ldots|u_{k_{n-1}}|\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\ \hskip-19.91684pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: jensen inequality}}}{{\leq}}\frac{n^{2+2s}N^{2}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{\mathtt{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert\sum_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n-1}=\mathtt{r}}|k_{1}|^{s}|u_{k_{1}}|\ldots|u_{k_{n-1}}|\right\rvert^{2}.\end{split}

By the Young convolution inequality (
1.50
) and the fact that
s>\frac{1}{2}
,

(2.55)

\left\lVert X_{G}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\lesssim n^{2s+2}N^{2}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}^{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{0,1}}^{2(n-2)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: embedding hs in fl01}}}{{\lesssim}}c_{0}^{2(n-2)}N^{2}n^{2s+2}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}^{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2n-2}.

A similar argument yields

(2.56)

\left\lVert\mathrm{d}X_{G}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{s})}^{2}\lesssim c_{0}^{2(n-2)}n^{2s+4}N^{2}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}^{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2n-4}.

By the Cauchy-Lipschitz Theorem the flow
\Phi_{G}^{\xi}
is locally well defined in
\dot{H}^{s}
.

Close to the Identity:
To prove (
2.53
) we use a bootstrap argument.
Let

I=\left\{\xi\in[0,2]\,|\,\sup_{\xi^{\prime}\in[0,\xi]}\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right\}.

This set is clearly closed and
0\in I
. It suffices to show that it is open: if
\xi\in I
, then

(2.57)

\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\int_{0}^{\xi}\left\lVert X_{G}(\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u))\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: est_vector field}}}{{\lesssim_{n,s}}}N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\xi\sup_{\xi^{\prime}\in[0,\xi]}\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}\lesssim_{n,s}N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}.

If we take

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{n,s}\left(N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{n-2}}=\epsilon_{0},

then we can arrange
\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}<\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
.
Therefore,
\sup_{\xi^{\prime}\in[0,\xi]}\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}<2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
, which implies that
\xi+\delta\in I
for
\delta>0
sufficiently small – thus
I
is open. Therefore
I=[0,2]
and (
2.57
) is valid
\forall\xi\in[0,2]
, yielding (
2.53
).

Lipschitz Property:

For
\xi\in[0,2]
,

\begin{split}\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u_{1})-\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u_{2})=u_{1}-u_{2}+\int_{0}^{\xi}[X_{G}(\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u_{1}))-X_{G}(\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u_{2}))]\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\end{split}

and so

\begin{split}&\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u_{1})-\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u_{2})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\!\leq\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\!+\!\int_{0}^{\xi}\!\!\sup_{\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2(\left\lVert u_{1}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})}\!\!\left\lVert\mathrm{d}X_{G}(w)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{s})}\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u_{1})-\Phi_{G}^{\xi^{\prime}}(u_{2})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\end{split}

using the fact that
\left\lVert\Phi_{G}^{\xi}(u_{j})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
for
\xi\in[0,2]
and
j\in\{1,2\}.

The Gronwall inequality and (
2.56
) yield (
2.54
).
∎

Remark 2.16
.

If the coefficients
g_{\bm{k}}
in (
2.52
) satisfy the reality condition (
2.13
) then, by (
1.47
),
\dfrac{g_{\bm{k}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}
satisfies the reality condition and hence
G
is real valued.
It is clear by the definition (
1.23
) that if two formal polynomials
F
and
G
satisfy the reality condition, then their Poisson bracket satisfies it.
Since in this manuscript we will only use formal polynomials from the KdV hierarchy (see
Lemma

2.1
) and we will transform them using symplectic transformations generated by the flow of some real valued Hamiltonian
G
(
2.52
), then by (
2.41
) we immediately see that all the formal polynomials we consider satisfy the reality condition (
2.13
).

Our next result allows us to control the Poisson bracket between a homogeneous
m
-formal polynomial and a homogeneous
l
-formal polynomial supported on the set of indices (
2.45
), such as the Hamiltonian
G_{n}^{(l)}
in (
2.51
) (see
Theorem

2.18
).
Thanks to the choice of the set
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
, we don’t lose any derivatives, as anticipated by
2.9
.
The proof is a generalization of
[
4
, Lemma 3.9]
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
N\geq 2
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\geq m
and
n,r\geq 3
.
Let
l\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
.
Consider
u\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
and

P(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}},\qquad G(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{g_{\bm{k}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}u^{\bm{k}},

with
\bm{g}\in Y_{n}^{l}
and
\bm{c}\in Y_{r}^{m}
.
Then

\{P,G\}(u)=\sum_{\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{n+r-2}}d_{\bar{\bm{k}}}u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}

where
\bm{d}\in Y_{n+r-2}^{m}
and

(2.58)

\left\lVert\bm{d}\right\rVert_{Y_{n+r-2}^{m}}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}Nn^{2m-1}r\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\left\lVert\bm{c}\right\rVert_{Y_{r}^{m}}.

Proof.

We have that

\begin{split}\{P,G\}(u)=&\ \sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{ij}{2\pi}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{r}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}\\
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}\\
k_{\alpha}=j=-k^{\prime}_{\beta}\end{subarray}}\frac{c_{\bm{k}}g_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k}^{\prime})}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{u_{k_{\alpha}}u_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}}=-\frac{i}{2\pi}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{r}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}\\
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}\\
k_{\alpha}=-k^{\prime}_{\beta}\end{subarray}}k_{\beta}^{\prime}\frac{c_{\bm{k}}g_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k}^{\prime})}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{u_{k_{\alpha}}u_{k_{\beta}^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

As in (
2.30
), we can reparametrize the latter sum using a variable
\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{n+r-2}

\begin{split}\{P,G\}(u)=&\ -\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{n+r-2}\\
(\bar{k}_{r},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r+n-2},\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\bar{k}_{j})\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{r}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}c(\bar{k}_{1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{\alpha-1},\sum_{j=r}^{r+n-2}\bar{k}_{j},\bar{k}_{\alpha},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r-1})\\
&\cdot\big(\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\bar{k}_{j}\big)\,\frac{g(\bar{k}_{r},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r+\beta-2},\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\bar{k}_{j},\bar{k}_{r+\beta-1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r+n-2})}{\Omega_{l}(\bar{k}_{r},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r+\beta-2},\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\bar{k}_{j},\bar{k}_{r+\beta-1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r+n-2})}u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}=:\sum_{\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{M}_{n+r-2}}d_{\bar{\bm{k}}}u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}\end{split}

renaming each time the indices.
Finally

(2.59)

|d_{\bar{\bm{k}}}|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}Nn\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{r}|c(\bar{k}_{1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{\alpha-1},\bar{k}_{r}+\ldots+\bar{k}_{r+n-2},\bar{k}_{\alpha},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r-1})|

by the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
.
Note that

(2.60)

\begin{split}\frac{|c(\bar{k}_{1},\ldots,\bar{k}_{\alpha-1},\bar{k}_{r}+\ldots+\bar{k}_{r+n-2},\bar{k}_{\alpha},\ldots,\bar{k}_{r-1})|}{\max(|\bar{k}_{1}|,\ldots,|\bar{k}_{r-1}|,|\bar{k}_{r}+\ldots+\bar{k}_{r+n-2}|)^{2m-2}}&\frac{\max(|\bar{k}_{1}|,\ldots,|\bar{k}_{r-1}|,|\bar{k}_{r}+\ldots+\bar{k}_{r+n-2}|)^{2m-2}}{\max(|\bar{k}_{1}|,\ldots,|\bar{k}_{r+n-2}|)^{2m-2}}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: jensen inequality}}}{{\leq}}&\ (n-1)^{2m-2}\left\lVert\bm{c}\right\rVert_{Y_{r}^{m}}.\end{split}

Bounds (
2.59
)-(
2.60
) yield (
2.58
).
∎

Main normal form theorem for the KdV hierarchy.

Now we are ready for the most important result of this section.
We prove that
Theorem

2.10
holds actually for all
F^{(j)}
in the KdV hierarchy (
2.9
), for
1\leq j\leq r-1
.
In particular we provide a symplectic transformation
\Phi
, invertible in a neighborhood of the origin of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
s\geq 2r
, close to the identity (in the sense of (
2.61
)), and we give a precise estimate of the coefficients of the transformed Hamiltonian.
The proof is a generalization of
[
4
, Proposition 3.10, Theorem 2]
.
The main difference is that we have to prove that the KdV hierarchy is transformed simultaneously, since we use it to construct each transformation (cf.
2.24
).
Since
F^{(j)}
has not bounded coefficients, we have to work in our more general functional setting (cf. (
2.10
)).

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 2r
. There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(r)
and a constant
c(r,s)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and all
\epsilon>0
such that
\epsilon\leq c(r,s)N^{-1}
the following holds.

There exists an invertible symplectic map
\Phi:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon)
with inverse
\Phi^{-1}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,\epsilon)

which are close to the identity in the sense that

(2.61)

\left\lVert\Phi^{\pm 1}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{r,s}N\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},

and Lipschitz-continuous:

(2.62)

\left\lVert\Phi^{\pm 1}(u)-\Phi^{\pm 1}(\underline{u})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert u-\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

Moreover on
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
we have, for
j=1,\ldots,r-1
,

(2.63)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{F}_{2\alpha}^{(j)}+R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}+R^{(j),(I\geq N^{1/2r})}+R_{\geq r+1}^{(j)},

where

(
2.63
a)

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{2}^{(j)}(u)

\displaystyle=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2j}|u_{k}|^{2},

(
2.63
b)

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{2\alpha}^{(j)}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{\alpha}}b_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}I^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{where $I=(I_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}=(|u_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}$ and $\frac{|b_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}|}{\max(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{\alpha}|)^{2j-2}}\lesssim_{\alpha}N^{2\alpha-3}$},

(
2.63
c)
r

\displaystyle R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}\\
3\leq\#\bm{k}\leq r\\
|k_{3}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{with $\frac{|c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}|}{\max(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{\#\bm{k}}|)^{2j-2}}\lesssim_{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-3}$},

(
2.63
d)
r

\displaystyle R^{(j),(I\geq N^{1/2r})}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=\lceil N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\rceil}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}\\
1\leq\#\bm{k}\leq r-2\end{subarray}}c_{m,\bm{k}}^{(j)}I_{m}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{with $\frac{|c_{m,\bm{k}}^{(j)}|}{\max(|m|,|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{\#\bm{k}}|)^{2j-2}}\lesssim_{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-1}$},

(
2.63
e)
r

\displaystyle R_{\geq r+1}^{(j)}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}\\
\#\bm{k}\geq r+1\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{where $\frac{|c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}|}{\max(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{\#\bm{k}}|)^{2j-2}}\leq\rho^{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-3}$ for some $\rho\lesssim_{r}1$},

and all the Hamiltonians have real coefficients and satisfy (
2.13
).

Remark 2.19
.

In
Section

2.4
, we actually prove the following stronger statement about the remainders
(c)r
–
(d)r
:

(
2.63
f)
r

\displaystyle R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}\\
5\leq\#\bm{k}\leq r\\
|k_{3}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{with $\frac{|c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}|}{\max(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{\#\bm{k}}|)^{2j-2}}\lesssim_{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-3}$},

(
2.63
g)
r

\displaystyle R^{(j),(I\geq N^{1/2r})}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=\lfloor N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\rfloor+1}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}\\
3\leq\#\bm{k}\leq r-2\end{subarray}}c_{m,\bm{k}}^{(j)}I_{m}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{with $\frac{|c_{m,\bm{k}}^{(j)}|}{\max(|m|,|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{\#\bm{k}}|)^{2j-2}}\lesssim_{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-1}$},

namely the fact that there are no terms of degree 3 and 4 in these remainders.

In order to prove this, we enlarge
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
in (
2.45
) as follows:

(2.64)

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{n,l,N}=\begin{cases}\Big\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l}\,\big|\,\dfrac{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-1}}{|\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})|}\leq N\Big\},&\text{if $n=3,4,$}\\
\vskip-2.84544pt\\
\Big\{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l}\,\big|\,\dfrac{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2l-1}}{|\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})|}\leq N\Big\}\setminus\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\right),&\text{if $n\geq 5$.}\end{cases}

With this definition, all results in
Section

2.2
continue to hold with
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
instead of
\mathcal{J}
. The only proof that must be modified is that of
2.24
below, see
2.26
for additional details.

Then
Lemma

2.30
-(ii) and
2.24
show that the transformations
\Phi_{3}=\Phi_{G_{3}}
and
\Phi_{4}=\Phi_{G_{4}}
, with
G_{3}
and
G_{4}
defined in (
2.85
) and (
2.86
), remove
all
terms of degree
3
and
4
from the KdV hierarchy. In particular, no such terms are present in the remainders
(c)r
–
(d)r
.

2.3
Normal form for the KdV hierarchy

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

2.18
. We begin with some preliminary results.

Convergence of the Taylor series (
2.41
):

We now state the following proposition which justifies the convergence of the Taylor series (
2.41
) under certain conditions on
\mathcal{H}
and
G
.
The proof, which is an adaptation of
[
4
, Proposition 3.10]
can be found in
Section

A.4
.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\geq j
,
N\geq 1
and let
F^{(j)}
be a
j
-formal polynomial

(2.65)

F^{(j)}=F_{2}^{(j)}+\sum_{\beta\geq 3}F_{\beta}^{(j)}=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2j}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{\beta\geq 3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\beta}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}},\qquad\left\lVert(c_{\bm{k}})_{\#\bm{k}=\beta}\right\rVert_{Y^{j}_{\beta}}\leq\rho^{\beta}N^{\beta-3},

for some
\rho>0
.
Consider

G_{n}^{(l)}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{g_{\bm{k}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}u^{\bm{k}}

defined in (
2.51
) with
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 3
, and assume that the following conditions hold:

(2.66)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{n}N^{n-3}

(2.67)

\displaystyle\{F_{2}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}.

Then there exists a constant
c=c(n,s,\rho,j)>0
such that for all
0<\epsilon\leq c\,N^{-1}
the time-1 flow of
G_{n}^{(l)}
is well-defined on
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
and invertible, in the sense that

\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon),\qquad\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{-1}=\Phi_{-G_{n}^{(l)}}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,\epsilon),\qquad\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{-1}=\mathrm{Id}_{B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)},

and they both are close to the identity, i.e.

(2.68)

\left\lVert\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\pm 1}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{n,s}N\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}.

Moreover on
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
we have that
F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}
is again a
j
-formal polynomial,

(2.69)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}=F_{2}^{(j)}+\sum_{\beta\geq 3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\beta}}q_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}.

Finally there exists
\widetilde{\rho}=\widetilde{\rho}(n,j,\rho)>0
such that

(2.70)

\left\lVert(q_{\bm{k}})_{\#\bm{k}=\beta}\right\rVert_{Y_{\beta}^{j}}\leq\widetilde{\rho}^{\beta}N^{\beta-3}

and

(2.71)

q_{\bm{k}}=c_{\bm{k}}\quad\mbox{if}\quad\#\bm{k}<n,\qquad q_{\bm{k}}=c_{\bm{k}}\delta(\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N})\quad\text{for}\quad\#\bm{k}=n.

Proving that (
2.66
) and especially (
2.67
) are satisfied for our choice of auxiliary Hamiltonians
G_{n}^{(l)}
is one of the key results of this section.

Remark 2.21
.

As explained in
[
4
, Remark 3.11]
, the exponent
\#\bm{k}-3
is quite natural because of the loss of one derivative in the Poisson bracket. Indeed, by
Lemma

2.17
we have

\left\{\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}}c_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}},\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{\widetilde{c}_{\bm{k}}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}\right\}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{r+n-2}}d_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}

with

\left\lVert\bm{d}\right\rVert_{Y_{r+n-2}^{j}}\lesssim Nn^{2j-1}r\left\lVert\bm{c}\right\rVert_{Y_{\#\bm{k}=r}^{j}}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{c}}\right\rVert_{Y_{\#\bm{k}=n}^{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{formula Fj prop},\eqref{eq: stima induttiva coeff g}}}{{\lesssim_{n,j,r}}}N^{1+n-3+r-3}\lesssim_{n,j,r}N^{(r+n-2)-3}.

Remark 2.22
.

Notice that whenever we use
Lemma

2.15
in the proof of
2.20
, we have to impose
\epsilon\lesssim_{n,s}[N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}]^{-\frac{1}{n-2}}
.
In principle one could fear that, since during the normal form procedure we compute a lot of Poisson brackets, then
\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}
may become huge in
N
.
However, since we are able to prove the stability property (
2.66
), then at each step we will always have to impose
\epsilon\lesssim_{n,s}N^{-1}
, a finite number of times. Therefore it is sufficient to choose the smallest constant among a finite number, see the proof of
Theorem

2.18
below.

Simultaneous transformation for the hierarchy

The next result concerns the Poisson bracket between certain classes of formal polynomials like
(b)
–
(d)r
. This is useful because we are going to use the fact that the KdV hierarchy commutes (
2.7
), even after some transformations (
2.42
), but inductively the hierarchy has the form (
2.63
).

Lemma 2.23
.

Consider
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 5
,
n_{3},n_{4}\geq 3
,
n_{5}\geq 1
,
M\geq 1
and let

G_{1}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{1}}\\
|k_{3}|\geq M\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(1)}u^{\bm{k}},\quad G_{2}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{2}}\\
|k_{3}|\geq M\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(2)}u^{\bm{k}},\quad G_{3}(u)=\sum_{\mathtt{r}=M}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{3}}}c_{(\mathtt{r},-\mathtt{r},\bm{k})}^{(3)}I_{\mathtt{r}}u^{\bm{k}},

G_{4}(u)=\sum_{\mathtt{r}=M}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{4}}}c_{(\mathtt{r},-\mathtt{r},\bm{k})}^{(4)}I_{\mathtt{r}}u^{\bm{k}},\quad G_{5}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n_{5}}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(5)}I^{\bm{k}},

with
\bm{c}^{(i)}\in Y_{n_{i}}^{j_{i}}
for
i=1,2
,
\bm{c}^{(i)}\in Y_{n_{i}+2}^{j_{i}}
for
i=3,4
and

\sup_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n_{5}}}\frac{|c_{\bm{k}}^{(5)}|}{\left\lVert\bm{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2j_{5}-2}}<+\infty,

where
j_{1},\ldots,j_{5}\geq 1.

Then

\{G_{1},G_{2}\}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{1}+n_{2}-2}\\
|k_{3}|\geq M\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(1,2)}u^{\bm{k}},\quad\{G_{1},G_{3}\}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{1}+n_{3}}\\
|k_{3}|\geq M\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(1,3)}u^{\bm{k}},\quad\{G_{1},G_{5}\}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{1}+2n_{5}-2}\\
|k_{3}|\geq M\end{subarray}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(1,5)}u^{\bm{k}},

\{G_{3},G_{4}\}=\sum_{\mathtt{r}=M}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{3}+n_{4}}}c_{(\mathtt{r},-\mathtt{r},\bm{k})}^{(3,4)}I_{\mathtt{r}}u^{\bm{k}},\quad\{G_{3},G_{5}\}=\sum_{\mathtt{r}=M}^{+\infty}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{3}+2n_{5}}}c_{(\mathtt{r},-\mathtt{r},\bm{k})}^{(3,5)}I_{\mathtt{r}}u^{\bm{k}},

Proof.

Poisson brackets between various
G_{i}
are formal polynomials in view of

Lemma

2.8
.

Let us prove the formula for
\{G_{1},G_{2}\}
. Note that

\{G_{1},G_{2}\}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\bm{k}}\in\mathcal{D}_{n_{1}+n_{2}-2}\end{subarray}}c_{\bar{\bm{k}}}^{(1,2)}u^{\bar{\bm{k}}}

where
c_{\bar{\bm{k}}}^{(1,2)}
explicitly depends on
\bm{c}^{(1)}
and
\bm{c}^{(2)}
, cf. (
2.30
).
In particular, if
|\bar{k}_{3}|<M
, then
c_{\bar{\bm{k}}}^{(1,2)}=0
by (
2.30
) and the fact that
\bm{c}^{(j)}
,
j=1,2
, are supported on
|k_{3}|\geq M
. The same argument works for the Poisson bracket
\{G_{1},G_{3}\}
.

For
\{G_{3},G_{4}\}
it is sufficient to notice that, by the Leibniz rule and
Lemma

2.8
-(iii),

\{I_{r_{1}}u^{\bm{k}_{1}},I_{r_{2}}u^{\bm{k}_{2}}\}=I_{r_{1}}I_{r_{2}}\{u^{\bm{k}_{1}},u^{\bm{k}_{2}}\}+I_{r_{1}}u^{\bm{k}_{2}}\{u^{\bm{k}_{1}},I_{r_{2}}\}+u^{\bm{k}_{1}}I_{r_{2}}\{I_{r_{1}},u^{\bm{k}_{2}}\},

which ensures that each monomial has at least one action with index
\geq M.

In order to justify the formula for
\{G_{3},G_{5}\}
, it suffices to note that

\{I^{\bm{k}},I_{r}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}=I_{r}\{I^{\bm{k}},u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}.

Finally, to justify the formula for
\{G_{1},G_{5}\}
, note that, by the Leibniz rule,

\{u^{\bm{k}},I^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\#\bm{k}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\#\bm{k}^{\prime}}\{u_{k_{\alpha}},I_{k^{\prime}_{\beta}}\}\,\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{u_{k_{\alpha}}}\,\frac{I^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{I_{k^{\prime}_{\beta}}}.

Moreover,

\{u_{k_{\alpha}},I_{k^{\prime}_{\beta}}\}=\frac{ik_{\alpha}}{2\pi}\frac{{\partial}|u_{k^{\prime}_{\beta}}|^{2}}{{\partial}\overline{u_{k_{\alpha}}}}=\frac{i{k_{\alpha}}}{2\pi}u_{k_{\alpha}}(\delta(k^{\prime}_{\beta}=k_{\alpha})+\delta(k^{\prime}_{\beta}=-k_{\alpha})).

As a result, the number of indices which are greater or equal than
M
(in absolute value) does not decrease.
∎

Our next result will be the key tool in order to inductively prove that the symplectic transformation
\Phi
simultaneously puts the KdV hierarchy in normal form up to certain remainders. This result is ultimately based on
[
26
, Theorem G.2]
, although that theorem cannot be applied directly since our transformation does not satisfy its hypotheses. Indeed, our mapping
\Phi
does not put the Hamiltonian in full Birkhoff normal form due to the truncation (
2.45
). See also
[
17
, Lemma 4.4]
and
[
18
, Proposition 3.6]
for similar results in the context of the Degasperis-Procesi equation.

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
r\geq n\geq 3
,
s\geq 2r
and
j=1,\ldots,r-1
.
Let

(2.72)

F^{(j)}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{F}_{2\alpha}^{(j)}+R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})}+R^{(j),(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})}+R_{\geq n}^{(j)},

where each summand on the right-hand side is of the form
(a)
–
(b)
and (
2.63
c)
n-1
–(
2.63
e)
n-1
.
Assume that

(2.73)

\{F^{(j)},F^{(l)}\}=0\qquad\mbox{for all}\quad j,l=1,\ldots,r-1.

Then, recalling that
F_{n}^{(j)}(u)=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}
, we have that

(2.74)

\{F_{2}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}=-\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}\qquad\mbox{for any}\ j=1,\ldots,r-1,\ \ l=1,\ldots,n-1,

with
G_{n}^{(l)}
defined in (
2.51
).

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.8
-(ii) we have

(2.75)

\{F_{2}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}=-\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}}{\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})u^{\bm{k}}=-\sum_{[\bm{k}]\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}/\mbox{Sym}(n)}\frac{\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})}{\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}\sum_{\bm{j}\sim\bm{k}}c_{\bm{j}}^{(l)}u^{\bm{j}}.

Since
s\geq j+l+\frac{1}{2},
we can apply
2.5
to (
2.73
), and isolating the homogeneous term of degree
n
we obtain

(2.76)

\{F_{2}^{(j)},F_{n}^{(l)}\}+\{F_{3}^{(j)},F_{n-1}^{(l)}\}+\ldots+\{F_{n-1}^{(j)},F_{3}^{(l)}\}+\{F_{n}^{(j)},F_{2}^{(l)}\}=0.

Notice that

\{F_{2}^{(j)},F_{n}^{(l)}\}+\{F_{n}^{(j)},F_{2}^{(l)}\}=i\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\left(\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}-\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}\right)u^{\bm{k}}.

By
Lemma

2.8
-(iii),
Lemma

2.23
and (
2.72
), (
2.76
) becomes

\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\right)}i\left(\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}-\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}\right)u^{\bm{k}}+\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}}e_{\bm{k}}^{(j,l)}u^{\bm{k}}=0.

Using
2.5
, we obtain the formula

(2.77)

\sum_{\bm{k}\sim\bm{j}}\left[\Omega_{j}(\bm{k})c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}-\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}\right]=0\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\cup\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}\right),

and in particular for all
\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}.

Therefore

\sum_{\bm{k}\sim\bm{j}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}=\frac{\Omega_{j}(\bm{j})}{\Omega_{l}(\bm{j})}\sum_{\bm{k}\sim\bm{j}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}\qquad\qquad\forall\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N},

which yields (
2.74
) in view of (
2.75
).
∎

Remark 2.25
.

We have shown that if (
2.63
) holds, then the Hamiltonian
G_{n}^{(l)}
in (
2.51
) simultaneously satisfies
n-1
homological equations, cf. (
2.74
). We note that this result requires a careful definition of
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
, (
2.45
), in order to avoid terms coming from interactions between the remainders, cf. (
2.77
).
This is one key technical difference with respect to the approach in
[
4
]
.

Remark 2.26
.

If instead of
\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}
we use
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{n,l,N}
in
2.19
, then
2.24
still holds.
Indeed (
2.76
) for
n=3
immediately gives (
2.77
) for all
\bm{j}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{3,l,N}
, since only
\{F_{2}^{(j)},F_{n}^{(l)}\}
and
\{F_{n}^{(j)},F_{2}^{(l)}\}
appear.
The same is true for
n=4
, since (
2.76
) becomes

\{F_{2}^{(j)},F_{4}^{(l)}\}+\{F_{3}^{(j)},F_{3}^{(l)}\}+\{F_{4}^{(j)},F_{2}^{(l)}\}=0,

and
Lemma

2.30
-(ii) below proves that there are no terms of degree 3 in the KdV hierarchy (at least for
j,l\leq r-1)
after the tranformation
\Phi_{3}
.

Proof of
Theorem

2.18
.

By
Lemma

2.1
, the theorem is true for
r=2
, with
\Phi=\mbox{Id}
.
Next fix
r\geq 3
. We fix
r-1
first integrals
F^{(j)}
in (
2.9
), for
j\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
.
The proof is by induction on
n
, the degree up to which we put these first integrals are in normal form (up to remainders). In particular, we will induct from
n=2
to
n=r
.
At the end of the
(n-1)
-th step, we will have carried out transformations
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{n-1}
in such a way that

(2.78)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi_{n-1}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{F}_{2\alpha}^{(j)}+R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})}+R^{(j),(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})}+R_{\geq n}^{(j)},\quad j=1,\ldots,r-1,

satisfying
(a)
–
(b)
and (
2.63
c)
n-1
–(
2.63
e)
n-1
.

As part of the
n
-th step we perform an additional transformation
\Phi_{n}
and we inductively check the following facts:

1.

For all
\epsilon\leq c(r,n,s)N^{-1}
, the symplectic transformation

\Phi_{n}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon),

and its inverse

\Phi_{n}^{-1}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,\epsilon),

(cf. (
2.40
)), are well-defined and both are close to the identity (cf. (
2.61
)).

2.

Setting

\Phi_{\leq n-1}=\Phi_{2}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n-1},

we note that for
j,j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\},

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{\leq n-1}\circ\Phi_{n},\qquad\text{and}\quad\{F^{(j_{1})}\circ\Phi_{\leq n-1}\circ\Phi_{n},F^{(j_{2})}\circ\Phi_{\leq n-1}\circ\Phi_{n}\}

are respectively a
j
and a
j_{1}+j_{2}+1
formal polynomial well-defined on
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
and

\{F^{(j_{1})}\circ\Phi_{\leq n-1}\circ\Phi_{n},F^{(j_{2})}\circ\Phi_{\leq n-1}\circ\Phi_{n}\}=0.

3.

The transformed Hamiltonian can be written as

(2.79)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi_{n}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{F}_{2\alpha}^{(j)}+R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}+R^{(j),(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}})}+R_{\geq n+1}^{(j)},\quad j=1,\ldots,r-1,

and it satisfies
(a)
–
(b)
and (
2.63
c)
n
–(
2.63
e)
n
.

We note that the remainders
R^{(j)}
in (
2.79
) are not equal to those in (
2.78
), but they satisfy (
2.63
c)
n
–(
2.63
e)
n
instead of (
2.63
c)
n-1
–(
2.63
e)
n-1
. This choice of notation, which will be kept throughout the proof, is made to avoid cumbersome indices.

Note that properties 1, 2, and 3 are true for
n=2
since
\Phi_{2}=\mbox{Id}
, which is the induction basis. Next we assume that properties 1, 2, and 3 hold for
n-1
and for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}.

The transformation
\Phi_{n}
is itself the composition of
n-1
symplectic transformations generated by

(2.80)

G_{n}^{(l)}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}}\frac{c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}}{i\Omega_{l}(\bm{k})}u^{\bm{k}}\quad\text{where}\quad\widetilde{F}_{n}^{(j)}=\left(F^{(l)}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n-1}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(1)}}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l-1)}}\right)_{n}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(l)}u^{\bm{k}}

defined in (
2.51
) for
l=1,\ldots,n-1
.
In order to prove points 1–3 above we proceed by induction on
l=1,\ldots,n-1
.
We inductively check the following facts:

(i)

For all
\epsilon\leq c(l,n,r,s)N^{-1}
, the symplectic transformation

\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon),

and its inverse

\Phi_{-G_{n}^{(l)}}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,\epsilon),

(cf. (
2.43
)), are well-defined and both are close to the identity (cf. (
2.61
)).

(ii)

Setting

\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}=\Phi_{2}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n-1}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(1)}}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l-1)}},

we note that for
j,j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\},

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}},\qquad\text{and}\quad\{F^{(j_{1})}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}},F^{(j_{2})}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}\}

are respectively a
j
and a
j_{1}+j_{2}+1
formal polynomial well-defined on
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
and

\{F^{(j_{1})}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}},F^{(j_{2})}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}\}=0.

(iii)

The transformed Hamiltonian can be written as

(2.81)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{F}_{2\alpha}^{(j)}+R^{(j),(\mu_{3}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})}+R^{(j),(I\geq N^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})}+R_{\geq n}^{(j)},\quad j=1,\ldots,r-1,

and it satisfies
(a)
–
(b)
and (
2.63
c)
n-1
–(
2.63
e)
n-1
.
Moreover the homogeneous part of degree
n
in (
2.81
) changes in the following way:

(F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}})_{n}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}\implies\left(F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}\right)_{n}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}\smallsetminus\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}\right)}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}.

It is clear that properties (i)–(iii) are satisfied before carrying out any transformation at order
n
, i.e. by taking
\Phi_{G_{n}^{(0)}}=\mbox{Id}
after defining
G_{n}^{(0)}=0
.
Indeed, (
2.78
) implies (
2.81
) for
l=0
by the induction hypothesis.

Let us prove points (i)–(iii) by induction on
l
.
They easily follow by
2.20
and
2.24
: let us check that the hypotheses are satisfied.
Notice that (
2.65
)-(
2.66
) follow by the inductive hypotheses, in particular by (
2.81
).
On the other hand, (
2.67
) follows by the definition of
G_{n}^{(l)}
and by
2.24
, whose hypotheses are again satisfied by the inductive hypotheses: in particular (
2.72
) follows by (
2.81
) and (
2.73
) follows by point (ii) at the previous inductive step.
Now we can say that

\bullet
Point (i) follows by
2.20
, in particular (
2.68
).

\bullet
Point (ii) follows by
2.20
.
We take
c(l,n,r,s)\leq\frac{c(l-1,n,r,s)}{2}
, so that the composition
\Phi_{\mathrm{old}}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}
is well-defined and so that we can apply
2.20
.
The fact that the transformed integrals commute follows by (
2.7
) and the fact that each transformation is symplectic.

\bullet
Point (iii) easily follows from
2.20
, in particular (
2.71
).

Finally we complete the induction on
n
proving points 1–3.

\bullet
Point 1: we take
c(n,r,s)=\min_{l=1,...,n-1}c(l,n,r,s)
so that the composition
\Phi_{G_{n}^{(1)}}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(n-1)}}
is well-defined.
A priori the codomain of
\Phi_{n}
is not
B_{s}(0,2\epsilon)
.
However, notice that by the triangle inequality

(2.82)

\left\lVert\Phi_{n}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left\lVert\Phi_{G_{n}^{(i)}}\left(\Phi_{G_{n}^{(i+1)}}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(n-1)}}(u)\right)-\Phi_{G_{n}^{(i+1)}}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(n-1)}}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{n,s}N\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}

which proves that
\Phi_{n}
is close to the identity in the sense of (
2.61
) and also that the codomain is correct, provided that one chooses
c(n,r,s)
sufficiently small.
The same is true for the inverse
\Phi_{n}^{-1}
.

\bullet
Point 2: the fact that the composition is well-defined follows again by choosing
c(n,r,s)\leq c(n-1,r,s)/2
, while the other facts follow from
2.20
and (
2.7
) as in point (ii).

\bullet
Point 3: follows by (
2.71
) and
Theorem

2.12
.

This concludes the induction in
n
. Finally, we fix
c(r,s)=\min_{n=2,\ldots,r}c(n,r,s)
and by an argument similar to (
2.82
), up to shrinking
c(r,s)
again we can conclude that
\Phi:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon\leq c(r,s)N^{-1}
.
The same is true for the inverse
\Phi^{-1}
.

Proof of (
2.62
).
By the contruction of
\Phi^{\pm 1}
as composition of the maps
\Phi_{\pm G_{n}^{(l)}}
, it follows that

\begin{split}\left\lVert\Phi^{\pm 1}(u)-\Phi^{\pm 1}(\underline{u})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: lipschitz continuity of phi}}}{{\leq}}\exp\left(C(r,s)\sum_{n=3}^{r}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}N(\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})^{n-2}\right)\left\lVert u-\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{hamiltoniana dopo tutte le trasformazioni}e)_{n},\eqref{eq: other lie transforms}}}{{\leq}}\exp\left(C(r,s)\sum_{n=3}^{r}N^{n-2}(\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})^{n-2}\right)\left\lVert u-\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert u-\underline{u}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\end{split}

using that
\epsilon N\leq c(r,s)
with
c(r,s)
is sufficiently small. ∎

Remark 2.27
.

We highlight that, in the second inductive procedure in the proof of
Theorem

2.18
(points (i)–(iii)), the order in which we apply the transformations
\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}
,
l=1,\ldots,n-1
, can be chosen arbitrarily.

Moreover, by slightly modifying
Theorem

2.13
, one can see that at the
n
-th step it is not necessary to choose
l\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, but it is enough to choose
l\in\{p,p+a,\ldots,p+(n-2)a\}
for some
p,a\in\mathbb{N}.

Remark 2.28
.

We highlight that, in
Theorem

2.18
, we simultaneously put
r-1
first integrals in normal form to order
r
(up to remainder terms). Our proof can be adapted to simultaneously put
m
first integrals in normal form to order
r
, provided
m\geq r-1
and
s\geq m+r
. We chose
m=r-1
in order to minimize the regularity
s
.

Remark 2.29
.

Let us highlight that each of the mappings composing
\Phi
can be defined on
B_{\sigma}(0,\epsilon)
for any
1\leq\sigma\leq s
, cf.
Lemma

2.15
and
2.24
. In particular, we can choose
\sigma
sufficiently large so that the composition
F^{(j)}\circ\Phi
and the Poisson brackets
\{F^{(j)}\circ\Phi,F^{(l)}\circ\Phi\}
are well-defined, e.g. (
2.78
). One can thus obtain the algebraic identity (
2.77
)
by defining
\Phi
on a sufficiently regular domain. This allows us to derive (
2.74
)
in the minimal regularity necessary to define
F^{(j)}
. In particular, this allows us to enunciate
Theorem

2.10
for
s\geq 1
, as explained in
2.11
.

2.4
The maps
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}

In this section we slightly modify the maps
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
following
2.19
.
In particular, we define
\Phi_{n}=\Phi_{G_{n}}
with
G_{n}
,
n=3,4
, defined in (
2.85
) and (
2.86
), as opposed to the previously defined
G_{n}^{(l)}
for
l\leq n-1
.
In particular, this will allow us to explicitly compute
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{4}
in (
2.35
) and to prove that there are no terms of degree 3 and 4 in the remainders of the transformed Hamiltonian (
2.33
).

First, we prove that third and fourth order resonances are trivial:

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{1}
be the set of
n
-resonant tuples defined in (
1.44
).
Then

(i)
\mathcal{R}_{3}^{1}=\emptyset
and

\mathcal{R}_{4}^{1}=\left\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})\in\mathcal{M}_{4}\mid(k_{1}+k_{2})(k_{1}+k_{3})(k_{1}+k_{4})=0\right\}.

Note that indices in
\mathcal{R}_{4}^{1}
correspond to integrable terms (cf.
Definition

2.7
).

(ii) For
N\geq 1
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{3,1,N}=\mathcal{M}_{3}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{4,1,N}=\mathcal{M}_{4}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{4}^{1}
(cf.
2.19
).

Proof.

(i) Note that for
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}
,

(2.83)

k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=0\quad\implies\quad\Omega_{1}(\bm{k})=k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}=3k_{1}k_{2}k_{3},

and for
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{4}
,

(2.84)

k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4}=0\quad\implies\Omega_{1}(\bm{k})=k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}=-3(k_{1}+k_{2})(k_{1}+k_{3})(k_{2}+k_{3}).

(ii) The fact that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{3,1,N}=\mathcal{M}_{3}
follows immediately from the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{3,1,N}
(cf. (
2.64
)) and (
2.83
).

To prove
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{4,1,N}=\mathcal{M}_{4}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{4}^{1}
, we distinguish two cases. Since the four indices cannot all have the same sign:

•

If three of them have the same sign, then the other one, say
k_{1}
, is maximal, i.e.
\max{(|k_{1}|,|k_{2}|,|k_{3}|,|k_{4}|)}=|k_{1}|
. Since
k_{1}=-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4}
, then for one index, say
k_{2}
, we have that
|k_{2}|\leq\frac{|k_{1}|}{3}
. Therefore
|k_{1}+k_{2}|\geq\frac{2}{3}|k_{1}|
and the thesis follows from (
2.84
).

•

If two indices have the same sign, by symmetry we can assume that
\max{(|k_{1}|,|k_{2}|,|k_{3}|,|k_{4}|)}=|k_{1}|
and that
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
have the same sign. Therefore
\frac{|k_{1}|}{|k_{1}+k_{2}|}\leq 1
and (
2.84
) concludes the proof.

∎

We define the transformations
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
in (
2.40
) as time-1 flows of Hamiltonians
G_{3}
and
G_{4}
which we construct next.
Using (
2.83
), we define

(2.85)

G_{3}=i\frac{\pi}{3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}}=i\frac{\pi}{9}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}

which exactly solves the homological equation:

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}=(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{3})_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: taylor formula for Hnew integral remainder}}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}_{3}+\{\mathcal{H}_{2},G_{3}\}=0

yielding

\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{3}=(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{3})_{2}+\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}+\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}+\ldots

where

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{3})_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: taylor formula for Hnew integral remainder}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\{\{\mathcal{H}_{2},G_{3}\},G_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{3},G_{3}\}=\frac{1}{2}\{\mathcal{H}_{3},G_{3}\}=-\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{4}\\
k_{2}+k_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{k_{2}k_{3}}.

Then one has to solve the homological equation

\{\mathcal{H}_{2},G_{4}\}+\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{4}

and find

(2.86)

G_{4}=-\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{4}\smallsetminus\mathcal{R}_{4}^{1}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{ik_{2}k_{3}\Omega_{1}(\bm{k})},\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=-\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{|u_{k}|^{4}}{k^{2}}.

These formulae are the result of straightforward computations which we briefly describe in the following lemma (which also proves (
2.35
)).

Lemma 2.31
.

Given the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi_{G_{3}}
, we have that

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=\frac{1}{2}\{\mathcal{H}_{3},G_{3}\}=-\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{4}\\
k_{2}+k_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{k_{2}k_{3}}

and choosing
G_{4}
as in (
2.86
) it follows

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=-\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{|u_{k}|^{4}}{k^{2}}.

Proof.

By the linearity of the Poisson bracket we obtain

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}&=\frac{1}{2}\left\{-\frac{\pi}{3}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}}u^{\bm{k}},i\frac{\pi}{9}\sum_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}}{k_{1}^{\prime}k_{2}^{\prime}k_{3}^{\prime}}\right\}=-\frac{i\pi^{2}}{54}\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}}\frac{1}{k_{1}^{\prime}k_{2}^{\prime}k_{3}^{\prime}}\{u^{\bm{k}},u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\},\end{split}

where

\begin{split}\{u^{\bm{k}},u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\left[\right.&k_{1}u_{k_{2}}u_{k_{3}}\left(\delta(k_{1}=-k_{1}^{\prime})u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}+\delta(k_{1}=-k_{2}^{\prime})u_{k_{1}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}+\delta(k_{1}=-k_{3}^{\prime})u_{k_{1}^{\prime}}u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}\right)\\
+&k_{2}u_{k_{1}}u_{k_{3}}\left(\delta(k_{2}=-k_{1}^{\prime})u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}+\delta(k_{2}=-k_{2}^{\prime})u_{k_{1}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}+\delta(k_{2}=-k_{3}^{\prime})u_{k_{1}^{\prime}}u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}\right)\\
+&k_{3}u_{k_{1}}u_{k_{2}}\left.\left(\delta(k_{3}=-k_{1}^{\prime})u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}+\delta(k_{3}=-k_{2}^{\prime})u_{k_{1}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}+\delta(k_{3}=-k_{3}^{\prime})u_{k_{1}^{\prime}}u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}\right)\right].\end{split}

By symmetry,

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=-\frac{i\pi^{2}}{54}\cdot\frac{i}{2\pi}\cdot 9\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=0\\
-k_{1}+k_{2}^{\prime}+k_{3}^{\prime}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{k_{1}u_{k_{2}}u_{k_{3}}u_{k_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k_{3}^{\prime}}}{-k_{1}k_{2}^{\prime}k_{3}^{\prime}}\end{split}

which yields the thesis upon renaming the indices.
Then

\begin{split}\{\mathcal{H}_{2},G_{4}\}+\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{4}=&\ -\frac{\pi}{12}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{4}^{1}\\
k_{2}+k_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{u^{\bm{k}}}{k_{2}k_{3}}\\
=&\ -\frac{\pi}{12}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{u_{-k}u_{k}u_{k}u_{-k}}{k^{2}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
j\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0,\pm k\}\end{subarray}}\frac{u_{j}u_{-j}u_{k}u_{-k}}{jk}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
j\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0,\pm k\}\end{subarray}}\frac{u_{j}u_{k}u_{-j}u_{-k}}{jk}\right)\end{split}

using
Lemma

2.30
.
Finally the last two sums vanish by symmetry.
∎

3
Dynamics

In this section we exploit the results provided in
Section

2.2
to study the dynamics of the KdV equation. We note that global well-posedness of the KdV equation in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

is well-known (for instance
[
15
, Chapter 3]
).
The following result allows us to approximate the dynamics over arbitrarily long (polynomial) timescales:

Theorem 3.1

(Approximate dynamics for long timescales)
.

Let
r\geq 3
.
Fix
s\geq 12r^{2}
, then there exists
\epsilon_{1}\lesssim_{r,s}1
such that for all
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{1}
the following holds. Let
u(0)\in\dot{H}^{s}
such that

\left\lVert u(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\epsilon

and let
u(t)
be the unique global solution to the KdV equation with initial datum
u(0)
, then for all
t\in[0,T]
with

(3.1)

T=\epsilon^{-\frac{r}{5}}

we have that

(3.2)

\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\epsilon,\qquad\left\lVert u_{j}(t)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}u_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq\epsilon^{\frac{8}{5}},

where

(3.3)

\theta_{j}(t)=j^{3}-\frac{1}{6j}|\Phi^{-1}(u(t))_{j}|^{2}+j\sum_{n=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n-1}}b_{(j,\bm{k})}|\Phi^{-1}(u(t)))^{\bm{k}}|^{2},\qquad\lvert b_{(j,\bm{k})}\rvert\lesssim_{r,s}\epsilon^{-\frac{2\#\bm{k}-1}{5}},

where
\Phi
was defined in
Theorem

2.10
.

The theorem is proved in
Section

3.3
.

Remark 3.2
.

The choice
s\geq 12r^{2}
is technical.
Choosing a large
N
in
Theorem

2.10
allows us to better control the remainders (
2.37
)–(
2.38
), but this gives rise to worse approximations of the dynamics on account of (
2.31
). We exploit the additional regularity (with respect to
Theorem

2.10
) in order to obtain better bounds on the remainder terms (
2.37
)–(
2.38
) without making
N
too large.

Remark 3.3
.

We do not try to optimize the relationship between
r
,
s
and
T
. For instance, by taking
N\sim\epsilon^{0-}
, then one may potentially reach timescales
T\approx\epsilon^{-r+}
at the cost of greatly increasing
s
(as a function of
r
).

3.1
Control of the remainders

In this subsection we control the remainders in (
2.33
).
The following is a result analogous to
[
4
, Proposition 3.3]
, which allows us to estimate the Poisson bracket between the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm squared and specific Hamiltonian functions which are like the remainders of the normal form (
2.37
)–(
2.39
).
The reason why we need such a result is that, in general, the vector field
X_{R}=\partial_{x}\nabla R
doesn’t map
\dot{H}^{s}
into itself.
However we can bound the growth of the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm along the flow of these remainders. A similar strategy was used in
[
41
]
in the context of the derivative NLS equation.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
s\geq 2
,
M>0
and
n\geq 3
.

(i) Let
R=\sum_{\beta\geq r+1}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\beta}}b_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}
, with
r\geq 1
, be a 1-formal polynomial (cf.
Definition

2.2
) with
|b_{\bm{k}}|\leq M^{\#\bm{k}}
for all
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
.
Then

\left\lvert\left\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R\right\}(u)\right\rvert\lesssim_{s,r}\left(M\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{r+1}\quad\text{for}\quad\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\left(2c_{0}M\right)^{-1}

where
c_{0}
is a universal constant.

(ii) Let
P_{n}
be a homogeneous 1-formal polynomial of the form

(3.4)

P_{n}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
\mu_{3}(\bm{k})\geq M\end{subarray}}b_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}},

where
\mu_{3}(\bm{k})
denotes the third largest number among
|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|
and
\bm{b}
are some bounded coefficients. Then

(3.5)

\left\lvert\left\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},P_{n}\right\}(u)\right\rvert\leq(2s+1)c_{0}^{n}n^{s+4}M^{-s+2}\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n}.

(iii) Let
P_{n+2}
be a homogeneous 1-formal polynomial of degree
n+2
of the form

P_{n+2}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
j\geq M\end{subarray}}b_{(j,-j,\bm{k})}I_{j}u^{\bm{k}},

where
\bm{b}
are some bounded coefficients. Then

\left\lvert\left\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},P_{n+2}\right\}(u)\right\rvert\lesssim_{s,n}M^{-2s}\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n+2}.

Proof.

(ii) Let
P_{n}
be the homogeneous polynomial in (
3.4
). By
Lemma

2.8
-(ii),

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},P_{n}\right\}(u)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
\mu_{3}(\bm{k})\geq M\end{subarray}}\left\lvert k_{1}^{2s+1}+k_{2}^{2s+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2s+1}\right\rvert\lvert u^{\bm{k}}\rvert

(3.6)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{3}\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|\geq|k_{3}|\geq M,|k_{4}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}\left(\left\lvert k_{1}^{2s+1}+k_{2}^{2s+1}\right\rvert+(n-2)|k_{3}|^{2s+1}\right)\lvert u^{\bm{k}}\rvert

where we reordered the first three indices and used the triangle inequality.
Notice that by (
1.50
), (
1.51
) and the fact that
n\geq 3
we have that

(3.7)

\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}|k_{1}|^{s}|k_{2}|^{s}|k_{3}|^{s-1}\lvert u^{\bm{k}}\rvert\leq\left\lVert|{\partial}_{x}|^{s}u\right\rVert_{\dot{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\lVert|{\partial}_{x}|^{s-1}u\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{0,1}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{0,1}}^{n-3}\leq c_{0}^{n-2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n}

using the same argument as in
Lemma

2.4
.
Since

|k_{3}|^{2s+1}\leq|k_{3}|^{s-1}|k_{2}|^{s}|k_{1}|^{2}\leq M^{-s+2}|k_{1}|^{s}|k_{2}|^{s}|k_{3}|^{s-1}

we have that the second summand in (
3.6
) is bounded by

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|\geq|k_{3}|\geq M,|k_{4}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}(n-2)|k_{3}|^{2s+1}\lvert u^{\bm{k}}\rvert\lesssim nc_{0}^{n-2}M^{-s+2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n}.\end{split}

In order to bound the first summand in (
3.6
), we distinguish two cases:

(a) If
k_{1}k_{2}>0
, then by the zero momentum condition
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
we obtain

|k_{1}|,|k_{2}|\leq n|k_{3}|\quad\text{and}\quad|k_{1}|\leq n|k_{2}|,

and so

(3.8)

\left\lvert k_{1}|k_{1}|^{2s}+k_{2}|k_{2}|^{2s}\right\rvert\leq 2n^{s+1}|k_{3}|^{s-1}|k_{1}|^{s}|k_{2}|^{2}\leq 2n^{s+1}M^{-s+2}|k_{1}|^{s}|k_{2}|^{s}|k_{3}|^{s-1}.

(b) If
k_{1}k_{2}<0
, since
\partial_{x}(x|x|^{2s})=(2s+1)|x|^{2s}
, by the mean value theorem we obtain

(3.9)

\begin{split}\left\lvert k_{1}|k_{1}|^{2s}+k_{2}|k_{2}|^{2s}\right\rvert&\leq(2s+1)\left\lvert|k_{1}|-|k_{2}|\right\rvert|k_{1}|^{2s}\leq(2s+1)|k_{1}|^{s}n^{s}|k_{2}|^{s}n|k_{3}|\\
&\leq(2s+1)n^{s+1}M^{-s+2}|k_{1}|^{s}|k_{2}|^{s}|k_{3}|^{s-1}.\end{split}

We are ready to bound first summand in (
3.6
)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq|k_{2}|\geq|k_{3}|\geq M,|k_{4}|,\ldots,|k_{n}|\end{subarray}}\left\lvert k_{1}^{2l+1}+k_{2}^{2l+1}\right\rvert\lvert u^{\bm{k}}\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:young_conseq}-\eqref{caseb}}}{{\leq}}(2s+1)n^{s+1}c_{0}^{n-2}M^{-s+2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n}

which concludes the proof of point (ii).

(iii) First, we note that
\sum_{j\geq M}I_{j}\leq M^{-2s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}
.
Moreover, since the actions commute with the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm, we have

\begin{split}\left\lvert\left\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},P_{n+2}\right\}(u)\right\rvert=&\ \sum_{j\geq M}I_{j}\bigg\lvert\bigg\{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}b_{(j,-j,\bm{k})}u^{\bm{k}}\bigg\}\bigg\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hom_pol_bound}}}{{\leq}}(2s+1)\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}n^{s+4}c_{0}^{n}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n}\,\sum_{j\geq M}I_{j}\\
&\leq(2s+1)M^{-2s}\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}n^{s+4}c_{0}^{n}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n+2}\end{split}

where we used (
3.5
) with
M=1
.

(i) Using (
3.5
) with
M=1
, together with the bound
|b_{\bm{k}}|\leq M^{\#\bm{k}}
, we find

\left\lvert\left\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R\right\}(u)\right\rvert\lesssim_{s}\sum_{n\geq r+1}n^{s+4}\left(c_{0}M\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{n}\lesssim_{s,r}\left(c_{0}M\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{r+1}

provided
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\left(2c_{0}M\right)^{-1}
.
∎

3.2
A priori bounds in the new variables

We are ready to prove some estimates on the dynamics in the new variables
v
.
We will exploit the special structure of the Hamiltonian (
2.63
) and
3.4
, where we study exactly how the remainders
(d)r
–
(e)r
have an impact on the
\dot{H}^{s}
-norm of the solution.

Theorem 3.5

(A priori bounds in the new variables)
.

Let
r\geq 3,

s\geq 2r
and
N\geq N_{0}(r)
(see
Theorem

2.10
).
Let
v(t)
be a solution to the Hamiltonian system associated with (
2.33
).
There exists positive constants
c(r,s),C(r,s)
such that, provided

(3.10)

\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\lVert v(\tau)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq c(r,s)N^{-1},

then

(3.11)

\begin{split}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}+t\,C(r,s)\left[N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}+\left(N\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,t],\dot{H}^{s})}\right)^{r+1}\right].\end{split}

Moreover, if for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
we define the real-valued functions

(3.12)

\theta_{j}(t)=\frac{j}{\pi}\partial_{J_{j}}\left[\mathcal{H}_{2}(J(t))+\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{4}(J(t))+\sum_{n=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}(J(t))\right],\quad\text{where $J_{j}(t)=|v_{j}(t)|^{2},$}

with
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
in
Theorem

2.10
, then

(3.13)

\begin{split}\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}&\leq C(r,s)\ t\left(1+t\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)\,\left[N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}+\left(N\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,t],\dot{H}^{s})}\right)^{r}\ \right].\end{split}

Proof.

Thanks to (
3.10
) at
t=0
, the fact that
\left\lVert\Phi(v)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
(cf.
Theorem

2.10
), and the local well-posedness of the KdV equation in
\dot{H}^{s}
, we have that the Hamiltonian system associated with (
2.33
) is locally well-posed and its solution is precisely
v(t)=\Phi^{-1}(u(t))
, cf.
[
2
,
16
]
.

Estimate (
3.11
).

Since
v
is the solution to the Hamiltonian system associated with (
2.33
),

(3.14)

\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}=\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi\}(v(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

By
Lemma

2.8
-(iii) and
Theorem

2.10
, we have that

\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi\}=\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}\}+\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}\}+\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R_{\geq r+1}\}.

Now we estimate each term separately.
By (
2.39
),
3.4
-(i) applied with
M=\rho N
, and (
3.10
),

\left\lvert\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R_{\geq r+1}\}(v(t))\right\rvert\lesssim_{r,s}\left(\rho N\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{r+1}\lesssim_{r,s}\left(N\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{r+1}.

By (
2.37
) and
3.4
-(ii) applied with
M=N^{\frac{1}{2r}}
, we have

\left\lvert\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}\}(v(t))\right\rvert\lesssim_{r,s}N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}\sum_{n=5}^{r}\left(N\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: hp dynam new var}}}{{\lesssim_{r,s}}}N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}.

Finally, by (
2.38
) and
3.4
-(iii) applied with
M=N^{\frac{1}{2r}}
we have

\left\lvert\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2},R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}\}(v(t))\right\rvert\lesssim_{r,s}N^{-\frac{s}{r}}\sum_{n=5}^{r}\left(N\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: hp dynam new var}}}{{\lesssim_{r,s}}}N^{-\frac{s}{r}}.

Using these estimates on (
3.14
), we obtain (
3.11
).

Estimate (
3.13
).
By the triangle inequality, we write

(3.15)

\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}+\left\lVert e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\ .

\bullet

First term in (
3.15
)
. Note that
v_{j}(t)
satisfies

\partial_{t}v_{j}(t)=i\theta_{j}(t)v_{j}(t)+(\partial_{x}\nabla R)_{j}(v(t))

where
\theta_{j}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
is defined in (
3.12
) and
R=R_{\geq r+1}+R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}+R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}
, following
Theorem

2.10
. Integrating in time, we find

(3.16)

\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq t\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left\lVert\nabla R(v(t^{\prime}))\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{2}}

We estimate each remainder term separately, but we bound
R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}
and
R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}
simultaneously. Setting

R^{(\mu_{2}\geq N^{1/2r})}:=R^{(\mu_{3}\geq N^{1/2r})}+R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}
, (
2.37
)–(
2.38
) yield

(3.17)

\left\lvert l^{2}\partial_{u_{-l}}R^{(\mu_{2}\geq N^{1/2r})}(v(t))\right\rvert\lesssim_{r,s}\sum_{n=5}^{r}N^{n-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\bm{k},-l)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\\
|k_{1}|\geq\ldots\geq|k_{n-1}|\\
|k_{1}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2r}}\end{subarray}}|l|^{2}|v_{k_{1}}(t)|\ldots|v_{k_{n-1}}(t)|

and therefore

\begin{split}\left\lVert\partial_{x}\nabla R^{(\mu_{2}\geq N^{1/2r})}(v(t))\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:R_mu2},\eqref{young convolution inequality}}}{{\lesssim_{r,s}}}\sum_{n=5}^{r}N^{n-3}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}_{|j|\geq N^{1/2r}}^{2}}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{0,1}}^{n-2}\\
&\lesssim_{r,s}\sum_{n=5}^{r}N^{n-3}N^{\frac{-s+2}{2r}}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: hp dynam new var}}}{{\lesssim_{r,s}}}N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}.\end{split}

Finally, we bound the last remainder using (
2.39
),

\begin{split}\left\lVert\partial_{x}\nabla R_{\geq r+1}(v(t))\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}&\leq\sum_{n=r+1}^{+\infty}\rho^{n}N^{n-3}\left\lVert\sum_{(\bm{k},-l)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}|l|^{2}|v_{k_{1}}(t)|\ldots|v_{k_{n-1}}(t)|\right\rVert_{\dot{\ell}_{l}^{2}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{young convolution inequality}}}{{\lesssim_{r,s}}}N^{-2}\sum_{n=r+1}^{+\infty}n\left(\rho Nc_{0}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{n-1}\lesssim_{r,s}\left(N\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\right)^{r}\end{split}

provided that
\rho Nc_{0}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}<1
, which follows by (
3.10
) by choosing
c(r,s)\leq(\rho c_{0})^{-1}
. Combining the remainder bounds with (
3.16
) yields:

(3.18)

\begin{split}\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq t\,C(r,s)\left[N^{-\frac{s}{2r}}+\left(N\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,t],\dot{H}^{s})}\right)^{r}\right].\end{split}

\bullet

Second term in (
3.15
)
. First, we bound:

(3.19)

\left\lVert e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|j|^{2}|v_{j}(0)|^{2}\,\Big|1-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}[\theta(\tau)-\theta(0)]\,\mathrm{d}\tau}\Big|^{2}\ .

Next we study the phase difference, which,
by
Theorem

2.10
, is

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}[\theta(\tau)-\theta(0)]\,\mathrm{d}\tau=\!\!\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\!\bigg(\frac{|v_{j}(0)|^{2}-|v_{j}(\tau)|^{2}}{6j}\!+\!j\sum_{n=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left[b_{(j,k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1})}+\ldots+b_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1},j)}\right]\!\left[J(\tau)^{\bm{k}}-J(0)^{\bm{k}}\right]\!\bigg)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{\pi},\end{split}

hence by (
2.35
)–(
2.36
),

\begin{split}\left\lvert\int_{0}^{t}[\theta(\tau)-\theta(0)]\,\mathrm{d}\tau\right\rvert\lesssim_{r}\int_{0}^{t}\left[\frac{1}{|j|}\left\lvert|v_{j}(\tau)|^{2}-|v_{j}(0)|^{2}\right\rvert+|j|\sum_{n=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}N^{2n-3}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n-1}}\left\lvert J(\tau)^{\bm{k}}-J(0)^{\bm{k}}\right\rvert\right]\,\mathrm{d}\tau.\end{split}

By the reverse triangle inequality

\left\lvert|v_{j}(\tau)|^{2}-|v_{j}(0)|^{2}\right\rvert=\left(|v_{j}(\tau)|+|v_{j}(0)|\right)\big\lvert|v_{j}(\tau)|-|v_{j}(0)|\big\rvert\leq\left(|v_{j}(\tau)|+|v_{j}(0)|\right)\left\lvert v_{j}(\tau)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)\right\rvert\ ,

and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality

(3.20)

\begin{split}\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{n-1}}\left\lvert J(\tau)^{\bm{k}}-J(0)^{\bm{k}}\right\rvert&\lesssim_{r}\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[0,\tau]}\left\lVert v(\tau^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\dot{L}^{2}}^{2n-4}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\left(|v_{k}(\tau)|+|v_{k}(0)|\right)\big||v_{k}(\tau)|-|v_{k}(0)|\big|\\
&\lesssim_{r}\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[0,\tau]}\left\lVert v(\tau^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\dot{L}^{2}}^{2n-3}\left\lVert v_{j}(\tau)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{L^{2}}},\end{split}

we have that

\begin{split}\left\lvert\int_{0}^{t}[\theta(\tau)-\theta(0)]\,\mathrm{d}\tau\right\rvert&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: hp dynam new var}}}{{\lesssim_{r}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left[\frac{1}{|j|}\left(|v_{j}(\tau)|+|v_{j}(0)|\right)\left\lvert v_{j}(\tau)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)\right\rvert+|j|\left\lVert v_{k}(\tau)-e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{k}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{k}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{L}^{2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\tau\\
&\lesssim_{r}t\,|j|\,\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\lVert v_{k}(\tau)-e^{i\int_{0}^{\tau}\theta_{k}(\tau^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}}v_{k}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{\ell}^{2}_{k}}.\end{split}

Plugging this bound into (
3.19
), we obtain:

\left\lVert e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau}v_{j}(0)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\lesssim_{r}t\!\!\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\lVert v_{j}(\tau)-e^{i\phi_{j}(\tau)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\!\!\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

Using (
3.18
) and (
3.15
), we obtain the desired bound (
3.13
).
∎

Remark 3.6
.

It is possible to obtain bounds on the
H^{s-1}
-norm of the approximation instead of the
H^{1}
-norm in (
3.13
). To do so, it suffices to include in
\theta(t)
certain terms from
{\partial}_{x}\nabla R^{(I\geq N^{1/2r})}
coming from differentiating the action, as is done in
[
4
, Section 7.1.3]
. However, in our case it suffices to use a norm which controls
L^{\infty}_{x}
, cf.
Theorem

4.1
.

It is also possible to obtain approximations over longer timescales (potentially up to
T=\epsilon^{-r+}
in (
3.1
)) by using nonlinear phases
\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau
instead of the linear phases
t\theta_{j}(0)
. These extended timescales rely on the improved bound (
3.18
), in contrast with the quadratic-in-time bound (
3.13
). This strategy is used in
[
5
]
for the Gravity Water Waves system (with
r=3
).

However, the linear approximation
t\theta_{j}(0)
has the key advantage of depending only on the initial datum, which is essential for propagating statistical information over long times. More precisely, the size of the quasi-synchronization event (
4.22
) is inversely proportional to the Lipschitz constant of the map sending the initial data to the phases
t\theta_{j}(0)
, and this constant grows only linearly in time. By contrast, using the phases
\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{j}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau
may lead to exponential growth of this Lipschitz constant
9
9

9

These bounds would further deteriorate if one used the phases in
[
4
, Section 7.1.3]
, which include non-integrable terms.
, as suggested by standard Grönwall estimates. If sharp, such growth would obstruct the proof of
Theorem

4.9
.

3.3
Approximate dynamics for long timescales

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

3.1
.
We want to exploit the normal form provided in
Theorem

2.10
.
For fixed
r
and
s
, the theorem allows us to do the normal form transformation in any ball of radius
\epsilon\leq c(r,s)N^{-1}
. In particular, we may fix

(3.21)

N=\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{5}},

provided that
\epsilon\leq\min\{N_{0}(r)^{-5},c(r,s)^{\frac{5}{4}}\}
.
Set
v(0)=\Phi^{-1}(u(0))
to be the initial datum in the new coordinates. Then

(3.22)

\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\left\lVert u(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert u(0)-v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: close to the identity - thm ham}}}{{\leq}}\epsilon+C(r,s)\epsilon^{\frac{9}{5}}\leq\frac{4}{3}\epsilon

provided that
\epsilon\lesssim_{r,s}1
is sufficiently small.
We proceed with a bootstrap argument.
Let

\begin{split}I=\bigg\{t\in[0,T]\,|\,&\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\left\lVert u(t^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\epsilon\bigg\},\end{split}

where
T=\epsilon^{-\frac{r}{5}}
as given in (
3.1
). Clearly,
0\in I
and
I
is closed. To show that
I
is open, fix
t\in I
.
Since
\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\epsilon
, we have that

\begin{split}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}&\leq\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert u(t)-v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: close to the identity - thm ham}}}{{\leq}}2\epsilon+C(r,s)\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{5}}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\leq C(r,s)\epsilon\ll\epsilon^{\frac{1}{5}}=N^{-1}.\end{split}

Since
v(t)
satisfies (
3.10
), we can apply (
3.11
):

\begin{split}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}&\leq\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}+C(r,s)\epsilon^{-\frac{r}{5}}\left[\epsilon^{\frac{s}{10r}}+\epsilon^{\frac{4}{5}(r+1)}\right]\leq\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}+C(r,s)\epsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}\end{split}

provided that
s\geq 12r^{2}
, which implies that

(3.23)

\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\frac{1}{3}\epsilon.

Therefore we have that

\begin{split}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}&\leq\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert u(t)-v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bootstrap1},\eqref{eq: close to the identity - thm ham}}}{{\leq}}\left\lVert v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\frac{1}{3}\epsilon+C(r,s)\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{5}}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bootstrap0}}}{{\leq}}\frac{5}{3}\epsilon+C(r,s)\epsilon^{\frac{9}{5}}<2\epsilon\end{split}

provided that
\epsilon\lesssim_{r,s}1
is sufficiently small.
This shows that
I=[0,T]
, and thus yields (
3.11
) for
t\in[0,T]
.

Finally, let
\theta_{j}
be the phases in (
3.12
). Using (
3.21
) and (
2.36
), we obtain (
3.3
).
In order to prove (
3.2
), we insert (
3.1
) and (
3.21
) into (
3.13
):

(3.24)

\begin{split}\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}&\leq C(r,s)\epsilon^{-\frac{r}{5}}\left(1+\epsilon^{-\frac{r}{5}+1}\right)\left[\epsilon^{\frac{s}{10r}}+\epsilon^{\frac{4}{5}r}\right]\leq\epsilon^{2}\end{split}

provided that
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 12r^{2}
and that
\epsilon\lesssim_{r,s}1
is sufficiently small.
Finally, by the triangle inequality,

\begin{split}\left\lVert u_{j}(t)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}u_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}&\leq\left\lVert u(t)-v(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\left\lVert v_{j}(t)-e^{it\theta_{j}(0)}v_{j}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}+\left\lVert u(0)-v(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: close to the identity - thm ham},\eqref{eq:bootstrap2}}}{{\leq}}C(r,s)\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{5}}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\right]\leq\epsilon^{\frac{8}{5}}.\end{split}

4
Large deviations principle

In this section, we prove an almost global large deviations principle for the KdV equation (
1.1
) with random initial data (
1.3
) satisfying the more flexible condition (
4.1
) below, instead of (
1.4
).

Theorem 4.1

(Almost global LDP for KdV)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda>0
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
be the (a.s. global) solution to (
1.1
) with initial datum (
1.3
) satisfying:

(4.1)

0\leq c_{k}=c_{-k},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|k|^{2\sigma}|c_{k}|^{2}<+\infty\quad\mbox{for some}\ \sigma\geq s=\max\left\{12\left\lceil\frac{7n}{1-\delta}\right\rceil^{2},\frac{1}{\delta}\right\}.

Then we have that

(4.2)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\left\lvert\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}\right\rvert=0.

Note the main theorem
Theorem

1.1
follows from
Theorem

4.1
. The proof of
Theorem

4.1
is divided into a matching upper bound (
Theorem

4.5
) and lower bound (
Theorem

4.9
).

The upper bound follows from the approximate solution (
3.2
) constructed in
Theorem

3.1
, where the moduli of the Fourier coefficients are conserved in time. As a result, its
\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}
-norm, which controls the
L^{\infty}
-norm, is constant in time. This allows us to propagate upper bounds from the initial data to any polynomial time.

The proof of the lower bound is more involved and requires the machinery introduced in
[
5
]
. The idea is to study the probability that the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the solution is close to its
\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}
-norm, which occurs when many phases are
quasi-synchronized
. This quasi-synchronization event contains the nonlinear structure of the problem, as the phases of the approximate solution are highly nonlinear functions of the initial data (via the normal form transformation), cf. (
3.3
). Proving that the probability of this event is sufficiently large over arbitrarily long polynomial timescales is the main challenge in the proof of the lower bound.

Our next result essentially follows from the proof of
Theorem

4.1
, and it shows that the dispersive focusing mechanism is dominant in the formation of extreme waves for the KdV equation.
Under the same assumptions as in
Theorem

4.1
, consider the a.s. global solution
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
in (
1.8
) to the KdV equation (
1.1
) with initial datum (
1.3
) satisfying (
4.1
).
Recall also the set
\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)
in (
1.9
), i.e. the event that the first
\mathtt{N}
phases are very close to zero.

Theorem 4.2

(Dispersive Focusing)
.

Under the same assumptions of
Theorem

4.1
we have

(4.3)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\left\lvert\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\cap\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)\right)+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}\right\rvert=0.

This result will be proved in
Section

4.3
.

Preliminary results.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, we study the KdV equation with random initial data:

(4.4)

\begin{cases}u_{t}+u_{xxx}+uu_{x}=0\\
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0,x)=\varepsilon\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c_{k}\eta_{k}^{\omega}e^{ikx}=\varepsilon\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\omega}(x),\end{cases}

where the coefficients
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
satisfy (
4.1
).
The sequence
(\eta_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is made of i.i.d standard complex Gaussian random variables (cf. (
1.52
)) on a complete probability space
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
.
Moreover we fix
\eta_{-k}^{\omega}=\overline{\eta_{k}^{\omega}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which together with the fact that
c_{k}=c_{-k}
in (
4.1
), guarantees that the random initial datum is real valued.

It is well known
[
38
]
that
\eta_{k}^{\omega}=R_{k}^{\omega}e^{i\phi_{k}^{\omega}}
where
R_{k}^{\omega}\geq 0
is a Rayleigh r.v.
\sim\mathtt{R}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)
and
\phi_{k}^{\omega}\in[0,2\pi)
is uniformly distributed.
Furthermore
(R_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\phi_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent.
Therefore we can write

(4.5)

\begin{split}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0,x)=&\ \varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\left(e^{i\phi_{k}^{\omega}+ikx}+e^{-i\phi_{k}^{\omega}-ikx}\right)=2\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\cos(kx+\phi_{k}^{\omega}).\end{split}

One can easily check that for any fixed
x\in\mathbb{T}
,
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0,x)
