is itself a centered real Gaussian random variable and that
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s
in (
4.1
) almost surely, since

(4.6)

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}=2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|k|^{2s}\,c_{k}^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}}=\sqrt{\pi}\,\varepsilon\,\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}.

We start with a large deviation result for the initial datum
\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}}
,
which was proved in
[
21
, Section 2]
for
c_{k}=\mathcal{O}(e^{-b|k|})
,
b>0
, and in
[
31
, Proposition 3.1]
for
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{1}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\lambda>0
and consider a sequence
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{1}
. Consider also a sequence

(R_{k}^{\omega})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of i.i.d. Rayleigh r.v.
\sim\mathtt{R}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)
on the same probability space
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
.
Then

(4.7)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{2}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\varepsilon\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\lambda\right)=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}^{2}}.

Remark 4.4
.

We note that the Fourier coefficients of
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)
are not independent, since
\eta_{k}=\overline{\eta_{-k}}
. As a result, whenever we use
4.3
, we will have
\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}=2\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}
, since
c_{k}=c_{-k}
. This explains the factor
4
appearing in (
4.2
).

This result can be easily extended to the linear flow of (
4.4
) which remains Gaussian. However, the nonlinear dynamics are only well-approximated by the linear flow for timescales
|t|\ll\varepsilon^{-2(1-\delta)}
. In order to go beyond these timescales, we use the approximation developed in
Theorem

3.1
:

(4.8)

u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,x)=2\,\varepsilon\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\cos\left(t\theta_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))+\phi_{j}^{\omega}+jx\right),\qquad r\geq\frac{7n}{1-\delta}

where the phases
\theta_{j}
are defined in (
3.3
) and the choice of
r
will be justified in
4.6
.
We highlight that, for
\sigma\geq 0
and
p\in[1,+\infty]
,

(4.9)

\left\lVert u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{\sigma,p}}=\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{\sigma,p}}=\varepsilon\left\lVert\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{F}L}^{\sigma,p}}.

4.1
Upper bound

We are now ready to prove the upper bound of the almost global LDP for the KdV equation.

Theorem 4.5
.

Consider the Cauchy problem with random initial data (
4.4
).
Let
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
be the corresponding unique a.s. global solution.
Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\lambda>0
. Then

(4.10)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)\leq-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}^{2}}.

Proof.

Consider the approximation
u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}
in (
4.8
) and define:

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\,|\,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\},\quad\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\,|\,\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t)-u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t)\right\rVert_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\right\}.

First, we write

(4.11)

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})={\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})+{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}^{c}).

By the reverse triangle inequality, we have that

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}^{c})&\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\cap\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)-\left\lVert u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t)\right\rVert_{L_{x}^{\infty}}<\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\right\}\right)\\
&\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\lVert u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t)\right\rVert_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}-\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:conserved_app_norm}}}{{\leq}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}}\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}-\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\right).\end{split}

In particular,
4.3
and the fact that
\delta<1
imply that

(4.12)

\log{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}^{c})=-\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\qquad\mbox{as}\ \varepsilon\rightarrow 0.

Next we bound the second term in (
4.11
).
By
Theorem

3.1
applied with
\epsilon=\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}
, and the embeddings
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\supseteq H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\supseteq\mathcal{F}L^{s,1}(\mathbb{T})
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
,
we have that
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\subset\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid\left\lVert u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(0)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{s,1}}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}\}
. As a result,

(4.13)

\begin{split}\log{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})\leq\log{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})\leq\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\lVert u^{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(0)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{s,1}}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}\right)\leq-\frac{\varepsilon^{-\frac{4}{7}-\frac{10}{7}\delta}}{8\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{2s}c_{k}^{2}}\end{split}

provided
\varepsilon
is small enough, where in the last inequality we used (
4.7
), with
c_{k}\mapsto k^{s}c_{k}
(cf. (
1.49
)). Note that when using
Theorem

3.1
, we must impose condition (
4.1
) on the regularity
s
in terms of
\delta
and
n
, which we explain in detail in
4.6
.

Finally, in view of (
4.12
)–(
4.13
), for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
we can arrange
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}^{c})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
. Then (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) yield (
4.10
), since the errors are uniform in time.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

We highlight that, when using
Theorem

3.1
with
\epsilon=\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}
, we can reach timescales
T=(\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)})^{-\frac{r}{5}}
. Imposing that such times are
T\geq\varepsilon^{-n}
leads to fixing some
r\geq\frac{7n}{1-\delta}
. Moreover,
Theorem

3.1
requires
s\geq 12r^{2}
, which explains (
4.1
).

4.2
Lower bound

Controlling the sup-norm of the solution via the
\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}
-bound allowed us to exploit the quasi-conservation of the Fourier moduli in time, which was the key to deriving the upper bound
Theorem

4.5
. In order to obtain a lower bound, however, we cannot disregard the dynamics of the phases of the Fourier coefficients.

In view of (
4.8
), these nonlinear phases are given by

\phi_{j}^{\omega}+t\,\theta_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))
. While
\phi_{j}^{\omega}
is uniformly distributed in
[0,2\pi)
, the nonlinear phases are not on account of the nontrivial way in which
\theta_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))
depend on the initial datum (and thus on the initial phases). Roughly speaking, the key idea is to study the set of quasi-synchronized phases (cf. (
4.23
))

(4.14)

\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid|\phi_{j}^{\omega}+t\,\theta_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))|\ll 1\quad\mbox{for many}\ j\}.

Within this set, we can replace the
L^{\infty}
-norm by the
\mathcal{F}L^{0,1}
-norm, which allows us to exploit the quasi-invariance of Fourier moduli once again.
One of the main challenges is therefore to show that the probability of the quasi-synchronization set does not become too small
over very long timescales
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In order to prove this property we start by constructing phases which are precisely synchronized.
To do so, we think of the initial datum as a (deterministic) function of a finite (but growing) number of phases, cf. (
4.16
).

Partially randomized initial datum.

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
to be fixed later, and define

(4.15)

\Pi_{>M}:\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}),\qquad\Pi_{>M}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}f_{j}e^{ijx}\right)=\sum_{|j|>M}f_{j}e^{ijx}.

For
\vec{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
consider the
partially randomized initial datum
(cf. (
4.5
))

(4.16)

\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(x;\vec{\phi})=2\sum_{j=1}^{M}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\cos(jx+\phi_{j})+\underbrace{2\sum_{j=M+1}^{+\infty}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\cos(jx+\phi_{j}^{\omega})}_{\Pi_{>M}\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(x)}

where
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j>M}
are i.i.d. uniformly distributed r.v. in
[0,2\pi)
,
(R_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. Rayleigh r.v.
\sim\mathtt{R}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)
, and
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j>M}
and
(R_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent.

Remark 4.7
.

If
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}=(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j=1}^{M}
are i.i.d. uniformly distributed r.v. in
[0,2\pi)
and independent of
(R_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in N}
and
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j>M}
, then our initial datum coincides with (
4.4
), namely

\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(x;\vec{\phi}^{\omega})=\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\omega}(x).

For
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
s
in (
4.1
), and recalling (
4.4
), we define the event

(4.17)

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)&=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\ \Big|\ \varepsilon\left\lVert\widetilde{u}^{\omega}_{0}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}=\varepsilon\left\lVert\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}^{\omega}_{0}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}\right\}.\end{split}

Notice that this definition does not depend on
M
, as the
H^{s}
-norm does not depend on the phases.

We also introduce the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}
generated by the random moduli and the high-Fourier modes, i.e.

(4.18)

\mathcal{G}=\sigma((R_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in\mathbb{N}},(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j>M}).

Note that the event
\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
depends only on the random variables
(R_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. As a result,
\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable. It will also be convenient to define the restriction of
\mathcal{G}
to the event
\mathcal{B}
denoted by

(4.19)

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=\{G\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\,\lvert\,G\in\mathcal{G}\}\subseteq\mathcal{G}.

Finally, we introduce the
restricted probability space

(\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s),\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}})
, where
\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}(G)={\mathbb{P}}(G)
for all
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
and
(\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s),\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}})
are complete and independent of the initial uniformly distributed phases
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
.

Random fixed point.

For any
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
, we may write the phases in (
4.8
) explicitly (cf. (
3.3
) and recall
\epsilon=\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}
) as:

(4.20)

\theta_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))=j^{3}-\frac{1}{6j}|\Phi^{-1}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))_{j}|^{2}+j\sum_{m=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\,\,\sum_{\bm{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{m-1}}b_{(j,\bm{k})}|\Phi^{-1}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0))^{\bm{k}}|^{2},\qquad|b_{(j,\bm{k})}|\lesssim_{r}\varepsilon^{-\frac{2\#k-3}{7}(1-\delta)},

where
\Phi^{-1}
are defined in
Theorem

2.10
, and
r\geq\frac{7n}{1-\delta}
. This choice of
r
is made so that
u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}
in (
4.8
) is a good approximation of the KdV dynamics under long timescales, as explained in
4.6
.

We thus define the random operator

(4.21)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M},\\
&\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)=-t\theta_{j}(\varepsilon\,\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi})),\qquad j=1,\ldots,M.\end{split}

This operator allows us to rewrite the problem of finding a point in (
4.14
) where phases exactly synchronize into a fixed point problem for the mapping
\mathcal{T}
. Below we will prove that for each
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
there exists

\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
such that

t\theta_{j}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}))+\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}=0\qquad\forall\,\,1\leq j\leq M.

The key difficulty is proving that
\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}
is
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
-measurable. We prove this via a random Brouwer fixed point theorem
[
6
]
, in the spirit of classical measurable selection theorems such as Kuratowski–Ryll-Nardzewski, see also
[
8
, Chapter 3.2]
.
The following proposition is an adaptation of
[
5
, Proposition 5.3]
.

Proposition 4.8

(Random fixed point)
.

Let
M,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
and
s
in (
4.1
).
There exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,\delta)>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and for any
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}
, the random operator
\mathcal{T}
defined in (
4.21
) satisfies the following properties:

(i)
Fixed point:
There exists a random variable
\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}=(\phi_{j}^{*,\omega})_{j=1}^{M}
in
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
such that for every
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)

\mathcal{T}(\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega},\omega)=\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}.

(ii)
Neighborhood of the fixed point:
If
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}=(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j=1}^{M}
are i.i.d uniformly distributed r.v. in
[0,2\pi)
and independent of
\mathcal{G}
in (
4.18
),
\beta\in(0,\pi)
and we define

(4.22)

\mathcal{N}(\beta)=\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\,\big\lvert\,|\phi_{j}^{\omega}-\phi_{j}^{*,\omega}|<\beta\quad\forall j=1,\ldots,M\},

then
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable and
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{N}(\beta))=\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)^{M}
.

(iii)
Stability property:
For any
\omega\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
we have

(4.23)

|\phi_{j}^{\omega}-\mathcal{T}(\phi_{j}^{\omega},\omega)|\leq(1+M|t|)\beta,\qquad j=1,\ldots,M.

(iv)
Factorization property:
For any measurable set
\mathcal{A}\in\mathcal{G}
,

(4.24)

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta))=\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)^{M}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}).

It is worth to notice that the factorization property (
4.24
) holds even if
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
is not independent of
\mathcal{G}
.
The proof of
4.8
is postponed to
Section

4.4
.
In particular the proof of (
4.24
) relies on
Lemma

4.10
below.
Finally we prove the lower bound of the large deviations principle which, together with
Theorem

4.5
, yields
Theorem

4.1
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Consider the Cauchy problem (
4.4
) with random initial data satisfying (
4.1
), which a.s. admits a unique global solution
u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)
.
Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\lambda>0
.
Then

(4.25)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\inf_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}^{2}}\geq 0.

Proof.

Recall
\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
in (
4.17
) and the approximate solution
u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}
in (
4.8
).
By the reverse triangle inequality, the embedding
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, and
Theorem

3.1
applied with
\epsilon=\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right)\geq{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t)-u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\right\}\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\right)

(4.26)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def: event small size initial datum},\eqref{eq: approx fasi lineari}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\right\}\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\right)

(4.27)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\left(\left\{u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,0)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\right\}\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta)\right),

where
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
was defined in (
4.22
). For
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta)
, and the estimate
\cos y\geq 1-y^{2}/2
, we have

\begin{split}u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,0)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: random approximate solution},\eqref{def: random phase nonlinear operator}}}{{\geq}}2\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\cos(\phi_{k}^{\omega}-\mathcal{T}_{k}(\vec{\phi}^{\omega},\omega))-\left\lvert\varepsilon\sum_{|k|>M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\cos(\phi_{k}^{\omega}+t\theta_{k}^{\omega})\right\rvert\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: stability}}}{{\geq}}2\varepsilon\left(1-\frac{(1+M|t|)^{2}\,\beta^{2}}{2}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}-C\left\lVert u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(0)\right\rVert_{H_{|k|\geq M}^{1}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def: event small size initial datum}}}{{\geq}}2\varepsilon\left(1-M^{2}\varepsilon^{-2n}\beta^{2}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}-CM^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}.\end{split}

We fix
\beta=M^{-1}\,\varepsilon^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
, and continue with the lower bound:

\begin{split}\eqref{third inequality}&\geq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\underbrace{\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}+CM^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}}{2\varepsilon\,(1-\varepsilon)}\right\}\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)}_{\mathcal{A}\ \mathrm{in\ \eqref{eq: factorization}}}\cap\,\,\mathcal{N}(\beta)\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: factorization}}}{{\geq}}\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)^{M}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}+CM^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}}{2\varepsilon\left(1-\varepsilon\right)}\right\}\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def: event small size initial datum}}}{{\geq}}\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)^{M}\left[{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}+CM^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}}{2\varepsilon\left(1-\varepsilon\right)}\right)-{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\lVert\tilde{u}_{0}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{\dot{H^{s}}}>\varepsilon^{-\frac{2}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\right)\right].\end{split}

Let us choose
M=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-\delta}\rfloor
. As a result of this choice, we have that

(4.28)

\begin{split}\varepsilon^{2\delta}&\log{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\bigg)\geq M\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)\\
&+\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log\left[{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}+CM^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}}{2\varepsilon\left(1-\varepsilon\right)}\right)-{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\lVert\tilde{u}_{0}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{\dot{H^{s}}}>\varepsilon^{-\frac{2}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\right)\right],\end{split}

with

(4.29)

0\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}M\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{\delta}\log\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{n+\frac{1}{2}-\delta}}{\pi}\right)=0.

Recall that
s\geq\frac{1}{\delta}>\frac{2+5\delta}{7\delta}
by (
4.1
). This choice of
s
ensures that
M^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}\ll\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
. As a result,

\frac{\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+C_{s}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}+CM^{1-s}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}}{2\varepsilon\left(1-\varepsilon\right)}=\lambda\varepsilon^{-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-\delta})

Finally, for any
S\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, (
4.28
) yields

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\inf_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\bigg)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: quasisynch zero}}}{{\geq}}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\inf_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log\left[{\mathbb{P}}\left(2\sum_{k=1}^{S}c_{k}R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-\delta})\right)-{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\lVert\tilde{u}_{0}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{\dot{H^{s}}}>\varepsilon^{-\frac{2}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\right)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: grande},\eqref{eq:intersection2}}}{{=}}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k=1}^{S}c_{k}^{2}},\end{split}

and we obtain (
4.25
) by letting
S\to+\infty.

∎

4.3
Dispersive focusing

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

4.2
.
Notice that the upper bound for the
\limsup
in (
4.3
) follows immediately by (
4.2
).
Let us prove the lower bound.
First let
\kappa=\min\left\{\frac{\delta}{2},\frac{1-\delta}{14}\right\}
and consider

(4.30)

\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\,|\,R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\varepsilon^{-\delta+\kappa},\,j=1,\ldots,\mathtt{N}\right\}.

We claim that

(i)

Outside the set
\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)
, extreme waves are less likely to appear,

(4.31)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)^{c}\right)<-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}};

(ii)

For
\varepsilon\lesssim_{\mathtt{N},\delta}1
sufficiently small,

(4.32)

\mathcal{N}(\beta)\cap\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\subseteq\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s),

where
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
is defined in (
4.22
), with
M=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-\delta}\rfloor
and
\beta=M^{-1}\varepsilon^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
as in
Theorem

4.9
, and
\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
is defined in (
4.17
).

Assume for the moment these two claims, and recall the approximate solution
u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}
in (
4.8
). Then

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\cap\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{for dispersive focusing}}}{{\geq}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,0)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\cap\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{inclusion}}}{{\geq}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,0)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta)\cap\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\right)\\
&\geq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,0)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta)\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\right)-{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon,r}^{\omega}(t,0)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)^{c}\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{disp foc 1}}}{{\geq}}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\right)\end{split}

proceeding as in
Theorem

4.9
. As a result, we need only prove the two claims (i) and (ii).

Claim
(i). It is sufficient to notice that

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)^{c}\right)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: moduli grandi}}}{{\leq}}&\sum_{k=1}^{\mathtt{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}u_{\mathrm{app},\varepsilon}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right\}\cap\left\{R_{k}^{\omega}<\varepsilon^{-\delta+\kappa}\right\}\right)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: random approximate solution}}}{{\leq}}&\sum_{k=1}^{\mathtt{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-\delta})\right\}\cap\left\{R_{k}^{\omega}<\varepsilon^{-\delta+\kappa}\right\}\right)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\mathtt{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\smallsetminus\{\pm k\}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\lambda\varepsilon^{-\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-\delta})\right)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: grande}}}{{\leq}}&\mathtt{N}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{4\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c_{k}^{2}-4\min\{c_{k}^{2},\,\,k=1,\ldots,\mathtt{N}\}}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\right)\end{split}

and (
4.31
) follows by taking logarithms, taking the supremum in
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-n}
and taking
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

Claim
(ii). Let
\omega\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)\cap\mathfrak{R}(\mathtt{N},\kappa,\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
.
We claim that
\omega\in\mathfrak{P}(\mathtt{N},\delta,\varepsilon)
, cf. (
1.9
).
By the triangle inequality we obtain (cf. (
4.8
))

(4.33)

\begin{split}\varepsilon\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\left\lvert e^{i\psi_{j}^{\omega}(t)}-e^{i\phi_{j}^{\omega}+it\theta_{j}(u_{0}^{\omega})}\right\rvert&\leq\varepsilon\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}-|u_{j}(t)|\right\rvert+\varepsilon\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}e^{i\phi_{j}^{\omega}+it\theta_{j}(u_{0}^{\omega})}-u_{j}(t)\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: approx fasi lineari}}}{{\leq}}2\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\end{split}

using
Theorem

3.1
with
\epsilon=\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)}
, cf. (
4.17
).
Within
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
we have

(4.34)

\left\lvert\phi_{j}^{\omega}+t\theta_{j}(u_{0}^{\omega})\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: stability}}}{{\leq}}(1+M\varepsilon^{-n})\beta\leq 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},

since
M=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-\delta}\rfloor
and
\beta=M^{-1}\varepsilon^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
. Using the fact that
c_{j}\neq 0
in (
1.9
),

\left\lvert e^{i\psi_{j}^{\omega}(t)}-e^{i\phi_{j}^{\omega}+it\theta_{j}(u_{0}^{\omega})}\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: moduli grandi},\eqref{disp foc 2}}}{{\lesssim_{\mathtt{N}}}}\varepsilon^{\frac{8}{7}(1-\delta)}\varepsilon^{-1+\delta-\kappa}\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1-\delta}{14}}

and by the triangle inequality

\left\lvert e^{i\psi_{j}^{\omega}(t)}-1\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{stima fasi approx}}}{{\leq}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1-\delta}{14}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\frac{1-\delta}{15}}

provided that
\varepsilon\lesssim_{\mathtt{N},\delta}1
is sufficiently small.
Finally, we use the inequality
\frac{|x|}{2}\leq|\sin(x)|\leq|e^{ix}-1|
for
|x|\ll 1
.

∎

4.4
Random Brouwer fixed point

Lemma 4.10

(Properties of conditional expectation)
.

Let
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
be a probability space.
Let
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a sub
\sigma
-algebra.

1) If
X\in L^{1}(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
, then

(4.35)

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\lvert\mathcal{G}\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[X\right].

2) If
X\in L^{1}(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
and
Y\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable, then

(4.36)

\mathbb{E}\left[XY\lvert\mathcal{G}\right]=Y\mathbb{E}\left[X\lvert\mathcal{G}\right].

3) If
X:\mathbb{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
\mathcal{G}
-measurable random variable,
Y:\mathbb{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a random variable independent of
\mathcal{G}

and
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a Borel function such that
\Psi(X,Y)
and
\Psi(x,Y)
are integrable (
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
), then

(4.37)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi(X,Y)\lvert\mathcal{G}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi(x,Y)\right]_{\lvert x=X}.

Proof.

See
[
39
, Chapter 10]
.
∎

For the existence of the fixed point we use
Theorem

4.11
below, whose proof may be found in
[
6
, Theorem 10]
.

Theorem 4.11

(Random Brouwer fixed point)
.

Let
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
be a complete probability space and
K
be a convex and compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}:K\times\mathbb{\Omega}\longrightarrow K
be such that:

1.

for any fixed
\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}
, the map
\mathcal{T}(\cdot,\omega):K\longrightarrow K
is continuous;

2.

for any
\phi\in K
, the map
\mathcal{T}(\phi,\cdot):\mathbb{\Omega}\longrightarrow K
is measurable.

Then there exists a random variable
\phi^{\omega}
such that
\mathcal{T}(\phi^{\omega},\omega)=\phi^{\omega}
almost surely in
\mathbb{\Omega}.

Proof of
4.8
.
We divide the proof in several points as in the statement.

(i)
Fixed point.
In order to use
Theorem

4.11
, we prove the following two properties:

(i.1)

Lipschitzianity with respect to
\vec{\phi}
:
For any
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
,
\vec{\phi},\underline{\vec{\phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, and
j=1,\ldots,M
,

(4.38)

\lvert\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)-\mathcal{T}_{j}(\underline{\vec{\phi}},\omega)\rvert\leq M|t|\varepsilon^{\frac{10}{7}(1-\delta)}\,\left\lVert\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}.

Proof.

A straightforward computation as in
Theorem

3.5
yields, using (
4.20
) and (
4.21
),

(4.39)

\begin{split}\lvert\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)&-\mathcal{T}_{j}(\underline{\vec{\phi}},\omega)\rvert\leq\frac{|t|}{6|j|}\left\lvert|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}))_{j}|^{2}-|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\underline{\vec{\phi}}))_{j}|^{2}\right\rvert\\
&+C(n,\delta)|tj|\sum_{m=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\varepsilon^{-\frac{2m-3}{7}(1-\delta)}\varepsilon^{\frac{10}{7}(m-2)(1-\delta)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}))_{k}|^{2}-|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\underline{\vec{\phi}}))_{k}|^{2}\right\rvert\end{split}

where we used the fact that
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
(cf. (
4.17
)) and that
\left\lVert\Phi^{-1}(u)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq 2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
.

By the reverse triangle inequality and (
2.62
), we have

(4.40)

\begin{split}&\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}))_{k}|^{2}-|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\underline{\vec{\phi}}))_{k}|^{2}\right\rvert\leq 4\varepsilon^{\frac{12}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\left\lVert\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi})-\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\underline{\vec{\phi}})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}.\end{split}

Inserting (
4.40
) in (
4.39
), using that
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
, provided that
\varepsilon\lesssim_{n,\delta}1
is sufficiently small, we obtain

\begin{split}\lvert\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)-\mathcal{T}_{j}(\underline{\vec{\phi}},\omega)\rvert&\leq M|t|\varepsilon^{\frac{12}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\,\left\lVert\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi})-\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\underline{\vec{\phi}})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq M|t|\varepsilon^{\frac{12}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\,\left\lVert\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi})\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\left\lVert\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}\\
&\leq M|t|\varepsilon^{\frac{10}{7}(1-\delta)}\,\left\lVert\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}.\end{split}

(i.2)

Measurability with respect to
\omega
:
For any
\vec{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, the map

\mathcal{T}(\vec{\phi},\cdot):(\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s),\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{M},\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R}^{M}))

is measurable, where
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R}^{M})
denotes the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
.

Proof.
Define the deterministic set

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\delta,\varepsilon,s,M)=\left\{v=(\vec{r},\widetilde{u}_{0,M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\dot{H}^{s}\,\big\lvert\,\Pi_{\leq M}\widetilde{u}_{0,M}=0,\,\,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{2}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\right\},

where

\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}:=\left(2\sum_{j=1}^{M}j^{2s}c_{j}^{2}r_{j}^{2}+\left\lVert\widetilde{u}_{0,M}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We also introduce the deterministic operator

(4.41)

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\delta,\varepsilon,s,M)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad j=1,\ldots,M,\\
&\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\vec{\phi},v)=-t\theta_{j}(\widetilde{u}_{0}(v,\vec{\phi})),\qquad\quad\widetilde{u}_{0}(v,\vec{\phi})=2\sum_{j=1}^{M}c_{j}r_{j}\cos(jx+\phi_{j})+\widetilde{u}_{0,M}(x),\end{split}

and the random map

(4.42)

\iota:\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)\subseteq\mathbb{\Omega}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\delta,\varepsilon,s,M)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\dot{H}^{s},\quad\iota(\omega)=\left((R_{j}^{\omega})_{j=1}^{M},\Pi_{>M}\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\omega})\right).

Then we have the following facts.

–

The random map
\iota
defined in (
4.42
) is measurable.
Indeed it is weakly measurable, in the sense that given a functional
f
in the dual of
\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\dot{H}^{s}
, then
f\circ\iota
is measurable.
Since
\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\dot{H}^{s}
is a separable Banach space, by the Pettis measurability theorem
[
37
]
,
\iota
is also strongly measurable.

–

The deterministic map
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is Lipschitz-continuous.
Indeed, proceeding as in (
4.39
)–(
4.40
), we find

\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\vec{\phi},v)-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\underline{\vec{\phi}},\underline{v})\rvert\leq M\,|t|\varepsilon^{\frac{12}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\,\left(\left\lVert v-\underline{v}\right\rVert_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}+\varepsilon^{-\frac{2}{7}-\frac{5}{7}\delta}\left\lVert\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}\right).

–

The map
\mathcal{T}
is given by the composition
\mathcal{T}(\vec{\phi},\omega)=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{\phi},\iota(\omega))
.

The measurability of
\mathcal{T}(\vec{\phi},\cdot)
follows from the fact that it is the composition the measurable map
\iota
and the continuous map
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{\phi},\cdot)
.

In order to set up the random fixed point argument, we first note that, for
\varepsilon\lesssim_{n,\delta}1
sufficiently small,

(4.43)

\begin{split}\lvert\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: approx_phases random}}}{{\leq}}&\ |t||j|^{3}+\frac{|t|}{6|j|}|\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}))_{j}|^{2}+C(n,\delta)|tj|\sum_{m=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\varepsilon^{-\frac{2m-3}{7}(1-\delta)}\left\lVert\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathtt{u}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}))\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2m-2}\\
\leq&\ M^{3}|t|+\frac{|t|}{6|j|}\varepsilon^{\frac{10}{7}(1-\delta)}+M|t|C(n,\delta)\sum_{m=3}^{\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor}\varepsilon^{-\frac{2m-3}{7}(1-\delta)}(2\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{7}(1-\delta)})^{2m-2}\leq 2M^{3}|t|.\end{split}

Using (i.1) and (i.2), we can apply
Theorem

4.11
on the convex and compact subset
K=[-2M^{3}|t|,2M^{3}|t|]^{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{M}
.
In particular, note that
\mathcal{T}(\cdot,\omega):K\longrightarrow K
, using (
4.43
) and the fact that
\mathcal{T}
is
2\pi
-periodic in
\vec{\phi}
. We note that the fixed point
\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}
is defined in the restricted probability space
(\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s),\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}})
and can be extended to the probability space
(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
by setting
\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}=0
for
\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\setminus\mathcal{B}(\delta,\varepsilon,s)
.

(ii)
Neighborhood of the fixed point:
The measurability of
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
follows from the fact that
\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mapsto(\vec{\phi}^{\omega},\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega})
is measurable and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\mapsto\left\lVert a-b\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}
is continuous.
Its probability is a consequence of (
4.24
) with
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{\Omega}.

(iii)
Stability property:
By step (i),

\mathcal{T}(\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega},\omega)-\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}=0
. For any
\omega\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)
,

\left\lvert\phi_{j}-\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\phi_{j}-\phi_{j}^{*,\omega}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega},\omega)-\mathcal{T}_{j}(\vec{\phi},\omega)\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{lipschitz},\eqref{def: intorno}}}{{\leq}}\left(1+M|t|\varepsilon^{\frac{10}{7}(1-\delta)}\right)\beta.

(iv)
Factorization property:
Notice that for any
\mathcal{G}
-measurable event
\mathcal{A}
:

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta))=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\beta)}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{tower property}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\beta)}\lvert\mathcal{G}\right]\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{take out what is known}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\beta)}\lvert\mathcal{G}\right]\right]

Now we apply (
4.37
) with
X=\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}
(which is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable by point (i)),
Y=\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
(which is
\mathcal{G}
-independent) and
\Psi(x,y)=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\beta;x)}(y)
, where

\mathcal{N}(\beta;x)=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\,\lvert\,\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}<\beta\}.

We find that

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{N}(\beta))=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\beta;x)}(\vec{\phi}^{\omega})\right]_{\lvert x=\vec{\phi}^{*,\omega}}\right]=\left(\frac{\beta}{\pi}\right)^{M}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A}).

\hfill\qed

Appendix A
Technical results

A.1
The KdV hierarchy

In this appendix we prove
Lemma

2.1
characterizing the KdV hierarchy.
We first introduce the notion of
rank
of a first integral given in
[
33
]
.
Given a monomial
P
in
u
and its derivatives, we define

(A.1)

P(u)=\prod_{k=0}^{r}({\partial}_{x}^{k}u)^{a_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{rank}(P)=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\left(1+\frac{k}{2}\right)a_{k},

where
k
is the order of derivation and
a_{k}
the corresponding exponent. Moreover,
A=\sum_{k=0}^{r}a_{k}
is the
degree
of
P(u)
as a monomial in
u
and
B=\sum_{k=1}^{r}k\,a_{k}
is the total number of derivatives in
P(u)
.

Lemma A.1
.

For
j=1,\ldots,n
let
m_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
,
s>\max_{j=1,\ldots,n}m_{j}+\frac{1}{2}
and

P_{j}(u)=\prod_{i=0}^{m_{j}}({\partial}_{x}^{i}u)^{a_{i}^{(j)}}\quad A_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{m_{j}}a_{i}^{(j)},\quad B_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{j}}ia_{i}^{(j)}.

If
u\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
then each
P_{j}(u)
is a continuous function of
x\in\mathbb{T}
and if
\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{j}(u)=0
on
\dot{H}^{s}
, then for any fixed
A,B\in\mathbb{R},

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
A_{j}=A,\,B_{j}=B\end{subarray}}P_{j}(u)=0\qquad\text{on $\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}).$}

Proof.

The continuity (hence the boundedness) of
P_{j}(u)
follows by the Sobolev embedding and the algebra property for
\dot{H}^{s}
with
s>\frac{1}{2}
.
For any
\lambda>0
we have

0=\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{j}(\lambda u)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda^{A_{j}}P_{j}(u).

Without loss of generality we can assume
A_{1}\geq A_{2},\ldots,A_{n}
and so

0=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda^{A_{j}-A_{1}}P_{j}(u).

Taking
\lambda\to\infty
we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
A_{j}=A\end{subarray}}P_{j}(u)=0.

We may thus assume that all the
P_{j}
’s have the same degree
A
from now on. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and consider
u_{m}(x)=u(mx)
.
We obtain

0=\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{j}(u_{m})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}m^{B_{j}}P_{j}(u)\right)(mx).

Again, without loss of generality, we can assume
B_{1}\geq B_{2},\ldots,B_{n}
. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{T}
,

(A.2)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
B_{j}=B_{1}\end{subarray}}P_{j}(u)\right)\left(mx\right)=0.

In particular, setting
x=\frac{2\pi p}{q}
, with
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
,
p<q
, then the sequence
\{mx\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
takes
q
values.
Taking the subsequence
m\equiv 1
(mod
q
) in (
A.2
), we find that
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
B_{j}=B_{1}\end{subarray}}P_{j}(u)
vanishes on
2\pi\mathbb{Q}\cap\mathbb{T}
, and thus in
\mathbb{T}
by continuity.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

2.1
.

Consider a monomial
P
in (
A.1
).
The following properties are easy to prove: (i) the operator
{\partial}_{x}
increases the rank by
\frac{1}{2}
, (ii) integration by parts leaves the rank constant, and (iii)
\nabla_{L^{2}}
decreases the rank by
1
.

Rank of monomials in
F^{(j)}
.

As proved in
[
29
,
32
]
, the first integrals
F^{(j)}
in (
2.6
) satisfy the following recursive formula:

(A.3)

J_{1}\nabla_{L^{2}}F^{(j+1)}=J_{2}\nabla_{L^{2}}F^{(j)},\qquad J_{1}={\partial}_{x},\quad J_{2}=-\left(\partial_{xxx}+\frac{2}{3}u\partial_{x}+\frac{1}{3}u_{x}\right).

Using properties (i)-(iii), we want to show that each monomial in
F^{(j)}
has rank
j+2
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Indeed,
F^{(1)}=\mathcal{H}
in (
1.23
) only has monomials of rank 3 and the only one of degree
2
up to a constant is
\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{x}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
.
Assuming by induction that each monomial in
F^{(j)}
has rank
j+2
and that the only monomial of degree
2
up to a constant is
\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}({\partial}_{x}^{j}u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
, we show that monomials in
F^{(j+1)}
must have rank
j+3
and that the only monomial of degree 2 up to a constant is
\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}({\partial}_{x}^{j+1}u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
.
By properties (i)-(iii) above, all non-zero monomials in
J_{2}\nabla_{L^{2}}F^{(j)}
have rank
j+2+\frac{1}{2}
, and the only monomial of degree 1 is, up to a constant,
{\partial}_{x}^{2j+3}u
.
Since the latter is the only monomial of degree 1,
Lemma

A.1
implies that
J_{2}\nabla_{L^{2}}F^{(j)}
is not identically zero.
If any monomials in
J_{1}\nabla_{L^{2}}F^{(j+1)}
had rank different from
j+2+\frac{1}{2}
, (
A.3
) would yield an equality between sums of monomials of different ranks, which, by
Lemma

A.1
, implies that the sum of any terms of rank different from
j+2+\frac{1}{2}
vanishes.
As a result, monomials in
J_{1}\nabla_{L^{2}}F^{(j+1)}
must have rank
j+2+\frac{1}{2}
, which, given that
J_{1}\nabla_{L^{2}}
decreases the rank by
\frac{1}{2}
, must come from monomials of
F^{(j+1)}
of rank
j+3
.
Moreover, the only monomial of degree 2 in
F^{(j+1)}
is, up to a constant,
\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}({\partial}_{x}^{j+1}u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x
.

Structure of
F^{(j)}
.

Each monomial
P
in
F^{(j)}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, has rank

j+2=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\left(1+\frac{k}{2}\right)a_{k}.

If we isolate the term of degree
n=\sum_{k=0}^{r}a_{k}
, we have

\sum_{k=0}^{r}ka_{k}=2(j+2-n),

which proves that we cannot have terms of degree
n>j+2
.
Moreover, if
n=2
, by integrating by parts we can always assume
a_{k}=0
for
k\neq j
and
a_{j}=2
.
From now on assume
n\geq 3
.
If
u
appears in a monomial of
F^{(j)}(u)
of degree
n
, with
r\geq j
derivatives, then it has to appear with degree 1, because

ra_{r}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{r}ka_{k}=2(j+2-n)\quad\implies\quad a_{r}\leq\frac{2(j+2-n)}{r}<2.

Suppose that there exists at least one nonzero
a_{r}
for
r\geq j
.
Then we have shown that
a_{r}=1
and

\sum_{k=1}^{r-1}ka_{k}=2(j+2-n)-r\leq 2j+4-2n-r\leq 2j-2-r.

Since
n\geq 3
, we may write
P(u)=({\partial}_{x}^{r}u)\,Q(u)
where
Q
is another monomial with at most two factors. Moreover, the total number of derivatives among the factors of
Q
is at most
2j-2-r
.
Integrating by parts
r-(j-1)
times, we find that

\int_{\mathbb{T}}({\partial}_{x}^{r}u)\,Q(u)\,\mathrm{d}x=(-1)^{r-(j-1)}\int_{\mathbb{T}}({\partial}_{x}^{j-1}u)\,{\partial}_{x}^{r-j+1}Q(u)\,\mathrm{d}x.

The total number of derivatives in
{\partial}_{x}^{r-j+1}Q(u)
is thus
2j-2-r+(r-j+1)=j-1
.

Since
F^{(j)}
is the sum of a degree 2 monomial plus monomials of degree
n\geq 3
, we have proved that, up to a multiplicative constant,
F^{(j)}
has the form:

F^{(j)}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{j}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sum_{n=3}^{j+2}P_{n}^{(j)}(u,u_{1},\ldots,u_{j-1})\,\mathrm{d}x=\pi\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}k^{2j}|u_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{n=3}^{j+2}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}u^{\bm{k}}.

Passing to Fourier variables, notice that in principle
c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{Q}(i)[k_{1},\ldots,k_{\#\bm{k}}]
, but since the rank is an integer number and each space derivative counts as
\frac{1}{2}
in the rank, we can conclude that there is an even number of space derivatives and that
c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{Q}[k_{1},\ldots,k_{\#\bm{k}}].

Finally notice that if
n\geq 3
and
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, then the total degree of
c_{\bm{k}}^{(j)}
is
\sum_{k=0}^{j}ka_{k}=2(j-n+2)\leq 2j-2.

∎

A.2
Intersection of resonant manifolds

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

2.13
, which follows from the stronger
Theorem

A.2
below by taking
\alpha_{1}=\ldots=\alpha_{n}=1
.

Theorem A.2
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the system of
n
equations

(A.4)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}k_{1}+\alpha_{2}k_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}k_{n}&=0\\
\alpha_{1}k_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{2}k_{2}^{3}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}k_{n}^{3}&=0\\
&\vdots\\
\alpha_{1}k_{1}^{2n-1}+\alpha_{2}k_{2}^{2n-1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}k_{n}^{2n-1}&=0,\end{cases}

where
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. We require that
k_{j}\neq 0
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
. Then:

•

If
S=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}
is odd, there is no integer solution to (
A.4
).

•

If
S
is even, all the solutions
(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{n})
are
weightedly paired
, meaning that if we build the vector

(k_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,k_{S}^{\prime})=(k_{1,1},\ldots,k_{1,\alpha_{1}},k_{2,1},\ldots,k_{2,\alpha_{2}},\ldots,k_{n,1},\ldots,k_{n,\alpha_{n}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{S},

where
k_{j,1}=k_{j,2}=\ldots=k_{j,\alpha_{j}}=k_{j}
, there exists a permutation
\sigma\in Sym(S)
such that

k_{\sigma(2i-1)}^{\prime}+k_{\sigma(2i)}^{\prime}=0\qquad\text{for $i=1,\ldots,\frac{S}{2}.$}

Proof.

First, we write (
A.4
) as a system:

\begin{bmatrix}k_{1}&k_{2}&\dots&k_{n}\\
k_{1}^{3}&k_{2}^{3}&\dots&k_{n}^{3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
k_{1}^{2n-1}&k_{2}^{2n-1}&\dots&k_{n}^{2n-1}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\alpha_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{n}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\neq 0
, we must have a singular matrix:

(A.5)

\begin{split}0&=\mathrm{det}\begin{bmatrix}k_{1}&k_{2}&\dots&k_{n}\\
k_{1}^{3}&k_{2}^{3}&\dots&k_{n}^{3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
k_{1}^{2n-1}&k_{2}^{2n-1}&\dots&k_{n}^{2n-1}\end{bmatrix}=k_{1}k_{2}\dots k_{n}\cdot\mathrm{det}\begin{bmatrix}1&1&\dots&1\\
k_{1}^{2}&k_{2}^{2}&\dots&k_{n}^{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
k_{1}^{2n-2}&k_{2}^{2n-2}&\dots&k_{n}^{2n-2}\end{bmatrix}\\
&=k_{1}k_{2}\dots k_{n}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(k_{i}^{2}-k_{j}^{2})=k_{1}k_{2}\dots k_{n}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(k_{i}-k_{j})(k_{i}+k_{j})=0,\end{split}

where we used the explicit formula for the determinant of the Vandermonde matrix.
This implies that there exist
i,j
such that
k_{i}+k_{j}=0
or
k_{i}-k_{j}=0
.
The rest of the proof proceeds by induction, in particular we will prove that if the theorem is true for a system of dimension
n-2
and
n-1
, then it is true for
n
. We need two base cases.

Base Case 1
: If
n=1
, then
\alpha_{1}k_{1}\neq 0
by hypothesis but (
A.4
) forces
\alpha_{1}k_{1}=0
, hence there is no solution.

Base Case 2:
If
n=2
, (
A.4
) becomes:

(A.6)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}k_{1}+\alpha_{2}k_{2}=0\\
\alpha_{1}k_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{2}k_{2}^{3}=0,\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\mbox{with}\ \alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{N}.

If we have a solution
(k_{1},k_{2})
to (
A.6
) with
k_{1}k_{2}\neq 0
, then (
A.5
) implies
k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}=0
. However, we cannot have
k_{1}=k_{2}
, since otherwise (
A.6
) implies that
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=0
, which is not possible. Therefore
k_{1}=-k_{2}\neq 0
. Now we distinguish the two cases presented in the statement:

•

If
S=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
is odd, there exist no solutions by Base Case 1.

•

If
S=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
is even, then
(k_{1},k_{2})
is a solution if and only if
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2},
which means that
(k_{1},k_{2})
is weightedly paired.

General Case:
Next we consider (
A.4
) with
n\geq 3
.
Let
\bm{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
be a solution to (
A.4
) with
\prod_{j=1}^{n}k_{j}\neq 0
.
By (
A.5
), there are three possibilities:

•

If
k_{i}-k_{j}=0
, up to a permutation of indices we can assume
k_{n-1}=k_{n}
and we arrive to

(A.7)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}k_{n-1}&=0\\
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}^{3}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}k_{n-1}^{3}&=0\\
&\vdots\\
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{2n-3}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}^{2n-3}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}k_{n-1}^{2n-3}&=0,\end{cases}

with
\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}
and
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n-2
.
By the inductive hypothesis, since
S^{\prime}=S
, if
S
is even, the system (
A.7
) has weightedly paired solutions, therefore
\bm{k}
is weightedly paired. If
S
is odd we have no solutions to (
A.7
), and thus
\bm{k}
doesn’t solve (
A.4
).

•

If
k_{i}+k_{j}=0
with
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\neq 0
, up to a permutation of indices we arrive to

(A.8)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}k_{n-1}&=0\\
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}^{3}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}k_{n-1}^{3}&=0\\
&\vdots\\
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{2n-3}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}^{2n-3}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}k_{n-1}^{2n-3}&=0,\end{cases}

with
\alpha_{n-1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{n-1}-\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
(without loss of generality) and
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n-2
. Notice that the indices we have removed are already weightedly paired.
Since
S^{\prime}=S-2\alpha_{n}
,
S-S^{\prime}\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\,2)
, hence by the inductive hypothesis the system (
A.8
) has weightedly paired solutions if
S
is even (and the same holds for
\bm{k}
), and no solutions if
S
is odd.

•

If
k_{i}+k_{j}=0
with
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}=0
, up to a permutation of indices can assume
k_{n-1}+k_{n}=0
and we arrive to

(A.9)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-2}^{\prime}k_{n-2}&=0\\
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}^{3}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-2}^{\prime}k_{n-2}^{3}&=0\\
&\vdots\\
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{2n-5}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}k_{2}^{2n-5}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-2}^{\prime}k_{n-2}^{2n-5}&=0.\end{cases}

with
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=\alpha_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,n-2
.
Notice that
k_{i}
and
k_{j}
that we have removed are already weightedly paired. Again
S-S^{\prime}\equiv 0\quad(mod\,\,2)
, hence by the inductive hypothesis the system (
A.9
) has weightedly paired solutions if
S
is even (and the same holds for
\bm{k}
), and no solutions if
S
is odd.

∎

A.3
Support of the remainders

We dedicate this section to the proof of
2.14
, which is an adaptation of
[
4
, Lemma 3.7 and Corollary 3.8]
. We start with a key technical lemma:

Lemma A.3
.

If
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
with
n\geq 3
and satisfies
k_{1}+k_{2}\neq 0
, then for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\max\left((n-2)^{2l}|k_{3}|^{2l+1},\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{j}^{2l+1}\right\rvert\right)\geq\frac{|k_{1}|^{2l}}{2}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
k_{1}
is positive.
Then we have two cases:

•

If
k_{2}
is positive, then

k_{1}\leq k_{1}+k_{2}=-(k_{3}+\ldots+k_{n})\leq(n-2)|k_{3}|,

which yields

(n-2)^{2l}|k_{3}|^{2l+1}\geq\frac{|k_{1}|^{2l}}{2}.

•

If
k_{2}
is negative, then

k_{1}^{2l+1}+k_{2}^{2l+1}=k_{1}^{2l+1}-|k_{2}|^{2l+1}=(k_{1}+k_{2})\left(\sum_{j=0}^{2l}k_{1}^{2l-j}|k_{2}|^{j}\right).

Dividing by
k_{1}+k_{2}\neq 0
we obtain

(A.10)

\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{j}^{2l+1}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert k_{1}^{2l+1}+k_{2}^{2l+1}\right\rvert-(n-2)|k_{3}|^{2l+1}\geq k_{1}^{2l}-(n-2)|k_{3}|^{2l+1}.

Thus, if
\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{j}^{2l+1}\right\rvert\leq k_{1}^{2l}/2
, then (
A.10
) yields
(n-2)^{2l}|k_{3}|^{2l+1}\geq(n-2)|k_{3}|^{2l+1}\geq k_{1}^{2l}/2
.

∎

Corollary A.4
.

Let
\bm{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{n}\cap(\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{n}^{l})
for some
n\geq 3
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
N>2
is such that

\left\lvert\frac{k_{1}^{2l-1}}{k_{1}^{2l+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2l+1}}\right\rvert\geq N

then either there exists
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{n-2}
such that

(A.11)

u^{\bm{k}}=|u_{a}|^{2}u^{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\quad\text{where}\quad a\geq N^{\frac{1}{2l-1}}

or

(A.12)

|k_{3}|^{2l+1}\geq\frac{N^{\frac{2l}{2l-1}}}{2(n-2)^{2l}}.

Proof.

It is clear that
|k_{1}|\geq N^{\frac{1}{2l-1}}
.
If
k_{1}+k_{2}=0
, then (
A.11
) is satisfied. Otherwise, since

|k_{1}^{2l+1}+\ldots+k_{n}^{2l+1}|\leq\frac{|k_{1}|^{2l-1}}{N}<\frac{|k_{1}|^{2l}}{2},

then by
Lemma

A.3
,

|k_{3}|^{2l+1}\geq\frac{|k_{1}|^{2l}}{2(n-2)^{2l}}\geq\frac{N^{\frac{2l}{2l-1}}}{2(n-2)^{2l}}.

∎

Proof of
2.14
.

By
A.4
, we have (
A.11
) or (
A.12
). (
2.49
) follows from (
A.11
) and the inequality
2n\geq 2l-1
. In order to prove (
2.50
), we use (
A.12
):

\left(\frac{N^{\frac{2l}{2l-1}}}{2(n-2)^{2l}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2l+1}}\geq\frac{N^{\frac{2l}{4l^{2}-1}}}{2^{\frac{1}{2l+1}}(n-2)^{\frac{2l}{2l+1}}}\geq N^{\frac{1}{2n}}

provided that
N\geq N_{0}(n)
is sufficiently large.
∎

A.4
Proof of
2.20

We first prove the existence of the map
\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi}
and its inverse. By (
2.66
),
N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\lesssim_{\rho,n}N^{n-2}
and by
Lemma

2.15
there exists
\epsilon_{0}=c(n,s,\rho)N^{-1}
such that for any

(A.13)

\epsilon\leq c(n,s,\rho)N^{-1}

there exists an invertible canonical transformation

(A.14)

\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,2\epsilon),\qquad\Phi_{-G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi}:B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2)\rightarrow B_{s}(0,\epsilon)

for
\xi\in[0,2]
.
Next we show (
2.68
):
\forall\xi\in[0,2]
,

\left\lVert\Phi_{\pm G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi}(u)-u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: close to the identity 1}}}{{\lesssim_{n,s}}}N\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y_{n}^{l}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: stima induttiva coeff g}}}{{\lesssim_{n,s,\rho}}}N^{n-2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:eps0}}}{{\lesssim_{n,s,\rho}}}N\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}^{2}.

Moreover by construction

\Phi_{G_{r}^{(l)}}^{\xi}\circ\Phi_{-G_{r}^{(l)}}^{\xi}(u)=u\qquad\forall u\in B_{s}(0,\epsilon/2).

Taylor formula for
F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi}
: Firstly we remark that by
Lemma

2.4
, since
s\geq j
we have that
F^{(j)}(u)
is a smooth function in
B_{s}(0,\frac{1}{c_{s}\rho N})
.
Up to reducing
\epsilon_{0}
in (
A.13
), we have that
F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(j)}}^{\xi}
is well-defined in
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
.
Remember that by (
2.41
), we have the Taylor formula

(A.15)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{1}=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F^{(j)}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{m+1}(F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}\xi

where
\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}=\{\cdot,G_{n}^{(l)}\}
, each term being well defined by
Lemma

2.8
.
We want to prove that the Taylor series converges, i.e. that the integral remainder

R_{m}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{m+1}(F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}\xi

tends to zero as
m\to\infty
, uniformly in
B_{s}(0,\epsilon).

By (
2.65
), we have to prove that

(A.16)

\sum_{\beta\geq 2}R_{m,\beta}\longrightarrow_{m\to+\infty}0,\quad\text{where}\quad R_{m,\beta}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{m+1}(F_{\beta}^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Estimates on the coefficients
: We begin by studying
\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{\beta}^{(j)}
in (
A.15
).

\bullet

\beta\geq 3
. By
Lemma

2.17
it is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
\beta+\alpha(n-2)
of the form

(A.17)

\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{\beta}^{(j)}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\beta+\alpha(n-2)}}d_{\bm{k}}(\alpha,\beta)u^{\bm{k}},\qquad\mathrm{with}\,\,d_{\bm{k}}(0,\beta)=c_{\bm{k}}\ \mathrm{for}\ \#\bm{k}=\beta,

where, using the recursive formula
\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{\beta}^{(j)}=\{\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha-1}F_{\beta}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}
, we have the recursive bound
10
10

10

From now on we omit the subscript
n
in the space
Y^{\theta}_{n}
whenever clear from the context.

\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha,\beta)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{d_est}}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{2\pi}Nn^{2j-1}[\beta+(\alpha-1)(n-2)]\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y^{l}}\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha-1,\beta)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}

which in turn gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha,\beta)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left\lVert(c_{\bm{k}})_{\#\bm{k}=\beta}\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}Nn^{2j-1}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{Y^{l}}\right)^{\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^{\alpha}\left[\beta+(i-1)(n-2)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{formula Fj prop}-\eqref{eq: stima induttiva coeff g}}}{{\leq}}\rho^{\beta}N^{\beta-3}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}Nn^{2j-1}\rho^{n}N^{n-3}\right)^{\alpha}\,\prod_{i=1}^{\alpha}\frac{\beta+(i-1)(n-2)}{i}

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{\beta}N^{\beta-3}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}Nn^{2j-1}\rho^{n}N^{n-3}\right)^{\alpha}n^{\alpha}e^{\beta-1}

where the last inequality may be found in page 1166 of
[
4
]
.

Therefore

(A.18)

\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha,\beta)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}\leq e^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2\alpha}(\rho N)^{\beta+\alpha(n-2)}N^{-3}.

\bullet

\beta=2
. By (
2.67
), it is a homogeneous
j
-formal polynomial of degree
2+\alpha(n-2)
of the form

\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{1}{(\alpha-1)!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha-1}\{F_{2}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{2+\alpha(n-2)}}d_{\bm{k}}(\alpha,2)u^{\bm{k}}

where, using (
A.18
) with
\alpha-1
instead of
\alpha
and
n=\beta
,

(A.19)

\frac{1}{\alpha!}\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha,2)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}e^{n-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2(\alpha-1)}(\rho N)^{n+(\alpha-1)(n-2)}N^{-3}.

Convergence of the Taylor series
:

\bullet

\beta\geq 3
.
Proceeding as in (
2.14
)–(
2.16
) (with
M=1
),

(A.20)

\begin{split}\left\lvert\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{\beta}^{(j)}(u)\right\rvert&\leq\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\beta+\alpha(n-2)}}|d_{\bm{k}}(\alpha,\beta)u^{\bm{k}}|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\alpha!}[\beta+\alpha(n-2)]^{j}\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha,\beta)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}(c_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})^{\beta+\alpha(n-2)}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: prima stima sui d}}}{{\leq}}[\beta+\alpha(n-2)]^{j}e^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2\alpha}(c_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\rho N)^{\beta+\alpha(n-2)}N^{-3}.\end{split}

Using this estimate with
\alpha=m+1
, we bound
R_{m,\beta}(u)
in (
A.16
).

\begin{split}|R_{m,\beta}(u)|&=\left\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{m}}{m!}(\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{m+1}F_{\beta}^{(j)})(\Phi_{G_{r}^{(l)}}^{\xi}(u))\,\mathrm{d}\xi\right\rvert\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: dominio e codominio phi e phi_inv}}}{{\leq}}[\beta+(m+1)(n-2)]^{j}e^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2(m+1)}(2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}c_{s}\rho N)^{\beta+(m+1)(n-2)}N^{-3}.\end{split}

Further restricting
\epsilon
in (
A.13
) such that

(A.21)

2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}c_{s}\rho N<2\epsilon\,c_{s}\rho N<e^{-1},

it follows that the series
\sum_{\beta\geq 3}R_{m,\beta}(u)
is absolutely convergent. Moreover, by further restricting
\epsilon
such that

(A.22)

\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2}(2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}c_{s}\rho N)^{n-2}<\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2}(2\epsilon c_{s}\rho N)^{n-2}<1,

we have that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sum_{\beta\geq 3}R_{m,\beta}(u)=0.

\bullet

\beta=2
. Proceeding as in (
2.14
)–(
2.16
) (with
M=1
),

(A.23)

\begin{split}\left\lvert\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{2}^{(j)}(u)\right\rvert&\leq\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{2+(n-2)\alpha}}|d_{\bm{k}}(\alpha,2)u^{\bm{k}}|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\alpha!}[n+(\alpha-1)(n-2)]^{j}\left\lVert\bm{d}(\alpha,2)\right\rVert_{Y^{j}}(c_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}})^{n+(\alpha-1)(n-2)}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: seconda stima sui d}}}{{\leq}}[n+(\alpha-1)(n-2)]^{j}\frac{1}{\alpha}e^{n-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2(\alpha-1)}(c_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\rho N)^{n+(\alpha-1)(n-2)}N^{-3}.\end{split}

Hence, taking
\alpha=m+1
,

\begin{split}|R_{m,2}(u)|&=\left\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{m}}{m!}(\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{m+1}F_{2}^{(j)})(\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\xi}(u))\,\mathrm{d}\xi\right\rvert\\
&\leq[n+m(n-2)]^{j}\frac{1}{m+1}e^{n-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2m}(2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}c_{s}\rho N)^{n+m(n-2)}N^{-3}.\end{split}

Using (
A.22
), it follows that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}R_{m,2}(u)=0
.

We may therefore take
m\rightarrow\infty
in (
A.15
), which yields

(A.24)

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{1}(u)=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F^{(j)}(u)=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{2}^{(j)}(u)+\sum_{\alpha=0}^{+\infty}\sum_{\beta\geq 3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{\beta}^{(j)}(u).

Using (
A.20
) and (
A.23
), one quickly sees that (
A.24
) converges absolutely in
\alpha
and
\beta
provided (
A.21
) and (
A.22
) are satisfied.

Estimates on the new coefficients
: We first isolate the homogeneous term of degree
m\geq 3
in (
A.24
):

Q_{m}^{(j)}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\geq 0,\,\,\beta\geq 3\\
\beta+\alpha(n-2)=m\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha}F_{\beta}^{(j)}(u)+\frac{\delta(\beta=n)}{(\alpha+1)!}\mathrm{ad}_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{\alpha+1}F_{2}^{(j)}(u)\right]=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{m}}q_{\bm{k}}u^{\bm{k}}

where, using (
A.17
),

(A.25)

q_{\bm{k}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\geq 0,\,\,\beta\geq 3\\
\beta+\alpha(n-2)=\#\bm{k}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha!}d_{\bm{k}}(\alpha,\beta)+\frac{\delta(\beta=n)}{(\alpha+1)!}d_{\bm{k}}(\alpha+1,2)\right].

We have therefore proved (
2.69
), i.e. on
B_{s}(0,\epsilon)
we have

F^{(j)}\circ\Phi_{G_{n}^{(l)}}^{1}=F_{2}^{(j)}+\sum_{m\geq 3}Q_{m}^{(j)}.

Finally, we must prove the estimates on the coefficients
q_{\bm{k}}
in (
2.70
).

•

If
\#\bm{k}<n
then it follows from (
A.25
) that
q_{\bm{k}}=d_{\bm{k}}(0,\#\bm{k})=c_{\bm{k}}
by (
A.17
), and (
2.70
) follows from hypothesis (
2.65
).

•

If
\#\bm{k}=n
, then
Q_{n}^{(j)}=F_{n}^{(j)}+\{F_{2}^{(j)},G_{n}^{(l)}\}
and it follows from (
A.25
) that

q_{\bm{k}}=d_{\bm{k}}(0,\#\bm{k})+d_{\bm{k}}(1,2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hom_eq prop}}}{{=}}c_{\bm{k}}\delta(\bm{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{n,l,N}),

proving (
2.71
). Estimate (
2.70
) trivially follows from hypothesis (
2.65
).

•

If
\#\bm{k}>n
, we use (
A.18
)–(
A.19
) together with (
A.25
):

\begin{split}\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{Y_{\#\bm{k}}^{j}}&\leq 2\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\geq 0,\,\,\beta\geq 3\\
\beta+\alpha(n-2)=\#\bm{k}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!e^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2\alpha}\!\!\!(\rho N)^{\#\bm{k}}N^{-3}=N^{-3}\!\!\!\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\lfloor\frac{\#\bm{k}}{n-2}\rfloor}e^{\#\bm{k}-\alpha(n-2)}\left(\frac{n^{j}\rho}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2\alpha}\!\!\!(\rho N)^{\#\bm{k}}\leq\tilde{\rho}^{\#\bm{k}}N^{\#\bm{k}-3}\end{split}

where

\tilde{\rho}\leq\,\max\left\{e^{\frac{n-1}{n-2}}\rho,\left(\frac{en^{2j}\rho^{n}}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{n-2}}\right\}\lesssim_{n,j,\rho}1.

This proves (
2.70
) and concludes the proof of
2.20
. ∎
